# Themes Index

Catalog of every theme/doctrine page, updated on each ingest.

| Theme | Summary | Key passages |
|---|---|---|
| [[Adoption]] | Taking as one's own a child not born to one — the Bible's first instance and its only formula spoken by the adopter, at 48:5; everything earlier in Genesis is surrogacy through a slave or an heir named for want of a son  Joshua 14:1–5: Joseph’s two tribal branches (4) give territorial expression to the standing of Ephraim and Manasseh in Genesis 48:5–6. Joshua does not repeat the adoption scene or explicitly explain its motive; the connection is a comparison of the two passages. | [[Jacob Blesses Ephraim and Manasseh]] · [[The Deaths of Jacob and Joseph]] · [[The Birth of Moses]] · [[Moses Returns to Egypt]] · [[Where There Is No Vision]] (Proverbs 29:21) · [[A Holy People and Its Food]]  · [[The Division of the Land Begins]] |
| [[Atonement]] | The work of the root *kpr* — *kipper*, *kippurîm*, *kappōret*, *kōper* — dealing with what stands between a holy God and a people, priest, or thing so approach is possible; a plain *seal* and *appease* in Genesis, a *ransom* in law, then atonement for priests and altar, yearly on the incense altar's horns, and in the half-shekel *"atonement money"*; Moses says that perhaps he will make atonement for the people’s sin (32:30); Exodus 37: Mercy seat and incense-altar horns made; no atonement rite performed.; Exodus 38: The silver prescribed as atonement money becomes sockets and fittings.  Leviticus 1: Leviticus 1:4 explicitly connects the accepted burnt offering with atonement for its offerer.; Leviticus 4:22–35: Three outer-altar procedures ending in forgiveness.  Leviticus 5:1–13: The priest makes atonement even with the flour sin offering (11–13). Atonement here cannot be defined as invariably requiring this offerer to provide an animal.  Leviticus 6:1–7: The priest makes atonement before Yahweh after the law has required repayment to the injured owner (4–7). The ritual remedy addresses fraud and false swearing as well as the unwitting wrong of 5:15. Leviticus 6:24–7:10: The priest who makes atonement receives the sin or trespass offering under 7:7, with the explicit exception that sin offerings whose blood enters the tent for atonement are burned (6:30). The common portion rule does not make all blood rites identical.  Leviticus 8: Explicitly for altar and priests.; Leviticus 9: inaugural offerings, blessing, and public manifestation; Leviticus 10:12–20: the sin offering given to priests to make atonement for the congregation; Leviticus 12: The priest makes atonement for the mother, and the stated result is cleansing (7–8).; Leviticus 14:1–32: restoration after healing ; Leviticus 14:33–57: house inspection, repair, and cleansing  Leviticus 15: atonement for discharge without a stated personal offense;; Leviticus 17: Yahweh gives blood on the altar to make atonement by reason of the life (11).; Leviticus 19:19–37: The priest makes atonement with the ram for the man’s sin, and forgiveness is promised (21–22).; Leviticus 23:23–32: Rest grounded in atonement  Leviticus 25:1–22: The Jubilee trumpet sounds on the Day of Atonement (9).; Numbers 6: atonement for an unintended defilement; Numbers 16:41–50: The WEB explicitly calls Aaron’s act with incense atonement (46–47). No new slaughter or blood Numbers 35: Numbers 35:33 uses atonement in the WEB for the land polluted by shed blood and specifies the shedder’s blood as the remedy. No animal offering or payment is prescribed here. The high priest’s death releases the protected killer (25–28), but the passage does not explicitly call that death atonement; the two provisions should not be collapsed.  Deuteronomy 32: The closing WEB line says Yahweh will make atonement for his land and people (43), beside avenging his servants’ blood. No priest, altar, or animal rite is prescribed here. The poem connects restored land and people with divine action against bloodshed, without defining a ritual mechanism. | [[Noah and the Flood]] · [[Wrestling at the Jabbok]] · [[Laws of Violence and Injury]] · [[The Ark, the Table, and the Lampstand]] · [[The Ordination of the Priests]] · [[The Incense Altar, the Ransom, and the Holy Oil]] · [[The Golden Calf]] · [[The Furnishings Made]] (Exodus 37:1–29) · [[The Altar, the Basin, the Court, and the Inventory]] (Exodus 38:1–31) · [[A Way That Seems Right]] (Proverbs 14:9) · [[The Plans of the Heart]] (Proverbs 16:6)  · [[The Burnt Offering]] · [[The Sin Offering for Priest and Congregation]] · [[The Sin Offering for Ruler and Individual]] (Leviticus 4:22–35)  · [[Confession and Offerings According to Means]] · [[The Trespass Offering for Holy Things and Unknown Guilt]] (Leviticus 5:14–19); The priest makes atonement with the ram and forgiveness is promised (16, 18). In the holy-things case, material restitution accompanies rather than replaces the offering.  · [[Restitution for Fraud and False Oaths]] |; Leviticus 4:1–21: The priest makes atonement for the congregation, and forgiveness follows explicitly (20). · [[The Priests’ Portions of Sin and Trespass Offerings]]  · [[Aaron and His Sons Consecrated]] · [[The Eighth Day and the Appearing Glory]] · [[The Uneaten Sin Offering]] · [[Purification After Childbirth]] · [[The Cleansing of the Leper]] · [[Mildew in a House]]  · [[Bodily Discharges and Cleansing]] · [[The Place of Sacrifice and the Life in the Blood]] · [[Holy Living in Field and Community]] · [[Trumpets and the Day of Atonement]]  · [[The Sabbatical Year and the Jubilee]] · [[The Unclean Sent Out and Wrongs Repaid]] · [[The Nazirite Vow]] · [[The Lamps and the Levites Cleansed]] · [[Offerings in the Land and Sins of Ignorance]] · [[Aaron Stands Between the Dead and the Living]] · [[Baal Peor and the Zeal of Phinehas]] — Numbers 25: The WEB explicitly calls Phinehas’s action atonement for Israel (13). · [[The Offerings of the Appointed Feasts]] · [[The War Against Midian]] · [[The Levitical Cities and the Cities of Refuge]] (Numbers 35:1–34) · [[An Unsolved Killing and the Broken-Necked Heifer]]  · [[The Song of Moses]] |
| [[Barrenness]] | A womb that does not open, in a book whose whole structure is a list of children being born; stated flat at 11:30 and then the engine of the promise for ten chapters; miscarriage and barrenness promised away in the land; Proverbs 30:16: the barren womb counted with Sheol, earth, and fire; Leviticus 20: The uncle’s-wife and brother’s-wife cases specify dying childless or being childless (20–21). Deuteronomy 7:12–26: No barrenness among people or livestock is promised to an obedient Israel (14). | [[From Shem to Abram]] · [[The Call of Abram]] · [[The Covenant Between the Pieces]] · [[Hagar and Ishmael]] · [[Circumcision, the Sign of the Covenant]] · [[The Three Visitors and Abraham's Intercession]] · [[Abraham and Abimelech]] · [[The Birth of Isaac and the Sending of Hagar]] · [[Nahor's Line and the Burial of Sarah]] · [[Jacob and Esau and the Sold Birthright]] · [[The Birth of Jacob's Children]] · [[The Consecration of the Firstborn]] · [[The Angel Sent Ahead]] · [[Four Things]] (Proverbs 30:16) · [[Penalties and a People Set Apart]] · [[The Suspected Wife]] · [[Blessing and the Removal of the Nations]] |
| [[Birthright and the Firstborn]] | *Bəkōrâ* — the firstborn's status and double portion, introduced at Genesis 25:31 in the mouth of a younger brother naming his price and never defined; six generations of Genesis invert it with no reason given, and then Deuteronomy 21:15–17 forbids exactly what the patriarchs keep doing; Exodus 34: Firstborn claim repeated with explicit redemption of sons; Numbers 1: Reuben's title and Joseph's two tribes in the register; Numbers 18: Firstborn humans and unclean animals must be redeemed, while firstborn cattle, sheep, and goats remain sacrificial (15–18).  Numbers 26: Reuben remains the firstborn (5), but Joseph is represented by two tribal counts (28–37). Manasseh exceeds Ephraim here, qualifying any equation of the younger’s promised greatness with constant numerical superiority.| [[Jacob and Esau and the Sold Birthright]] · [[The Stolen Blessing]] · [[Isaac Sends Jacob to Paddan Aram]] · [[Jacob, Leah, and Rachel]] · [[The Birth of Jacob's Children]] · [[Return to Bethel; the Deaths of Rachel and Isaac]] · [[Joseph Sold into Slavery]] · [[Judah and Tamar]] · [[Pharaoh's Dreams and Joseph's Rise]] · [[Jacob Blesses Ephraim and Manasseh]] · [[Jacob Blesses His Sons]] · [[The Restoration of Job]] · [[Moses Returns to Egypt]] · [[The Heads of the Fathers' Houses]] · [[The Last Plague Announced]] · [[The Passover]] · [[The Death of the Firstborn and the Departure]] · [[The Consecration of the Firstborn]] · [[Laws of Holiness and Compassion]] · [[The Covenant Renewed]] · [[A Dry Morsel with Quietness]] (Proverbs 17:2) · [[Vows, Valuations, and Devoted Things]] · [[The Census in the Wilderness of Sinai]] · [[The Levites and the Firstborn Counted]] · [[The Lamps and the Levites Cleansed]] · [[The Duties and Portions of Priests and Levites]]  · [[The Second Census]] Deuteronomy 12:1–28: Firstborn of herd and flock eaten at the chosen place (6, 17). | Numbers 33: Egyptians bury their firstborn as Israel departs (4), recalling the final plague rather than discussing inheritance rights. · [[The Stages of the Journey]] · [[The Place Yahweh Will Choose]] · [[Tithes for Feasting and Provision]] · [[Consecrate the Firstborn of the Herd and Flock]] · [[Captives, Firstborn Sons, and Public Shame]] |
| [[Blessing]] | After eating to fullness Israel is to bless Yahweh for the good land (10). Here the people bless the giver in acknowledgment; the direction differs from God granting prosperity to the people.  Deuteronomy 10:1–11: Blessing in Yahweh’s name is one of Levi’s appointed functions (8). The verse gives neither a spoken formula nor an explicit distribution of duties among the tribe’s members. Deuteronomy 11:1–17: rain, grain, wine, oil, and fullness promised for listening. Deuteronomy 11:18–32: a blessing set before Israel, to be set on Mount Gerizim (26–29). Deuteronomy 12:1–28: Meat eaten *"according to Yahweh your God's blessing"* (7, 15).  Deuteronomy 27:11–26: Six tribes stand on Gerizim to bless the people (12), but this unit supplies only curse formulas (15–26). The assignment to bless must not be mistaken for a written series of matching blessings. Deuteronomy 28: conditional blessing in the land.  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: The blessings of 28:3–6 and 12–13 are reversed in 28:16–19 and 43–44. Both halves retain their conditional setting, rather than making election an unconditional guarantee of prosperity.  Deuteronomy 33: The blessing before Moses’ death embraces distinct tribal gifts within Yahweh’s care for Israel (1–29). It does not erase the curses of chapter 28 or the predicted betrayal of chapter 32.  Joshua 8:30–35: altar and inclusive public reading. Joshua 14:6–15: Joshua blesses Caleb and gives him Hebron (13). The narrator reports the blessing without preserving its words; the inheritance enacts the favorable recognition.  Joshua 15:13–19: Achsah asks her father for a blessing and specifies springs of water (19). Caleb answers with both upper and lower springs. Here blessing takes the concrete form of a resource grant rather than a recorded spoken benediction.  Joshua 17:14–18: Joseph’s descendants invoke Yahweh’s blessing to explain their numbers (14). Joshua accepts their strength as a reason they can act, rather than treating blessing as exemption from labor or conflict. | [[The Creation of the World]] · [[From Adam to Noah]] · [[The Sons of God | [[Blood]] | *Dām* — not a substance but a claim: the life is in it, spilled blood cries out, and it is given for the altar; the two rules governing it are pre-Sinai and universal; sacrificial blood and leaven; one covenant blood divided between altar and people (Exodus 24:6–8); blood on the priests' right ear, thumb, and toe, and sprinkled with oil; Exodus 34: Blood-and-leaven prohibition repeated with a Passover overnight rule; Proverbs 6:17: hands that shed innocent blood, hated by Yahweh.; Proverbs 28: The man tormented by life blood remains a fugitive until death, without support, in the WEB’s rendering  Leviticus 1: The livestock offerer kills, while Aaron’s sons present or sprinkle blood around the altar (1:5, 11).; Leviticus 3: Aaron's sons sprinkle the victim's blood around the altar (3:2, 8, 13), while 3:17 prohibits eating blood in all dwellings; Leviticus 4:1–21: Blood is sprinkled seven times before the veil, placed on the inner altar’s horns, and poured at the outer altar’s base (6–7, 17–18). · [[The Sin Offering for Priest and Congregation]]; Leviticus 4:22–35: Outer altar horns and base, without entry into the tent.  Leviticus 5:1–13: The bird’s blood is sprinkled on the altar’s side and drained at its base (9). The flour procedure prescribes no blood from this offering yet promises atonement and forgiveness (11–13). Leviticus 6:24–7:10: Sin-offering blood on clothing must be washed in a holy place (6:27). Blood brought into the tent for atonement rules out eating that offering (6:30); trespass-offering blood is sprinkled around the altar (7:2). Blood destination matters for the treatment of the flesh.; Leviticus 7:11–21: Blood-sprinkling priest receives a bread portion  Leviticus 7:22–38: The prohibition expressly includes blood of birds and animals in every dwelling, with cutting off as the sanction (26–27). Leviticus 8: Applied to altar, body parts, and garments.; Leviticus 9: inaugural offerings, blessing, and public manifestation; Leviticus 12: The blood in the purification provisions is the mother's postpartum blood (4–5, 7).; Leviticus 14:1–32: restoration after healing ; Leviticus 14:33–57: house inspection, repair, and cleansing  Leviticus 15: menstrual and prolonged bleeding confer uncleanness;; Leviticus 17: Blood is excluded from food because of its relation to life and its God-given altar function (10–14).; Leviticus 19:19–37: Eating meat with blood still in it is prohibited (26), continuing the food restriction already developed in Leviticus 17.; Leviticus 20: The repeated formula assigning offenders’ blood to themselves accompanies death penalties (9, 11–13, 16, 27).; Numbers 16:41–50: Unlike the blood applications of Leviticus 16:14–19, Numbers 16:46–48 narrates incense and Numbers 35: Blood shed in homicide pollutes the land, and the WEB says atonement for that blood requires the shedder’s blood (33). The context distinguishes murder from unwitting killing and requires judgment and witnesses (12, 22–30); verse 33 cannot erase those safeguards. Deuteronomy 12:1–28: Home-slaughtered blood poured on the ground like water, *"for the blood is the life"* (16, 23).  Deuteronomy 27:11–26: Secretly killing a neighbor and accepting a bribe to kill an innocent person are cursed (24–25). Concealment and payment do not make the taking of life permissible.  Joshua 20: The avenger of blood may pursue, but the city must not surrender the person who killed unintentionally without prior hatred (3–5). | [[The Surety, the Sluggard, and the Seven Abominations]] · [[Cain and Abel]] · [[The Covenant with Noah]] · [[I Know That My Redeemer Lives]] · [[Joseph Sold into Slavery]] · [[Moses Returns to Egypt]] · [[Water Turned to Blood]] · [[The Passover]] · [[Laws of Property and Restitution]] · [[Justice, Sabbaths, and Feasts]] · [[The Blood of the Covenant]] · [[The Ordination of the Priests]] · [[The Incense Altar, the Ransom, and the Holy Oil]] · [[The Covenant Renewed]] · [[Do Not Consent to Sinners]] · [[The Wicked Flee When No One Pursues]] (Proverbs 28:17) · [[Where There Is No Vision]] (Proverbs 29:10)  · [[The Burnt Offering]] · [[The Peace Offering]] · [[The Sin Offering for Ruler and Individual]] (Leviticus 4:22–35)  · [[Confession and Offerings According to Means]] · [[The Priests’ Portions of Sin and Trespass Offerings]] · [[Thanksgiving, Vows, and the Peace Offering]]  · [[Fat, Blood, and the Priests’ Share]]  · [[Aaron and His Sons Consecrated]] · [[The Eighth Day and the Appearing Glory]] · [[Purification After Childbirth]] · [[The Cleansing of the Leper]] · [[Mildew in a House]]  · [[Bodily Discharges and Cleansing]] · [[The Place of Sacrifice and the Life in the Blood]] · [[Holy Living in Field and Community]] · [[Penalties and a People Set Apart]] · [[Aaron Stands Between the Dead and the Living]] · [[The Levitical Cities and the Cities of Refuge]] (Numbers 35:1–34) · [[The Place Yahweh Will Choose]] · [[Consecrate the Firstborn of the Herd and Flock]] · [[Refuge for the Unwitting Killer]] · [[An Unsolved Killing and the Broken-Necked Heifer]] · [[Care for the Neighbor and Ordered Distinctions]]  · [[The People Answer Amen to the Curses]]  · [[The Cities of Refuge Appointed]] |
| [[Brotherhood]] | *ʾāḥ* — sibling, kinsman, and the person you are answerable for; Genesis's first death is a fratricide and its first ethical question is *Am I my brother's keeper?*, which the book spends forty-six chapters answering in narrative; The camp command expressly reaches brother, companion, and neighbor, and Moses’ following call to consecration includes son and brother (32:27–29); Proverbs 6:19: discord among brothers, the climax of the list Yahweh hates; Proverbs 19: Relatives shun the poor: kinship alone does not guarantee help in adversity; Ecclesiastes 4:8–12: neither son nor brother, and the companion who lifts ; Song 8:1: wished-for sibling status as freedom to kiss openly; Song 8:8–12: family plans, the woman’s answer, and her own vineyard Leviticus 19:1–18: Brother, neighbor, and the children of one’s people appear together in the commands against hatred and vengeance (17–18). Responsibility for another reaches the heart and the handling of conflict, not only outward injury.  Leviticus 25:23–34: The impoverished brother is helped through the nearest kinsman, who is directed to redeem what was sold (25); Leviticus 25:35–55: *brother* fixes who is protected from interest and slavery  Numbers 20:14–21: Israel addresses Edom as a brother, but kinship does not produce permission to pass. The appeal fails, and Israel withdraws before Edom’s force (14–21). Deuteronomy 2:1–23: Esau’s descendants are explicitly Israel’s brothers, although they fear Israel (4, 8). Brotherhood here obliges restraint; it does not establish an unrestricted right of passage. Deuteronomy 3:12–22: The repeated reference to brothers makes shared military service a condition of returning to settled homes (18–20). Receiving an inheritance first does not end mutual obligation. Joshua 4: The eastern tribes’ crossing (12–13) puts shared responsibility into action. Having land east of the river does not release them from helping the others.  Joshua 21: Israel supplies Levitical households from its own inheritances (3, 8); the tribal grants make room for those without a separate territorial allotment. | [[The Surety, the Sluggard, and the | [[Burial and the Grave]] | What the living do with the dead, and why Scripture treats it as a claim rather than a disposal; Genesis reports a great many deaths before it reports a single burial, and then spends eighteen verses on the price of the ground and one on the interment; Ecclesiastes 3:16–22: Return to dust without a narrated burial; Ecclesiastes 6:1–9: No burial despite many children and years; Ecclesiastes 11:7–12:8: The everlasting home, mourners, and return to earth.  Numbers 20:1–13: Miriam dies and is buried at Kadesh (1). Numbers 20:22–29: Thirty days of mourning are narrated, but no burial procedure. Numbers 33: The Egyptians are burying the firstborn when Israel departs (4). Aaron’s death is recorded later (38–39), but no burial procedure is described.  Deuteronomy 10:1–11: Aaron is explicitly buried at Moserah (6); no agent or procedure is named. Numbers 20:28 places his death on Mount Hor without narrating a burial. The location discrepancy remains unresolved.  Deuteronomy 32:48–52: Being gathered to his people accompanies the command that Moses die as Aaron died (50). No burial site, procedure, or agent is given here; the phrase must not be expanded into a report of a shared ancestral tomb. | [[Nahor's Line and the Burial of Sarah]] · [[The Death of Abraham and the Line of Ishmael]] · [[Return to Bethel; the Deaths of Rachel and Isaac]] · [[Joseph Sold into Slavery]] · [[The Cupbearer and the Baker]] · [[Joseph and the Famine]] · [[Jacob Blesses Ephraim and Manasseh]] · [[Jacob Blesses His Sons]] · [[The Deaths of Jacob and Joseph]] · [[Seven Days of Silence]] · [[Let the Day Perish]] · [[Why Do the Wicked Live]] · [[The Crossing of the Sea]] · [[From Dust to Dust]] (Ecclesiastes 3:16–22) · [[Riches Without Enjoyment]] (Ecclesiastes 6:1–9) · [[The Wicked Buried and the Work Beyond Finding]] — the wicked buried · [[Remember Your Creator]] · [[The Seventy Elders and the Quail]]  · [[The Waters of Meribah]] · [[The Death of Aaron]] · [[The Stages of the Journey]]  · [[New Tablets and the Journey Onward]] · [[Captives, Firstborn Sons, and Public Shame]]  · [[Moses Commanded to Ascend Nebo]] · [[The Death of Moses]] · [[The Capture of Ai]] · [[The Five Kings in the Cave at Makkedah]] — defeated kings, courage, and burial. |
| [[Call and Election]] | God choosing and summoning particular people, giving no reason for the choice and stating a purpose that reaches past them; the founding text is Genesis 12:1, and eleven verses later it is shown to be unconditional; Bezalel called by name and Oholiab appointed with him (31:2–6); Exodus 33: Known by name; the people distinguished by presence; Named appointment becomes public summons and shared willing work; Exodus 38: Named appointments lead to credited work within a wider company.; Leviticus 20: Yahweh has separated Israel from the peoples to belong to him (24, 26).  Numbers 16:1–40: Moses directs the dispute to Yahweh’s choice of who may approach (5–7) and stakes his own commission on a distinctive divine act (28–30).  Numbers 17: Yahweh makes budding the announced sign of his choice (5); the next morning Aaron’s rod alone is described as bearing buds, blossoms, and almonds (8). Numbers 34: Yahweh names Eleazar, Joshua, and the tribal representatives for the distribution (17–29). Appointment carries a defined responsibility rather than an unbounded personal privilege. Deuteronomy 3:23–29: Joshua’s appointment is attached to a defined task: crossing before the people and causing them to inherit (28). Moses’ service continues in preparing the successor who will enter where he cannot. Deuteronomy 7:1–11: Israel is holy and chosen as Yahweh’s own possession (6). Deuteronomy 9:1–6: Following the denial that numerical greatness explains election (7:7–8), this unit denies that Israel’s righteousness explains possession (4–6). The ancestral oath, not superiority, grounds the gift.  Deuteronomy 10:12–22: The owner of all heaven and earth chooses the fathers’ offspring (14–15), yet shows no partiality and loves the foreigner (17–19). Deuteronomy 12:1–28: Election extends to a place, set against *"every place that you see"* (5, 13). Deuteronomy 26:16–19: Promised honor follows belonging and does not explain the original choice.  Deuteronomy 27: Israel is declared to have become Yahweh's people today (9), alongside the earlier chosen status of 7:6 and possession declaration of 26:18.  Deuteronomy 31: Joshua’s succession is publicly affirmed before all Israel (3, 7–8). The commissioned successor is encouraged for a task grounded in Yahweh’s prior word, not presented as self-appointed. Joshua 3: Yahweh begins to establish Joshua publicly so Israel will know that he is with him as with Moses (7). The crossing confirms an existing commission; it is not Joshua’s first call. Joshua 4: The public confirmation promised Joshua in 3:7 is reported accomplished in 4:14. Selection of one representative per tribe (2–5) separately gives the memorial a national scope. | [[The Call of Abram]] · [[Abram in Egypt]] · [[Abram and Lot Separate]] · [[The Covenant Between the Pieces]] · [[Circumcision, the Sign of the Covenant]] · [[The Three Visitors and Abraham's Intercession]] · [[The Birth of Isaac and the Sending of Hagar]] · [[The Binding of Isaac]] · [[The Death of Abraham and the Line of Ishmael]] · [[Jacob and Esau and the Sold Birthright]] · [[The Stolen Blessing]] · [[Isaac Sends Jacob to Paddan Aram]] · [[Jacob's Ladder at Bethel]] · [[The Burning Bush]] · [[I Am Yahweh]] · [[The Heads of the Fathers' Houses]] · [[Frogs, Lice, and Flies]] · [[Livestock, Boils, and Hail]] · [[Locusts and Darkness]] · [[The Last Plague Announced]] · [[Israel at Mount Sinai]] · [[Bezalel, Oholiab, and the Sabbath Sign]] · [[Show Me Your Glory]] · [[Bezalel's Workmen and the Surplus]] · [[The Altar, the Basin, the Court, and the Inventory]] (Exodus 38:1–31) · [[Penalties and a People Set Apart]]  · [[The Rebellion of Korah]]  · [[Aaron's Rod Buds]] · [[Joshua Commissioned]] · [[The Borders of the Land]] · [[Moses Pleads to Cross the Jordan]] · [[Has Any People Heard God and Lived]] · [[A People Chosen in Love]] · [[Not Because of Your Righteousness]]  · [[What Yahweh Requires of Israel]] · [[The Place Yahweh Will Choose]] · [[A Holy People and Its Food]] · [[The Law for the King]] · [[A People for Yahweh and a God for Israel]]  · [[Stones and an Altar on Mount Ebal]] · [[The Word Is Near and the Choice Is Life]] · [[Be Strong as Joshua Takes the Lead]] · [[After the Death of Moses]]  · [[Crossing the Jordan]] · [[The Twelve Stones from the Jordan]] |
| [[Circumcision]]  Joshua 5:10–12: Passover follows the circumcision and healing of 5:1–9. The sequence fits Exodus 12:48’s exclusion of the uncircumcised; it does not prove a complete absence of Passover observance throughout the wilderness period. | The covenant's sign, cut into the flesh on the eighth day of life and onto every male of the household including bought foreigners; a mark that can be perfectly kept and entirely missed, which is what Deuteronomy, Jeremiah, and Paul all say about it; and, in Genesis 34, proposed as a commercial term, performed on a whole Gentile town, and used on the third day as the means of destroying it; comparison with the Sabbath sign and cutting-off sanction (31:13–17); Leviticus 12: The son's circumcision takes place on the eighth day (3), repeating the timing of Genesis.  Deuteronomy 10:12–22: The command to circumcise the heart is immediately explained by the demand to cease being stiff-necked (16). | [[Circumcision, the Sign of the Covenant]] · [[The Birth of Isaac and the Sending of Hagar]] · [[Dinah and the Massacre at Shechem]] · [[Moses Returns to Egypt]] · [[I Am Yahweh]] · [[The Passover]] · [[The Death of the Firstborn and the Departure]] · [[Laws of Holiness and Compassion]] · [[Bezalel, Oholiab, and the Sabbath Sign]] · [[Purification After Childbirth]] · [[Covenant Warnings and Remembered Promises]] · [[The Passover in the Second Year]]  · [[What Yahweh Requires of Israel]] · [[Return Restoration and the Circumcised Heart]] · [[The Second Circumcision at Gilgal]] — Joshua 5:1–9: The second circumcision is national renewal across generations, not a second operation on already circumcised men (2–7). The departing generation had the sign; the wilderness-born children did not. The text gives no reason for the wilderness omission.  · [[Passover at Gilgal and the End of the Manna]] |
| [[City and Civilization]] | The first city and the founding of herding, music, and metalwork in Cain’s line; Proverbs 11:10–14 adds the city strengthened by upright people and wise counsel; Ecclesiastes 9:13–10:4: a little city delivered by a poor wise man; Song 6:4–10: Cities as measures of beauty; Song 6:11–7:9: Pools, gate, and towers  Leviticus 25:23–34: Walls mark a legal distinction: a walled-city house can be sold permanently after a year, while village houses return at Jubilee (29–31). Numbers 35: Forty-eight cities house the Levites and provide land for animals; six also protect killers awaiting adjudication or confined after it (2–15, 24–28). City boundaries become a legal boundary of safety (26–27).  Deuteronomy 4:41–43: Bezer, Ramoth, and Golan serve as places of protective flight (41–43). Urban space has a legal purpose in preserving the eligible fugitive’s life.  Joshua 20: The city gate is where elders hear the fugitive’s case; the city must receive him and give him a place to live (4).  Joshua 21: The cities include surrounding land for livestock (2, 42). This is settlement provision, not a register of new conquests. | [[The Line of Cain and the Birth of Seth]] · [[Noah's Sons and the Curse of Canaan]] · [[The Table of Nations]] · [[The Tower of Babel]] · [[Abram and Lot Separate]] · [[The Rescue of Lot and Melchizedek]] · [[Lot and His Daughters]] · [[The Months of Old]] · [[The Oppression in Egypt]] · [[Bricks Without Straw]] · [[Wisdom Calls in the Street]] · [[A False Balance]] (Proverbs 11:1–31) · [[A Strong Tower]] (Proverbs 18:10–11, 19) · [[The Forgotten Wise Man]] · [[Seeking Him by Night]] · [[I Opened, but He Had Gone]] · [[My Dove Is Unique]] · [[Return, Return, Shulammite]] (Song 6:11–7:9)  · [[Redemption of Land and Houses]] · [[The Levitical Cities and the Cities of Refuge]] (Numbers 35:1–34)  · [[Three Cities Beyond the Jordan]] · [[Refuge for the Unwitting Killer]]  · [[The Cities of Refuge Appointed]]  · [[The Cities of the Levites]] |
| [[Clean and Unclean]] | Ritual status need not imply wrongdoing; Leviticus 18 also describes moral defilement of persons and land; assumed at 7:2 with no definition, and conspicuously not used at 9:3, which grants every moving thing as food; the goat and its mother’s milk; the priests washed and the altar cleansed for seven days; Exodus 34: Goat-and-milk rule repeated without a stated explanation; Exodus 38: Basin made from mirrors; prescribed washing and later use remain distinct.; Proverbs 20: Moral purity language distinguished from ritual status; Proverbs 30: Self-declared purity exposed as moral filth, not a ritual rule; Ecclesiastes 9:1–12: Ritual distinction gives no exemption from death  Leviticus 1: Water washes the livestock victim’s innards and legs (1:9, 13), and the bird’s crop with its filth is discarded beside the altar (16). Leviticus 5:1–13: The ritual/moral distinction needs qualification: these particular hidden-contact cases incur guilt and require confession and a sin offering (2–6). This does not make every unclean condition a moral offense.  Leviticus 6:8–23: Ashes taken to a clean place outside the camp. Leviticus 6:24–7:10: Garments stained with sin-offering blood are washed in a holy place; earthen cooking vessels are broken and bronze ones scoured and rinsed (6:27–28). These are handling rules for most holy flesh, not a statement that the flesh is unclean or merely a hygiene prescription.; Leviticus 7:11–21: Peace-offering eating governed by time, purity, and sacred ownership  Leviticus 7:22–38: Fat from an animal that dies of itself or is torn may be used for other service but may not be eaten (24). Leviticus 8: Washing and altar purification prepare for service.; Leviticus 10:1–11: distinction explicitly assigned to priests; Leviticus 10:12–20: breast and thigh eaten in a clean place; Leviticus 11: edible species distinguished from carcass impurity; Leviticus 12: Childbirth brings a temporary ritual status: seven days followed by thirty-three for a son,.; Leviticus 13:1–46: skin signs, repeated inspection, and clean-verdict exceptions ; Leviticus 13:47–59: garment inspection and handling; Leviticus 14:1–32: restoration after healing ; Leviticus 14:33–57: house inspection, repair, and cleansing  Leviticus 15: distinct discharge, emission, and menstrual rules;; Leviticus 17: Eating an animal that died or was torn requires washing clothes, bathing, and waiting until evening (15).; Leviticus 18: The earlier nonmoral definition needs qualification: Leviticus 12 and 15 describe bodily conditions without necessarily identifying an offense, while 18:20–30 expressly associates defilement with prohibited conduct and the land.; Leviticus 19:19–37: Seeking mediums or wizards is said to defile (31).; Leviticus 20: The separation of clean and unclean animals is joined to Israel’s separation from the peoples (24–26).; Leviticus 21: Corpse defilement has a limited close-kin exception for ordinary priests (1–3) and none for the high priest even for parents (11).; Numbers 6: a sudden death nearby voids the days of a vow; at Numbers 9 uncleanness delays a duty without cancelling it; Numbers 18: Clean members of Aaron’s household may eat the wave offerings and first fruits (11–13); the most holy portions have a narrower male entitlement (9–10).  Numbers 19: Corpse impurity requires third- and seventh-day sprinkling; preparers incur temporary impurity. Numbers 35: Blood pollutes the inhabited land (33–34). This moral liability differs from the corpse-contact impurity treated by Numbers 19: homicide is not remedied here by washing or ash-water sprinkling. | [[Noah and the Flood]] · [[The Covenant with Noah]] · [[Return to Bethel; the Deaths of Rachel and Isaac]] · [[The Second Journey]] · [[The Second Test]] · [[Zophar's First Speech]] · [[Though He Slay Me]] · [[Bildad's Third Speech]] · [[The Burning Bush]] · [[Livestock, Boils, and Hail]] · [[Israel at Mount Sinai]] · [[Laws of Holiness and Compassion]] · [[Justice, Sabbaths, and Feasts]] · [[The Ordination of the Priests]] · [[The Incense Altar, the Ransom, and the Holy Oil]] · [[The Covenant Renewed]] · [[The Altar, the Basin, the Court, and the Inventory]] (Exodus 38:1–31) · [[The Basin Made and Used - Exodus 30, 38, and 40]] · [[The Tabernacle Raised and the Glory]] (Exodus 40:1–38) · [[Wine Is a Mocker]] (Proverbs 20:9, 11, 30) · [[The Words of Agur]] (Proverbs 30:12) · [[One Event Comes to All]]  · [[The Burnt Offering]] · [[The Sin Offering for Priest and Congregation]]  · [[Confession and Offerings According to Means]]  · [[The Altar Fire and the Priests’ Meal Offerings]] · [[Penalties and a People Set Apart]] Deuteronomy 12:1–28: Meat at home eaten by unclean and clean alike, like game (15, 22). |; Leviticus 4:1–21: The carcass is burned outside the camp at a clean place where ashes are deposited (12). · [[The Priests’ Portions of Sin and Trespass Offerings]] · [[Thanksgiving, Vows, and the Peace Offering]]  · [[Fat, Blood, and the Priests’ Share]]  · [[Aaron and His Sons Consecrated]] · [[Nadab and Abihu and the Strange Fire]] · [[The Uneaten Sin Offering]] · [[Clean and Unclean Animals]] (Leviticus 11:1–47) · [[Purification After Childbirth]] · [[Examining Diseases of the Skin]]  · [[Mildew in Garments]] · [[The Cleansing of the Leper]] · [[Mildew in a House]]  · [[Bodily Discharges and Cleansing]] · [[The Place of Sacrifice and the Life in the Blood]] · [[Forbidden Unions and the Defiled Land]] · [[Holy Living in Field and Community]] · [[The Holiness of the Priests]] · [[Eating the Holy Things]] · [[Vows, Valuations, and Devoted Things]] · [[The Unclean Sent Out and Wrongs Repaid]] · [[The Suspected Wife]] · [[The Nazirite Vow]] · [[The Lamps and the Levites Cleansed]] · [[The Passover in the Second Year]] · [[Miriam and Aaron Against Moses]] · [[The Duties and Portions of Priests and Levites]]  · [[The Red Heifer]] · [[The War Against Midian]] · [[The Levitical Cities and the Cities of Refuge]] (Numbers 35:1–34) · [[The Place Yahweh Will Choose]] · [[A Holy People and Its Food]] · [[Consecrate the Firstborn of the Herd and Flock]] · [[Captives, Firstborn Sons, and Public Shame]] · [[Care for the Neighbor and Ordered Distinctions]] · [[The Assembly and the Holy Camp]] · [[Divorce, Remarriage, and the Newlywed]] · [[Pledges, Wages, Justice, and Gleaning]] · [[First Fruits and the Tithe Declaration]] |
| [[Compassion and Mercy]] | *Raḥămîm*, a plural noun built on *reḥem*, womb — not pity from above and not forgiveness, but the involuntary turn of the insides toward someone in trouble; Genesis's densest cluster of the vocabulary is a chapter in which nobody forgives anybody, where a father in Canaan asks God for it and a son in Egypt has it kindled in him sixteen verses later and leaves the room; help, food, and refreshment; The people are spared the announced destruction through Moses’ appeal to God’s deliverance and promise (32:11–14); Exodus 33: Freely given mercy is part of God’s self-disclosure; Exodus 34: Mercy and patience proclaimed with forgiveness and accountability; Proverbs 3:21–35: Timely help is required when the good owed is within reach; Proverbs 12:10 extends righteous care to an animal’s life; Proverbs 19: Overlooking an offense and pity for the poor join practical kindness to divine reward; Proverbs 21: The wicked show no mercy; kindness is pursued; Proverbs 22: The generous eye shares food with the poor; Proverbs 24:11–12: rescue those led away to death; the plea of ignorance refused; Proverbs 28: Wealth accumulated by excessive interest is destined for someone who pities the poor (8); mercy meets confessed and renounced sin (13); giving to the poor is set against deliberately closed eyes (27); Proverbs 31:6–9: Attention to bitter suffering must accompany practical justice; Proverbs 31:20, 26: Generosity accompanies wise instruction; Ecclesiastes 4:1–3: The missing comforter within an unequal distribution of power Leviticus 19:1–18: Leaving food for the poor and foreigner and refusing to abuse deaf or blind people (9–10, 14) give protection practical form. These are thematic examples of care; no claim is made that a particular Hebrew mercy word occurs here.; Numbers 16:41–50: Aaron’s urgent action benefits his accusers (41, 47). This is a thematic reading of rescue  Numbers 21:4–9: The God who sends the deadly snakes also provides a way for the bitten to live (6–9). Mercy is a thematic description of this rescue; it does not cancel the deaths already narrated. Numbers 22:22–35: The donkey’s evasions preserve the rider who strikes her. Numbers 35: The congregation must deliver the unwitting killer from the avenger and restore him to refuge (24–25). Protection includes resident foreigners (15), yet requires residence until the high priest dies. Mercy here describes the protection, not an explicit forgiveness formula. Deuteronomy 4:15–31: *Yahweh your God is a merciful God*, the ground of return from exile (31).  Deuteronomy 4:41–43: Protection is made accessible in three named cities for the unwitting killer without previous hatred (41–43). This is preservation of life, not an explicit pronouncement of forgiveness or exemption for deliberate murder. Deuteronomy 7:1–11: The command to show no mercy to the seven nations (2) stands in tension with Yahweh’s mercy toward returning Israel in Deuteronomy 4:31 and his covenant loving kindness here (9). Deuteronomy 7:12–26: *Your eye shall not pity them* (16) repeats 7:2's refusal of mercy, in standing tension with 4:31. Deuteronomy 9:7–29: Yahweh listens to Moses despite the people’s evil (18–19). Preservation rests on the appealed-to relationship and redemption (26–29), not a finding that Israel deserves survival.  Deuteronomy 10:1–11: Yahweh does not destroy Israel, restores the written words, and sends the people onward (1–5, 10–11). Mercy takes the form of continued covenant life without denying the preceding rebellion.  Deuteronomy 10:12–22: The awesome ruler gives food and clothing to the foreigner and upholds widow and fatherless (17–18).  Deuteronomy 32: Yahweh has compassion on servants whose power is gone (36). Unlike the explicit return of 30:1–3, this line names helplessness rather than a prior confession. These are different emphases, not grounds to delete either account of restoration.  Joshua 2: Rahab appeals to the kindness she has shown and asks reciprocal protection for her whole household (12–14). Mercy here takes the concrete form of a pledged exception within impending conquest.  Joshua 6: Rahab’s household survives the destruction because she hid the messengers (17, 25), and Joshua enforces the spies’ pledge (22). This concrete exception does not soften the report that other young and old are killed (21). Joshua 20: Elders must give the unintentional killer a place to live and refuse to surrender him to the pursuing avenger (4–5).  Joshua 21: The allocation includes the six refuges from Joshua 20: Hebron, Shechem, Golan, Kedesh, Bezer, and Ramoth. This chapter does not repeat the admission and trial procedures. | [[The Reconciliation with Esau]] · [[The Second Journey]] · [[Joseph Reveals Himself]] · [[The Deaths of Jacob and Joseph]] · [[Seven | [[Covenant]]  Joshua 5:10–12: The renewed sign in 5:1–9 is followed by Passover and eating in the promised land (10–12). This is continuity of covenant life, not a newly narrated covenant ceremony or new terms. | The land is sworn to the fathers (1), and the power to acquire wealth serves the establishment of that covenant (18). The warning that Israel may perish (19–20) remains alongside the oath, not erased by it. Deuteronomy 9:1–6: The sworn word to Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob is explicitly a reason for dispossession and Israel’s entry (5). The text joins this fidelity to judgment on the nations, without praising Israel’s conduct. Deuteronomy 9:7–29: The tablets carry the words of the assembly and are explicitly covenant tablets (9–11, 15). Their public breaking (17) stands beside Moses’ appeal to the ancestral servants and the people as Yahweh’s inheritance (26–29).  Deuteronomy 10:1–11: Replacement tablets contain the former words spoken at the assembly (1–5). The covenant ark preserves them; continued travel toward the sworn land follows the threat of destruction (10–11). Restoration maintains rather than replaces the commandments.  Deuteronomy 10:12–22: The fathers’ loved and chosen offspring must answer with wholehearted obedience (12–16). Deuteronomy 11:18–32: blessing and curse set before Israel and given a place in the land (26–29). Deuteronomy 26:16–19: Reciprocal declarations join prior promise and present obligation.  Deuteronomy 27: Written law, offerings, and a meal accompany the commanded ceremony in the land (2–8).  Deuteronomy 27:11–26: All the people are commanded to assent to each curse (15–26). Public agreement makes the community acknowledge obligations reaching secret actions as well as visible ones; the ceremony remains prospective. Deuteronomy 28: conditional blessing in the land.  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: Land, multiplication, freedom, and national standing are all threatened with reversal (36, 43–44, 62–68). Destruction language stands in tension with Leviticus 26:44–45, where Yahweh refuses utter destruction and covenant cancellation; the witnesses are retained without making this unit a final declaration of abandonment. Deuteronomy 29: The Moab covenant is additional to Horeb (1), grounded in the ancestral oath (13), and embraces those present and absent (14–15). Belonging includes women, children, and resident foreigners (10–11); it gives no immunity to secret apostasy.  Deuteronomy 31: The land is sworn to the fathers (7), and divine presence grounds courage (6, 8). Tension retained: 31:16–18 threatens forsaking in response to covenant abandonment. The assurance here cannot simply cancel that warning. Deuteronomy 31:9–13: The written law is entrusted to those who bear the ark of Yahweh’s covenant and to the elders (9). Covenant continuity includes the whole listening community and children who do not yet know (12–13), not only the preservation of a document.  Deuteronomy 31:14–23: Israel will forsake Yahweh and break his covenant after entering the land (16, 20). Divine forsaking in response (17–18) remains in explicit tension with the assurance of 31:6, 8, while Joshua again receives promised presence (23). The song preserves testimony against covenant breach.  Deuteronomy 32: The song unfolds the breach predicted in 31:16–21: the cared-for people abandon their maker (15–18). The faithful Rock’s justice (4) exposes their corruption (5). The forecast does not withdraw the concluding command to obey and teach children (46–47).  Deuteronomy 33: Levi’s keeping of the covenant grounds praise (9). The national blessing follows the warnings of breach, preserving both promised care and responsibility.  Joshua 3: The covenant ark leads Israel into the river (3, 6, 11, 17). Covenant presence accompanies entry; this is no new covenant ceremony and no new set of terms is given. Joshua 4: The covenant ark stands at the center of the event remembered by the stones (7, 9, 18). This preserves covenant memory without instituting a new covenant ceremony. · [[The Gibeonite Deception]] Joshua 9: Joshua makes a covenant to let the envoys live (15), only afterward discovering that they are local Hivites (7, 16). This conflicts with Deuteronomy 7:1–2’s prohibition of a covenant with those peoples. The leaders preserve the oath rather than kill them (18–20); the narrative retains this tension without declaring the original treaty lawful. Joshua 10: The peace with Gibeon becomes the reason for the Amorite attack (1, 4). Joshua now defends the people he promised to preserve. Yahweh’s promised help (8) supports the rescue without expressly declaring the treaty’s deceptive origin lawful; the tension with Deuteronomy 7:1–2 remains. | [[Noah and the Flood]] · [[The Covenant Covenant custody preserves the standard by which the people are accused, rather than guaranteeing their fidelity.  · [[The Song of Moses]]  · [[Moses Blesses the Tribes]]  · [[Crossing the Jordan]] · [[The Twelve Stones from the Jordan]] · [[The Second Circumcision at Gilgal]] — Joshua 5:1–9: Read beside Genesis 17:8–14, the land and the bodily sign belong together. Joshua 5 renews observance among the children; it does not state new covenant terms or narrate a new covenant-making ceremony.  · [[Passover at Gilgal and the End of the Manna]]  · [[The Law Read at Mount Ebal]] — Joshua 8:30–35: altar, inscription, and the whole assembly hearing the law. · [[The Sun Stands Still at Gibeon]] |
| [[Creation]] | The world made by God's word out of formlessness, ordered in six days, declared good — run backwards day by day in the flood, and quoted back with amendments when it restarts; at Sinai, creation supplies the Sabbath pattern and the scope of the image prohibition; six-day creation grounds the Sabbath sign (31:17); Proverbs 3:13–20: Earth, heavens, depths, and dew ordered through divine wisdom.; Proverbs 8:22–31: Wisdom present as heavens, the deep's circle, and the sea's boundary are made; Proverbs 20: Yahweh makes the capacities by which people hear and see; Proverbs 22: Yahweh the maker of rich and poor; Proverbs 24:3–4: the house built with creation's three gifts; Proverbs 30: Wind, waters, and the ends of the earth become questions about the identity and power of their governor; Proverbs 30:15–31: insatiable things, traceless ways, small and stately creatures; Ecclesiastes 1:1–11: Earth and recurring natural movements outlast their human observers; Ecclesiastes 3:16–22: Common origin in dust and shared creaturely mortality; Ecclesiastes 11:7–12:8: The Creator remembered; the given spirit returns to God. Deuteronomy 4:15–31: the forbidden likenesses list Genesis 1's creatures and lights in nearly reverse order (16–19). | [[The Creation of the World]] · [[The Garden of Eden]] · [[From Adam to Noah]] · [[The Sons of God and the Corruption of the Earth]] · [[Noah and the Flood]] · [[The Covenant with Noah]] · [[Let the Day Perish]] · [[There Is No Umpire Between Us]] · [[Though He Slay Me]] · [[Bildad's Third Speech]] · [[I Will Not Put Away My Integrity]] · [[Where Shall Wisdom Be Found]] · [[Elihu on God's Justice]] · [[Elihu on God's Greatness]] · [[Out of the Whirlwind]] · [[Behemoth and Leviathan]] · [[The Birth of Moses]] · [[The Burning Bush]] · [[Aaron's Staff Before Pharaoh]] · [[Water Turned to Blood]] · [[Frogs, Lice, and Flies]] · [[Livestock, Boils, and Hail]] · [[Locusts and Darkness]] · [[The Passover]] · [[The Crossing of the Sea]] · [[The Song of the Sea]] · [[The Ten Commandments]] · [[Bezalel, Oholiab, and the Sabbath Sign]] · [[The Work Completed]] (Exodus 39:32–43) · [[The Tabernacle Raised and the Glory]] (Exodus 40:1–38) · [[Wisdom's Worth and the Founding of the Earth]] · [[Wisdom at the Creation]] · [[A Way That Seems Right]] (Proverbs 14:31) · [[Wine Is a Mocker]] (Proverbs 20:12) · [[A Good Name]] (Proverbs 22:2) · [[Through Wisdom a House Is Built]] · [[Where There Is No Vision]] (Proverbs 29:13) · [[The Words of Agur]] (Proverbs 30:4) · [[Four Things]] (Proverbs 30:15–31) · [[Vanity of Vanities]] · [[From Dust to Dust]] (Ecclesiastes 3:16–22) · [[Cast Your Bread on the Waters]] · [[Remember Your Creator]] · [[No Form Seen and No Image Made]] · [[Has Any People Heard God and Lived]] |
| [[Curse]] | The counter-word to blessing; God's alone until 9:25, when a man says it over a grandson who was not in the room; Proverbs 3:21–35: The wicked house is under Yahweh’s curse, without making disaster proof of guilt.; Proverbs 20: A parental curse brings an extinguished lamp upon the speaker; Proverbs 24:24–25: the partial judge cursed by the peoples; Proverbs 26:2: the undeserved curse does not come to rest; Proverbs 30: The servant’s curse warns the slanderer against provoking a morally serious complaint (10); the next saying condemns those who curse their father (11); Ecclesiastes 10:20: the king and the rich not cursed even in thought, for words travel; Leviticus 26: the fullest catalog so far, ending in exile and a refusal to destroy ; Numbers 22:1–21: Balak seeks a curse as preparation for battle and expulsion (6, 11).  Numbers 22:36–23:26: Balak’s repeated demand is denied twice.  Numbers 23:27–24:25: The curse sought by Balak remains unavailable. Instead, 24:9 declares those who curse Israel cursed, recalling the protective pattern of Genesis 12:3 and 27:29 without claiming an explicit citation. Deuteronomy 27:11–26: Twelve curses receive twelve communal responses (15–26). These are covenant sanctions Israel must acknowledge, not hostile magic directed at another nation. The last curse embraces failure to uphold the law by doing it.  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: The opening curses reverse city, field, basket, offspring, and coming and going from the blessing list (15–19). The expanded sanctions overtake disobedience and reach exile and return to Egypt (45–68). Deuteronomy 29: The person who hears the curse and inwardly claims peace is singled out for the book’s sanctions (19–21). The national devastation that follows exposes the emptiness of that private self-blessing.  Joshua 6: Taking devoted things threatens to make Israel’s camp accursed (18). Joshua also pronounces loss of firstborn and youngest son upon Jericho’s rebuilder (26). The two sanctions concern different acts; neither should be collapsed into a general curse on all residents. Joshua 8:30–35: altar and inclusive public reading. Joshua 9: Joshua pronounces a curse assigning continuing servitude (23). Unlike the death imposed on Jericho’s rebuilder in Joshua 6:26, this sanction preserves the recipients’ lives while binding their labor. The text does not explicitly identify it as fulfillment of Noah’s curse. | [[The Fall]] · [[Cain and Abel]] · [[The Line of Cain and the Birth of Seth]] · [[From Adam to Noah]] · [[Noah's Sons and the Curse of Canaan]] · [[The Table of Nations]] · [[Bildad's Second Speech]] · [[The Call of Abram]] · [[Abram in Egypt]] · [[The Stolen Blessing]] · [[Jacob Blesses His Sons]] · [[The Deaths of Jacob and Joseph]] · [[Let the Day Perish]] · [[Job's Oath of Innocence]] · [[Laws of Holiness and Compassion]] · [[Do Not Withhold Good]] · [[A False Balance]] (Proverbs 11:1–31) · [[Wine Is a Mocker]] (Proverbs 20:20) · [[The Field of the Sluggard]] · [[Answer a Fool]] · [[The Words of Agur]] (Proverbs 30:10–11) · [[Folly in High Places and Everyday Work]] · [[Covenant Warnings and Remembered Promises]] · [[The Suspected Wife]]  · [[Balak Sends for Balaam]]  · [[Balaam's First Two Oracles]]  · [[Balaam's Last Oracles]] · [[Set These Words Before You]] · [[The Gibeonite Deception]] | Deuteronomy 11:18–32: a curse for following other gods, to be set on Mount Ebal (26–29). · [[Captives, Firstborn Sons, and Public Shame]] · [[The Assembly and the Holy Camp]]  · [[The People Answer Amen to the Curses]]  · [[Curses for Disobedience]] · [[The Covenant in Moab and the Hidden Things]] · [[Return Restoration and the Circumcised Heart]] · [[The Word Is Near and the Choice Is Life]]  · [[The Fall of Jericho]]  · [[The Law Read at Mount Ebal]] |
| [[Deception]] | Producing a false belief without saying anything false — the true half-statement, the substituted person, the borrowed clothing; Genesis's chosen line does it for four generations and is never disowned for it; *bĕmirmâ* passes from Isaac's verdict on Jacob to the narrator's on Jacob's sons; at Sinai, false testimony is named as a wrong against a neighbor; a master who *"has dealt deceitfully"* with a female servant; falsehood made judicial; Aaron’s excuse contradicts the narrated act; Proverbs 6:12–19: winking signs, a lying tongue, a false witness.; Proverbs 10:9–10, 18: A perverted course is exposed, the wink causes sorrow, and hatred hidden behind lying lips is condemned; Proverbs 19: The liar ranks below the poor; corrupt witness attacks justice; Proverbs 20: False measures and buyers whose boasting contradicts their bargaining; Proverbs 21: Treasure by a lying tongue and the false witness; Proverbs 23:3, 7: bread of lies, and a host whose heart is not with you; Proverbs 26:23–26: the glazed pot and the hater disguised by his lips; Proverbs 30: Agur asks for distance from falsehood and lies; Proverbs 30:20: self-acquittal where no trace is left; Proverbs 31:30: Charm is an unreliable measure of worth  Leviticus 6:1–7: False dealings over entrusted or lost property and false swearing compound material injury (2–5). Correcting the falsehood includes returning what belongs to the neighbor; a verbal assurance alone is not the prescribed remedy. Leviticus 19:1–18: Lying and deceiving one another are explicitly forbidden beside stealing (11). Unlike narratives reporting deception without a verdict, this instruction directly prohibits it. Deuteronomy 5: False testimony against a neighbor is prohibited (20). Deuteronomy 11:1–17: "lest your heart be deceived" into serving other gods (16).  Joshua 2: Rahab says the men left at dusk while she has actually hidden them under flax on her roof (4–6). The narrative makes the false report plain but does not pronounce a general rule about lying. Protection of life and deception remain an ethical tension, not a reason to erase either feature. · [[The Gibeonite Deception]] Joshua 9: Old clothes, patched equipment, and moldy bread support an explicitly false claim of distant origin (4–13, 22). Fear for their lives explains the Gibeonites’ action (24); Joshua still condemns it and imposes servitude. Unlike the divinely commanded ambush at Ai, this ruse exploits Israel’s failure to seek counsel. | [[The Surety, the Sluggard, and the Seven Abominations]] · [[The Fall]] · [[Cain and Abel]] · [[Abram | [[Divine Grief and Relenting]] | Whether God can change his mind, and whether he can be hurt — *nāḥam* and *ʿāṣab* used of God for the first time at 6:6; Exodus 32:14 explicitly reports that Yahweh repented of the evil he had announced; Exodus 33: The announced course changes in response to intercession; Exodus 34: Renewal follows relenting; Jonah later joins mercy to relenting  Numbers 22:36–23:26: Numbers 23:19 denies that God lies or repents in the context of reversing Israel’s blessing (20). Deuteronomy 9:7–29: Destruction is threatened (14), but Moses reports that Yahweh listened (19). Unlike Exodus 32:14, the stored WEB here describes the outcome as listening; no equivalence of source-language verbs is asserted. | [[The Sons of God and the Corruption of the Earth]] · [[I Repent in Dust and Ashes]] · [[The Restoration of Job]] · [[The Burning Bush]] · [[The Crossing of the Sea]] · [[The Golden Calf]] · [[Show Me Your Glory]] · [[The Covenant Renewed]] · [[The People Refuse the Land]]  · [[Balaam's First Two Oracles]] · [[Remember the Calf and Moses' Intercession]] |
| [[The Dwelling of God]] | God living in the middle of his people — residence, not only visitation or accompaniment; *šākan*, *to dwell*, and its noun *miškān*, the tabernacle; the glory that settled on Sinai announced at Exodus 25:8 as *"that I may dwell among them"*, which the Septuagint renders *"I will be seen among you"*; its furniture designed first, the ark as the place where God will *meet* Moses and speak; The promised dwelling becomes an assembled tent with an inner boundary; The dwelling includes an outer court and a continuing service of light; the priest clothed for entering the holy place; *"I will dwell among the children of Israel"* as the purpose of the exodus; makers equipped and appointed for the sanctuary (31:1–11); Exodus 33: The outside tent and restored promise of presence; Exodus 34: Presence requested again; the promised house is still unbuilt; Exodus 34:29–35: Recurring entry before Yahweh continues before the sanctuary is built.; Exodus 35: The promised sanctuary now receives its materials and public commission; Gifted workers and sufficient materials serve the promised sanctuary; Exodus 36:8–38: the enclosure is made; final erection and indwelling glory remain ahead; Exodus 37: Furniture made; installation, service, and filling remain distinct.; Exodus 38: Outer equipment and court made; erection and filling remain distinct; Exodus 39: Clothing for holy-place service prepared before the sanctuary’s erection.; Ecclesiastes 5:1–2: going to God's house, while God is in heaven  Leviticus 1: Yahweh speaks from the Tent of Meeting (1:1), and the door and its altar become the place of accepted approach (3–5).; Leviticus 2: The sanctuary altar of the continuing speech receives grain memorials as well as animal offerings (2:2, 8–9). Produce is brought to Yahweh through priestly service; a most holy remainder stays with the priests.; Leviticus 3: The Tent of Meeting is the place of approach, slaughter, and priestly altar service (3:2, 8, 13); Leviticus 4:1–21: The tent’s interior is involved in the remedy for priestly and communal sin: blood is brought before the veil and to the inner altar (5–7, 16–18). · [[The Sin Offering for Priest and Congregation]]; Leviticus 4:22–35: Remedy at the outer altar without interior blood rite.  Leviticus 6:8–23: Maintained fire, restricted holy eating, and wholly burned priestly grain. Leviticus 6:24–7:10: The court is the authorized place to eat sin-offering flesh (6:26). Blood entering the Tent of Meeting for atonement marks the exception requiring burning (6:30); different sanctuary spaces carry different rules.  Leviticus 8: Sanctuary and servants prepared together.; Leviticus 9: inaugural offerings, blessing, and public manifestation; Leviticus 10:1–11: guarded approach and continued service; Leviticus 10:12–20: sanctuary spaces govern priestly eating; Leviticus 12: The sanctuary is temporarily closed to the mother during purification; afterward she.; Leviticus 14:1–32: restoration after healing   Leviticus 15: tabernacle defilement grounds the death warning;; Leviticus 17: The door of the Tent of Meeting is the required place of sacrifice (4–9).; Leviticus 19:19–37: The trespass offering is brought to the Tent of Meeting’s door (21), and reverence for the sanctuary is commanded (30).; Leviticus 20: Giving offspring to Molech is said to defile God’s sanctuary (3), although no entry into it is described.; Leviticus 21: The high priest must not leave or profane the sanctuary in the context of the death and mourning instructions (10–12).  Leviticus 24:1–9: The lamps stand outside the veil in the Tent of Meeting (3), while bread is arranged before Yahweh on the gold table (6). Divine dwelling calls for recurring care, not only the construction of furniture; holy bread then sustains the priestly family.; *my tent among you* promised while the tent already stands (Leviticus 26:11); Numbers 1: the tabernacle carried by the Levites; Numbers 7:89: the voice heard inside the Tent; Numbers 16:41–50: The Tent is the point of appearance and divine speech (42–45), and the place to which Aaron  Numbers 17: The rods are laid before the testimony where Yahweh meets Moses (4, 7), and Aaron’s is retained there (10).; Numbers 18: The Tent of Meeting is guarded through assigned responsibilities (1–7, 22–23).  Numbers 19: Purification outside the camp protects the sanctuary within it.  Numbers 22:36–23:26: The second oracle declares Yahweh present with Israel (23:21).  Numbers 27:1–11: The Tent of Meeting's entrance is the public setting for the petition before Moses, Eleazar, princes, and congregation (2). The daughters' approach for judgment is not described as entry into the sanctuary's restricted interior. Numbers 35: Yahweh’s presence among Israel makes the inhabited land subject to a prohibition of defilement (34). Homicide law is therefore tied to divine dwelling beyond the sanctuary precinct itself.  Deuteronomy 1:34–46: Yahweh says he is not among the proposed attackers (42). Numbers 14:44 adds the ark and Moses remaining in camp; Deuteronomy does not mention the ark here.  Deuteronomy 10:1–11: The wooden ark receives the covenant tablets and is borne by Levi (1–5, 8). No tabernacle construction or glory descent is narrated here. Exodus 37:1 and 40:20 give different construction detail and a later narrative setting for depositing the testimony. Deuteronomy 12:1–28: One place chosen *"to put his name there"*; never named in Deuteronomy (5, 11).  Deuteronomy 31:14–23: Yahweh appears in a pillar of cloud over the Tent's door (15), yet announces the future hiding of his face and Israel's recognition of his absence (17–18). Manifest presence at the commissioning is no guarantee against withdrawal after covenant breach.  Deuteronomy 33: God himself is Israel’s dwelling place, with everlasting arms beneath (27); Benjamin is covered in safety (12). Neither image requires a named temple site.  Joshua 3: The living God’s presence among Israel is shown through the passage of the covenant ark (10–13). The title Lord of all the earth broadens the horizon beyond a local river or territory. Joshua 4: The ark remains with its priests in the river until the crossing is complete (10–11). The narrative does not identify the Gilgal stones as a sanctuary.  Joshua 6: The covenant ark occupies the procession’s center, and the reserved metals enter the treasury of Yahweh’s house (6–13, 24). The wording does not establish a stone temple at Jericho. Joshua 18:1–10: The whole congregation establishes the Tent of Meeting at Shiloh (1), where Joshua casts lots before Yahweh (10). The text narrates a sanctuary location without describing a permanent stone temple or a new glory-cloud manifestation.  Joshua 19:49–51: The Tent’s door at Shiloh is the setting of distribution before Yahweh (51). Sanctuary and public administration meet without a report of a new glory manifestation or a stone temple. | [[The Blood of the Covenant]] · [[The Offering for the Sanctuary]] · [[The Ark, the Table, | [[Envy]] | *Qānāʾ* — one Hebrew word for both envy and jealousy, whose first occurrence in Scripture is a Philistine reaction to a hundredfold harvest; it drives Rachel and Joseph's brothers, and then becomes a name for God; at Sinai, God’s claim to exclusive worship differs from coveting a neighbor’s household; Proverbs 3:21–35: The violent man must be neither envied nor imitated; Proverbs 6:34: the husband's *qinʾâ*, jealousy that no gift appeases; Proverbs 23:17; 24:1: envy of sinners answered by the fear of Yahweh and a future; Proverbs 24:19: do not fret because of evildoers; Ecclesiastes 4:4: all labor and achievement the envy of a neighbor; Ecclesiastes 9:1–12: Envy ends with the dead’s earthly portion Deuteronomy 5: The demand not to covet reaches a neighbor’s wife, house, field, servants, animals, and everything belonging to the neighbor (21). | [[Isaac in Gerar]] · [[The Birth of Jacob's Children]] · [[Jacob's Flight from Laban]] · [[Joseph Sold into Slavery]] · [[The Brothers' First Journey to Egypt]] · [[The Second Journey]] · [[The Silver Cup and Judah's Plea]] · [[Joseph Reveals Himself]] · [[The Oppression in Egypt]] · [[The Ten Commandments]] · [[Do Not Withhold Good]] · [[The Lamp of the Commandment]] · [[A Way That Seems Right]] (Proverbs 14:30) · [[Do Not Envy Sinners]] · [[Through Wisdom a House Is Built]] · [[Do Not Boast About Tomorrow]] · [[Two Are Better Than One]] (Ecclesiastes 4:4–12) · [[One Event Comes to All]] · [[Love Is Strong as Death]] (Song 8:5–7) · [[The Suspected Wife]] · [[The Seventy Elders and the Quail]] · [[Miriam and Aaron Against Moses]] · [[The Ten Commandments Recounted]] |
| [[Faith]] | Leaning your weight on someone and finding they hold; *heʾĕmin*'s first occurrence is Genesis 15:6, and the same chapter has the man who believed ask for proof four verses later; Exodus 34: Land security during pilgrimage requires reliance on the promise; trust with the whole heart, not leaning on one's own understanding (Proverbs 3:5–6); Proverbs 3:21–35: Yahweh grounds confidence when sudden fear threatens.; Proverbs 20: Trust expressed through waiting and limited knowledge of the way; Proverbs 22:19: trust in Yahweh as the stated goal of instruction; Proverbs 28: Trust in Yahweh is contrasted with greed that produces conflict; trust in oneself is contrasted with walking in wisdom; Proverbs 30: God as shield; trust without an inference that all harm is excluded; Proverbs 31:11–12: A husband’s confidence rests on the woman’s trustworthy conduct  Leviticus 25:1–22: The question about food (20) meets a promise of sufficient old stores (21–22).  Numbers 20:1–13: Yahweh’s verdict names the leaders’ failure to believe in him (12), despite their receiving the command and producing water.  Numbers 21:1–3: Dependence on Yahweh for victory contrasts with the earlier presumptuous ascent.  Numbers 21:4–9: Looking at the appointed serpent brings life (8–9). Reading this response as trust is an interpretation of the action; the stored WEB describes looking, while John 3:14–17 explicitly develops belief.; Numbers 21:21–35: Yahweh’s command not to fear Og grounds confidence in promised delivery and the already completed victory over Sihon (34). The text records the assurance and outcome without describing Moses’ inner emotional response.  Deuteronomy 1:34–46: Caleb’s wholehearted following is set against the condemned generation (36). The surrounding narrative contrasts the refusal to trust in 1:32 with an armed initiative that ignores the present word in 1:42–43.  Deuteronomy 3: The command not to fear is grounded in promised delivery and the precedent of Sihon (2). The text reports reassurance and victory, without describing Moses’ private emotional response. Deuteronomy 3:12–22: Joshua’s encouragement rests on victories his own eyes have seen (21–22). The call to courage is an argument from remembered divine action, not a claim that Joshua never experienced fear. Deuteronomy 9:1–6: The saying about the Anakim’s invincibility is answered with assurance that Yahweh goes ahead (2–3). Confidence is grounded in his action, not a denial of the enemy’s strength or a boast of Israel’s merit. Deuteronomy 9:7–29: At Kadesh, rebellion is described as neither believing Yahweh nor listening to his voice (23). Faith here is inseparable from response to the command to possess the land.  Deuteronomy 31: Courage is grounded in Yahweh’s continuing presence rather than in Moses’ continued leadership (6–8). This is a thematic description of trust, not a claim that the WEB uses the noun faith here. Deuteronomy 31:14–23: Joshua is commanded to be strong and courageous because God will be with him as he brings Israel into the land (23). Trust describes the response invited by the promise, not a vocabulary item asserted to occur here.  Joshua 2: Rahab’s confession that Yahweh has given Israel the land becomes action: she shelters the spies and asks protection for her family (9–14). Hebrews 11:31 later interprets her reception of them as faith; Joshua itself narrates the confession and risk.  Joshua 6: Marching precedes the visible collapse (3–20). Hebrews 11:30–31 explicitly reads both the encircling and Rahab’s survival through faith (WEB verified at bible-api.com, 2026-11-10). This is a later interpretation, not a claim about Hebrew vocabulary in Joshua. Joshua 14:6–15: Caleb contrasts the discouraging report with his own loyalty and requests the very hill country remembered for formidable inhabitants (7–12). His confidence includes dependence on Yahweh’s presence rather than a claim that age or strength guarantees victory.  Joshua 17:14–18: Joshua’s assurance challenges the house of Joseph’s focus on enemy strength (16–18). Confidence is invited, but the passage neither names their response as faith nor records their subsequent obedience. | [[The Covenant Between the Pieces]] · [[The Call of Abram]] · [[Noah and the | [[Famine and Plenty]] | Hunger and manna teach dependence (3); the good land promises bread without scarcity (7–10). Fullness is to produce gratitude, but can instead produce forgetting (11–14). Deuteronomy 11:1–17: early and late rain or a shut sky, according to loyalty. Deuteronomy 28: conditional blessing in the land.  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: Failed rain, insects, confiscated harvests, and siege hunger undo plenty (23–24, 33, 38–42, 51–57). The children themselves become food in the warning; the text describes familial devastation rather than approving it. | [[Abram in Egypt]] · [[Isaac in Gerar]] · [[Pharaoh's Dreams and Joseph's Rise]] · [[The Brothers' First Journey to Egypt]] · [[The Second Journey]] · [[Joseph Reveals Himself]] · [[Jacob's Family Settles in Egypt]] · [[Joseph and the Famine]] · [[Elihu on God's Greatness]] · [[Out of the Whirlwind]] · [[Manna and Quail]] · [[A False Balance]] (Proverbs 11:1–31) · [[Whoever Loves Correction]] (Proverbs 12:9, 11, 27) · [[Hope Deferred]] (Proverbs 13:4, 23, 25) · [[The Words of Agur]] (Proverbs 30:8–9) · [[Four Things]] (Proverbs 30:22, 25)  · [[The Sabbatical Year and the Jubilee]] · [[Blessings for Obedience (Leviticus)]] · [[Covenant Warnings and Remembered Promises]] · [[The Seventy Elders and the Quail]] · [[Do Not Forget When You Are Full]] (Deuteronomy 6:10–25) · [[Remember the Wilderness and the Giver of Wealth]] (Deuteronomy 8:1–20) · [[Remember His Deeds and Guard Your Hearts]] · [[Blessings for Obedience (Deuteronomy)]]  · [[Curses for Disobedience]] |
| [[Favor and Grace]] | *Ḥēn* — favor freely given; first in Scripture at 6:8, after the verdict and before any word about the man's character; Exodus 33: Personal favor becomes an appeal for the people; Exodus 34: Personal favor becomes an appeal for presence, pardon, and belonging; favour *"in the sight of God and man"* promised (Proverbs 3:4); Proverbs 3:21–35: Grace is given to the humble, a saying cited in James and 1 Peter; Proverbs 4:1–9: Wisdom’s garland describes gracious adornment and honor.; Proverbs 8:35: favour from Yahweh to whoever finds Wisdom, the phrase of 18:22; Proverbs 12:2 explicitly associates favor with goodness; Proverbs 19: Royal goodwill is sought and pictured as dew; household prudence is a divine gift; Proverbs 22: Loving favor above silver; gracious speech wins the king; Proverbs 28: The person who rebukes another finds more favor afterward than the flatterer; Ecclesiastes 9:1–12: Skill does not secure favor Deuteronomy 9:1–6: The land is given to a stiff-necked people, not for their righteousness (4–6). This supports a thematic account of unearned gift; it is not a claim about which Hebrew grace term occurs or a complete doctrine of individual salvation. Deuteronomy 9:7–29: Survival after the calf supports the denial that possession proves merit (9:4–6). The people endure through heard intercession and the appealed-to prior relationship, not an assertion of innocence (19, 26–29).  Deuteronomy 33: Joseph receives the good will of the one who lived in the bush (16), and Naphtali is satisfied with favor (23). Particular gifts occur within the shared national blessing. | [[The Sons of God and the Corruption of the Earth]] · [[Noah and the Flood]] · [[Abram and Lot Separate]] · [[A Wife for Isaac]] · [[Jacob's Ladder at Bethel]] · [[Jacob's Flocks]] · [[The Reconciliation with Esau]]  · [[Joseph in Potiphar's House]] · [[The Cupbearer and the Baker]] · [[Pharaoh's Dreams and Joseph's Rise]] · [[The Second Journey]] · [[Joseph Reveals Himself]] · [[Joseph and the Famine]] · [[Elihu Speaks in Anger]] · [[The Last Plague Announced]] · [[The Death of the Firstborn and the Departure]] · [[Laws of Holiness and Compassion]] · [[Show Me Your Glory]] · [[The Covenant Renewed]] · [[Trust in Yahweh with All Your Heart]] · [[Do Not Withhold Good]] · [[Get Wisdom]] · [[Wisdom at the Creation]] · [[A False Balance]] (Proverbs 11:1–31) · [[Whoever Loves Correction]] (Proverbs 12:2) · [[Hope Deferred]] (Proverbs 13:15) · [[A Strong Tower]] (Proverbs 18:22) · [[Better Is the Poor Who Walks in Integrity]] (Proverbs 19:6, 12, 14) · [[A Good Name]] (Proverbs 22:1, 11) · [[The Wicked Flee When No One Pursues]] (Proverbs 28:23) · [[Where There Is No Vision]] (Proverbs 29:26) · [[One Event Comes to All]] · [[Yahweh Bless You and Keep You]] · [[Not Because of Your Righteousness]] · [[Remember the Calf and Moses' Intercession]]  · [[Moses Blesses the Tribes]] |
| [[The Fear of God]] | Walking in Yahweh’s ways and fearing him (6) answer the remembered discipline of a father (5). Reverence is expressed in conduct and dependence.  Deuteronomy 10:12–22: Fear frames the call to love, obey, serve, cling to Yahweh, and swear by his name (12, 20).  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: Failure to fear Yahweh’s glorious name accompanies disobedience (58); exile then brings fearful insecurity morning and evening (65–67). Reverence and debilitating terror have distinct roles in the warning. Deuteronomy 31:9–13: The assembled people hear in order to learn, fear Yahweh, and do the law (12). Children also learn to fear him (13). Reverence is teachable through hearing and is directed toward conduct, as in the king’s reading in Deuteronomy 17:19.  Joshua 2: Rahab describes the inhabitants’ loss of courage at news of Yahweh’s deeds (9–11). Her confession and protective action distinguish her response from collective terror; fear of Israel and reverent allegiance should not simply be equated. Joshua 4: Israel is to fear Yahweh forever (24). It also fears Joshua as it feared Moses (14); respect for the commissioned leader and enduring fear of God have distinct objects. | [[Job of Uz]] · [[Bildad's Second Speech]] · [[Abram in Egypt]] ·
