| [[Festivals and First Fruits]]  Joshua 5:10–12: Passover is kept at Gilgal on the fourteenth day at evening, followed by unleavened cakes and parched grain (10–11). Leviticus 23:10–14 regulates eating harvest produce; Joshua does not narrate a first-fruits offering, so neither performance nor neglect is established. | Remembered deliverance and agricultural produce ordered into recurring worship; three appearances, offerings, and distinct later calendar provisions; Aaron proclaims a feast to Yahweh and the people sacrifice and celebrate before the calf (32:5–6). The use of the divine name and familiar festival practices does not authorize the image; Exodus 34: Calendar renewed with protected land during pilgrimage; first fruits of all one's increase as honour to Yahweh (Proverbs 3:9–10); Leviticus 2: Yeast and honey may be offered as first fruits but not burned for a pleasant aroma (2:11–12); parched first-fruits grain has its own burned memorial (14–16). First fruits are not a single uniform altar preparation.; Leviticus 19:19–37: New fruit trees have three years of forbidden fruit, a fourth year holy for praise, and a fifth year for eating (23–25). Leviticus 23:1–8: Passover and Unleavened Bread dated; assemblies and seven days of offerings. Leviticus 23:9–22: Sheaf, fifty-day count, leavened loaves, and gleanings.; Leviticus 23:23–32: Trumpet memorial and atonement dates  Leviticus 25:1–22: Seven Sabbaths of years and a fiftieth year (8–10) extend the calendar’s counting patterns.; the Passover kept at Sinai and given a second date; Numbers 15: the first of the dough; Numbers 18: The best oil, vintage, grain, and first-ripe fruit brought to Yahweh become Aaron’s household provision (12–13). Deuteronomy 31:9–13: The feast of tents in the year of release becomes the occasion for reading the law at the chosen place (10–11). The express summons includes men, women, little ones, and resident foreigners (12). Deuteronomy 16:16’s male pilgrimage requirement is not a prohibition on these others attending. | [[The Passover]] · [[The Consecration of the Firstborn]] · [[Laws of Holiness and Compassion]] · [[Justice, Sabbaths, and Feasts]] · [[The Golden Calf]] · [[The Covenant Renewed]] · [[Trust in Yahweh with All Your Heart]] · [[The Meal Offering]] · [[Holy Living in Field and Community]] · [[Sabbath, Passover, and Unleavened Bread]] · [[First Fruits and the Feast of Weeks]] · [[Trumpets and the Day of Atonement]] · [[The Feast of Booths]] (Leviticus 23:33–44)  · [[The Sabbatical Year and the Jubilee]] · [[The Passover in the Second Year]] · [[The Two Silver Trumpets]] · [[The Twelve Spies]] · [[Offerings in the Land and Sins of Ignorance]] · [[The Duties and Portions of Priests and Levites]] · [[Daily, Sabbath, and New Moon Offerings]] · [[The Offerings of the Appointed Feasts]] · [[Passover, Weeks, and Booths]] · [[First Fruits and the Tithe Declaration]] · [[Read the Law to All Israel]]  · [[Passover at Gilgal and the End of the Manna]] |
| [[Folly]] | Wisdom's opposite, and a stance before it is a deficit — the fool *despises* the wisdom and instruction offered him (Proverbs 1:7); the simple, the fool, the mocker, and the *nābāl*; the fool's portrait turned on Job by Eliphaz (5:2–3) and the friends' own speech called *nəbālâ* by God (42:8); Proverbs 3:21–35: Fools end in shame; the wise inherit glory; Proverbs 6:32: the adulterer void of understanding; Proverbs 8:5: fools invited to *"an understanding heart"*; Proverbs 10:1, 8, 10, 13–14, 18, 21, 23: The foolish son grieves his mother; chatter, slander, refusal of understanding, and pleasure in evil give folly a social cost; Proverbs 19: Folly ruins a way, then blames Yahweh; the foolish son damages his household; Proverbs 20: Drink, quarrels, neglected work, and rash dedication; Proverbs 21: The wanderer, the swallowing fool, and the scoffer; Proverbs 22: The simple pass on; folly bound in a child's heart; Proverbs 23:9: words wasted in a fool's ears; Proverbs 24:7–9: the fool silent in the gate; the mocker detested; Proverbs 24:30: the vineyard of the man void of understanding; Proverbs 26:1–12: the fool's portrait, and the commands to answer and not answer him; Proverbs 28: The companion of gluttons shames his father; the person who trusts in himself is a fool; Proverbs 30:22, 32: the fool filled with food; foolish self-exaltation silenced; Ecclesiastes 2:2–3: laughter called foolishness; folly laid hold of under wisdom's guidance; Ecclesiastes 2:13–16: the fool walks in darkness, yet shares the wise man's end; Ecclesiastes 2:19: successor possibly foolish, never identified; Ecclesiastes 4:5: the fool folds his hands and ruins himself; Ecclesiastes 4:13: the old king who no longer receives admonition; Ecclesiastes 5:1–4: the sacrifice and many words of fools, in whom God has no pleasure; Ecclesiastes 6:1–9: The fool as the measure of wisdom’s questioned advantage; Ecclesiastes 9:1–12: Madness before death; Ecclesiastes 9:13–10:4: a little folly outweighs wisdom; the fool exposes himself; Ecclesiastes 10:5–20: folly set in high office; the fool swallowed by his own lips | [[The Purpose of the Proverbs]] · [[Eliphaz's | [[Forgiveness]] | *Nāśāʾ* — to lift, to carry away. **Genesis withholds the word until its last chapter**: 50:17 is the first time in the Bible one human being asks another for forgiveness, and 50:20 is the granting of it. Forty-nine chapters of fratricide, deception, rape and sale are settled instead by marks, treaties, heaps of stones and silence — and what Joseph finally gives is not a verdict but food. The idiom the brothers use is the one Sinai will reserve for God at Exodus 34:7; an angel who *"will not pardon"*; Moses explicitly asks God to forgive the people after confessing their great sin (32:31–32); Exodus 33: Restored presence is not a blanket cancellation of consequences; Exodus 34: Pardon sought after God declares forgiveness without erasing judgment; Proverbs 10:12, 18: Love’s covering of wrongs offers an image of relational forbearance, not an explicit declaration of divine pardon; Proverbs 19: Overlooking an offense is called glory, alongside continued correction and truthful judgment; Proverbs 28: Mercy follows confession and renunciation of sins; Leviticus 4:1–21: Forgiveness is explicitly the outcome of priestly atonement for the congregation (20). · [[The Sin Offering for Priest and Congregation]]; Leviticus 4:22–35: Explicit promise for ruler, goat offerer, and lamb offerer.  Leviticus 5:1–13: Forgiveness is expressly promised after both the bird and flour procedures (10, 13); the less costly provision does not promise a lesser pardon.  Leviticus 6:1–7: The closing promise of forgiveness (7) applies to the listed wrongs, including fraud and false swearing. Pardon is set beside required restitution, not permission to retain the neighbor’s goods.; Leviticus 19:19–37: Forgiveness is explicitly promised to the man after priestly atonement with the ram (22). Deuteronomy 29: Yahweh refuses pardon to the apostate who presumes peace while persisting in stubbornness (19–20). The warning targets defiant self-assurance; it is not a universal denial that repentant sinners may be forgiven. | [[The Deaths of Jacob and Joseph]] · [[Joseph Reveals Himself]] · [[The Silver Cup and Judah's Plea]] · [[The Reconciliation with Esau]] · [[Cain and Abel]] · [[The Arrows of the Almighty]] · [[Zophar's First Speech]] · [[Elihu Speaks in Anger]] · [[Locusts and Darkness]] · [[The Angel Sent Ahead]] · [[The Golden Calf]] · [[Show Me Your Glory]] · [[The Covenant Renewed]] · [[A Wise Son Makes a Glad Father]] · [[A Dry Morsel with Quietness]] (Proverbs 17:9) · [[Better Is the Poor Who Walks in Integrity]] (Proverbs 19:11) · [[The Wicked Flee When No One Pursues]] (Proverbs 28:13) · [[The Sin Offering for Ruler and Individual]] (Leviticus 4:22–35)  · [[Confession and Offerings According to Means]] · [[The Trespass Offering for Holy Things and Unknown Guilt]] (Leviticus 5:14–19); Forgiveness is expressly promised in both cases (16, 18). The holy-things case joins pardon to restitution and priestly atonement; ignorance alone does not settle the liability.  · [[Restitution for Fraud and False Oaths]] · [[Holy Living in Field and Community]] · [[The People Refuse the Land]] · [[The Defeat at Hormah]] · [[Offerings in the Land and Sins of Ignorance]] · [[Vows of Men and Women]] · [[An Unsolved Killing and the Broken-Necked Heifer]] · [[The Covenant in Moab and the Hidden Things]] |
| [[The Glory of God]] | *Kəbôd YHWH* — God's presence made visible, from the root *kbd*, *heavy*, that Exodus also uses for Pharaoh's heart; first seen in the wilderness of Sin, *"in the cloud"*, by a congregation that had just wished it had died in Egypt, and traced through Sinai to the filled tabernacle; the glory *settles* (*šākan*) on Sinai like devouring fire; announced at 25:8 as God's *dwelling* (*šākan*) among the people; priestly garments *"for glory and for beauty"*; the meeting place *"sanctified by my glory"*; Exodus 33: Glory requested; goodness and bounded sight promised; Exodus 34: The promised passing is narrated through a proclamation of divine character; Exodus 34:29–35: The encounter leaves a visible effect on the mediator; Exodus 39: Garments designed for glory and beauty made; no manifestation reported.; Proverbs 25:2: *"the glory of God to conceal a thing"*, set against the glory of kings, to search; Leviticus 9: inaugural offerings, blessing, and public manifestation; Leviticus 10:1–11: glorified before the people in judgment  Numbers 16:1–40: Glory appears to the congregation gathered by Korah (19), preceding the threat of destruction and the intercessory plea.; Numbers 16:41–50: The cloud covers the Tent and Yahweh’s glory appears as the congregation gathers against  Numbers 20:1–13: Yahweh’s glory appears to Moses and Aaron when they fall on their faces at the Tent (6). | [[The Crossing of the Sea]] · [[Manna and Quail]] · [[Israel at Mount Sinai]] · [[The Blood of the Covenant]] · [[The Offering for the Sanctuary]] · [[The Priestly Garments]] · [[The Ordination of the Priests]] · [[Show Me Your Glory]] · [[The Covenant Renewed]] · [[The Shining Face of Moses]] · [[The Priestly Garments Made]] (Exodus 39:1–31) · [[The Work Completed]] (Exodus 39:32–43) · [[The Tabernacle Raised and the Glory]] (Exodus 40:1–38) · [[The Glory of Kings]] (Proverbs 25:2, 27) · [[The Eighth Day and the Appearing Glory]] · [[Nadab and Abihu and the Strange Fire]] · [[The Cloud over the Tabernacle]] · [[The People Refuse the Land]]  · [[The Rebellion of Korah]] · [[Aaron Stands Between the Dead and the Living]]  · [[The Waters of Meribah]] |
| [[Guilt and Conscience]] | Liability incurred (*ʾāšām*) as against the moment a person finally says so; Hebrew has no word for conscience, so Genesis supplies narrative instead — and 42:21's confession is unprompted, unobserved, and twenty-two years late; Proverbs 20: The heart cannot certify itself; Yahweh searches inwardly; Proverbs 28: Flight without a pursuer pictures the insecurity of wickedness (1); bloodguilt leaves a fugitive without support (17); and denial cannot make parental robbery innocent (24); Proverbs 30: God’s reproof can expose a liar, and slandering a servant can leave the accuser guilty; Leviticus 4:1–21: The congregation is guilty while the matter remains hidden; recognition then calls for its offering (13–14). · [[The Sin Offering for Priest and Congregation]]; Leviticus 4:22–35: Guilt followed by recognition and a prescribed remedy.  Leviticus 5:1–13: Awareness of liability leads to confession of the particular sin (5), not merely an unspecified feeling of guilt.  Leviticus 6:1–7: Guilt leads to returning the goods in full with an added fifth (4–5). The WEB speaks of the day of being found guilty; these verses do not specify whether discovery, confession, or a judicial verdict establishes that point.  Deuteronomy 1:34–46: The confession in verse 41 acknowledges guilt, but does not authorize the people’s chosen remedy. The text judges the ascent without declaring whether their feelings were sincere. | [[The Fall]] · [[Cain and Abel]] · [[Abraham and Abimelech]] · [[Judah and Tamar]] · [[The Brothers' First Journey to Egypt]] · [[The Second Journey]] · [[The Silver Cup and Judah's Plea]] · [[Joseph Reveals Himself]] · [[The Deaths of Jacob and Joseph]] · [[There Is No Umpire Between Us]] · [[Job's Oath of Innocence]] · [[Moses Flees to Midian]] · [[The Plans of the Heart]] (Proverbs 16:2, 25) · [[Wine Is a Mocker]] (Proverbs 20:9, 27) · [[The Wicked Flee When No One Pursues]] (Proverbs 28:1, 13, 17, 24) · [[The Words of Agur]] (Proverbs 30:6, 10, 12) · [[The Sin Offering for Ruler and Individual]] (Leviticus 4:22–35)  · [[Confession and Offerings According to Means]] · [[The Trespass Offering for Holy Things and Unknown Guilt]] (Leviticus 5:14–19); Verses 17–19 explicitly separate liability from knowledge. This is actual wrongdoing described by the law, not a declaration that every anxious suspicion proves an offense.  · [[Restitution for Fraud and False Oaths]] · [[Covenant Warnings and Remembered Promises]] · [[The Unclean Sent Out and Wrongs Repaid]] · [[The Suspected Wife]] · [[The Defeat at Hormah]] · [[Offerings in the Land and Sins of Ignorance]] · [[Reuben, Gad, and the Land Beyond the Jordan]]  · [[The Sentence and the Presumptuous Ascent]] (Deuteronomy 1:34–46) · [[An Unsolved Killing and the Broken-Necked Heifer]] |
| [[Hardening of the Heart]] | The heart made *strong* (*ḥāzaq*), *heavy* (*kābēd*), or *hard* (*qāšâ*) against God's word — Pharaoh's, announced by God at Exodus 4:21 before Moses has met him; narrated first as hardened with no agent, then as self-hardened (8:15), only at 9:12 as hardened by Yahweh, and from chapter 10 by Yahweh alone; the order of agents recorded with three readings, none adopted; Proverbs 21: A turned royal heart and a hardened wicked face; Proverbs 28: The man who hardens his heart falls into trouble, contrasted with the man who always fears  Deuteronomy 2:24–37: Verse 30 explicitly attributes Sihon’s hardened spirit and obstinate heart to Yahweh, with delivery into Israel’s hand as the purpose. The refusal is also Sihon’s act. The WEB supports this combination of divine agency and human refusal; no claim about exact Hebrew verbs is needed.  Deuteronomy 10:12–22: Israel is commanded to circumcise its heart and cease being stiff-necked (16). Deuteronomy 29: Witnessed signs coexist with the absence of a divinely given knowing heart (2–4). The warning also holds the stubborn apostate responsible (18–21). The tension between divine gift and human responsibility is stated, not resolved by equating verse 4 with a deliberate hardening act. | [[Moses Returns to Egypt]] · [[Bricks Without Straw]] · [[Aaron's Staff Before Pharaoh]] · [[Water Turned to Blood]] · [[Frogs, Lice, and Flies]] · [[Livestock, Boils, and Hail]] · [[Locusts and Darkness]] · [[The Last Plague Announced]] · [[The Consecration of the Firstborn]] · [[The Crossing of the Sea]] · [[Manna and Quail]] · [[Water from the Rock]] · [[Wisdom Calls in the Street]] · [[The King's Heart]] (Proverbs 21:1, 29) · [[The Wicked Flee When No One Pursues]] (Proverbs 28:14) · [[Where There Is No Vision]] (Proverbs 29:1)  · [[The Defeat of Sihon Recounted]]  · [[What Yahweh Requires of Israel]] · [[The Year of Release and the Open Hand]] · [[The Covenant in Moab and the Hidden Things]] · [[Return Restoration and the Circumcised Heart]] · [[The Whole Land Taken]] |
| [[Healing]] | God restoring bodies, households, and water — *rāpāʾ*, first when God heals Abimelech's house after Abraham prays (Genesis 20:17), used once more in Genesis of the Egyptian physicians who embalm Jacob, and made a name at Marah: *"I am Yahweh who heals you"*, on condition of listening; the assailant owes lost time and full provision for healing; sickness taken away from Israel's midst; *rip̄ʾûṯ*, health from the fear of Yahweh (Proverbs 3:8); Proverbs 4:22: the father's words as *marpēʾ*, health to the whole body; Proverbs 12:18, 25 includes healing and encouraging human speech; Ecclesiastes 3: Healing paired with killing  Numbers 21:4–9: The bitten person who looks lives (9). Bodily rescue follows intercession and a commanded sign, rather than a described medical procedure; the narrator does not explain the physical mechanism. Deuteronomy 7:12–26: Sickness removed and the diseases of Egypt laid on Israel's enemies instead (15).  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: The repeated inability to be healed intensifies the threatened disease (27, 35, 59–61). It is not a medical classification or a warrant for diagnosing sufferers as covenant-breakers. | [[Abraham and Abimelech]] · [[The Deaths of Jacob and Joseph]] · [[Bitter Water at Marah]] · [[Laws of Violence and Injury]] · [[The Angel Sent Ahead]] · [[Trust in Yahweh with All Your Heart]] · [[Keep Your Heart]] · [[Whoever Loves Correction]] (Proverbs 12:18, 25) · [[Hope Deferred]] (Proverbs 13:17) · [[A Gentle Answer]] (Proverbs 15:4, 30) · [[The Plans of the Heart]] (Proverbs 16:24) · [[A Dry Morsel with Quietness]] (Proverbs 17:22) · [[A Strong Tower]] (Proverbs 18:14) · [[A Time for Every Purpose]] (Ecclesiastes 3:1–15) · [[Miriam and Aaron Against Moses]]  · [[The Brass Serpent]] · [[Blessing and the Removal of the Nations]]  · [[Curses for Disobedience]] · [[The Second Circumcision at Gilgal]] — Joshua 5:1–9: The circumcised people remain in their places in camp until healed (8). No duration, healer, or miraculous mechanism is stated. |
| [[Holiness]] | What is set apart for God and so unavailable for common use — first a day (Genesis 2:3), then a patch of desert ground (Exodus 3:5, the Bible's first *qōdeš*); dangerous before it is good, introduced with a boundary (*"Don't come close"*), and later made a demand on a people (*"You shall be holy; for I … am holy"*); at Sinai, the holy day orders ordinary household time; a tool profanes the altar; three zones of approach on one mountain; The veil distinguishes the holy place from the most holy (26:33); The court and veil distinguish spaces; the lamp is tended outside the veil; holy garments and the plate *"HOLY TO YAHWEH"*; the altar *"most holy"*, making holy whatever touches it; Yahweh sanctifies the people who keep his holy Sabbath (31:13–15); Exodus 33: Dangerous nearness, protected approach, and a distinct people; Exodus 34: The renewed ascent excludes companions and grazing animals; Exodus 34:29–35: Awe and distinction coexist with an invitation to approach.; Exodus 35: A holy day and holy garments belong to one commanded project; Ordinary technical skills and donated materials serve the sanctuary; Exodus 36:8–38: the inner veil and entrance screen preserve distinct boundaries; Exodus 37: Holy oil and pure incense made; their preparation is not an anointing rite.; Exodus 38: Court and basin prepare bounded, washed approach without an ordination rite; Exodus 39: Holy garments and the inscribed holy crown are made.; Proverbs 30: Agur acknowledges his lack of knowledge of the Holy One and fears dishonoring God’s name through theft  Leviticus 1: Approach is ordered through the tent entrance, the altar, prescribed victims, and priestly duties.; Leviticus 2: The remainder assigned to Aaron and his sons is most holy (2:3, 10). Holiness therefore extends to the portion retained for the priests, not only to what burns on the altar.; Leviticus 4:1–21: The remedy for sin observes distinctions within sacred space: tent entrance, before the veil, inner altar, and outer altar (4–7). · [[The Sin Offering for Priest and Congregation]]; Leviticus 4:22–35: Unblemished victims and prescribed approach.  Leviticus 6:8–23: Maintained fire, restricted holy eating, and wholly burned priestly grain. Leviticus 6:24–7:10: Most holy flesh can be eaten by authorized priests. Contact with it, blood on garments, and cooking vessels receive distinct handling rules (6:27–28). Holy status does not mean moral improvement through touch or universal permission to eat.; Leviticus 7:11–21: Peace-offering eating governed by time, purity, and sacred ownership  Leviticus 7:22–38: What is brought to Yahweh can become the priestly family’s food by his allocation (34). Leviticus 8: Sanctuary, equipment, people, and garments sanctified.; Leviticus 10:1–11: holy approach and sacred/common distinction; Leviticus 10:12–20: holy place for most holy food, clean place for lesser portions; Leviticus 11: divine holiness and deliverance govern ordinary eating; Leviticus 12: The mother must neither touch a holy thing nor enter the sanctuary until the days of. ; Leviticus 13:47–59: garment inspection and handling; Leviticus 14:1–32: restoration after healing ; Leviticus 14:33–57: house inspection, repair, and cleansing  Leviticus 15: household purity protects the sanctuary boundary;; Leviticus 17: Sacrificial allegiance and ordinary meals fall under the sanctuary’s ordering: field sacrifices must become offerings to Yahweh, blood is withheld from consumption, and carcass eating requires cleansing (3–16).; Leviticus 18: Yahweh’s self-identification frames household boundaries and rejection of neighboring customs (2–5, 30). Leviticus 19:1–18: The command to all Israel rests on Yahweh’s own holiness (2). Parent-respect, Sabbath, worship, harvest sharing, truthful dealing, and love belong within one address. Holiness here includes social conduct as well as sacred boundaries.; Leviticus 19:19–37: Distinct kinds in breeding, sowing, and clothing (19), holy fourth-year fruit (23–25), and reverence for the sanctuary (30) stand alongside love for the foreigner and honest measures (33–36).; Leviticus 20: The commands to sanctify themselves and keep the statutes stand beside Yahweh’s declaration that he sanctifies them (7–8).; Leviticus 21: Yahweh’s holiness and sanctifying action ground priestly conduct and sanctuary boundaries (6–8, 15, 23). Leviticus 23:1–8: Holiness expressed in appointed communal assemblies. Leviticus 23:9–22: Leavened first-fruits bread is holy; altar burning is distinct.; Leviticus 23:23–32: Holy convocations  Leviticus 24:1–9: The bread is most holy and must be eaten in a holy place by Aaron and his sons (9). Being holy does not mean being wholly burned or never eaten; holiness determines its recipients and the place of its use.; Leviticus 24:10–23: The name profaned in the camp receives judicial protection (11–16).  Leviticus 25:1–22: Israel must make the fiftieth year holy (10, 12).; Numbers 1: the Levite cordon and the stranger who comes near; Numbers 6: a lay person holy to Yahweh by vow; Numbers 15:32–36: holy time enforced without a stated cleansing rite; Numbers 15:37–41: All Israel is addressed about fringes across generations (38); remembering and doing the commandments serves holiness to their God (40). The calling is not restricted to priests.  Numbers 16:1–40: The challengers appeal to the holiness of the whole congregation (3), but Moses distinguishes that claim from Yahweh’s choice of who may approach (5–10).  Numbers 17: Israel fears that everyone who approaches the tabernacle dies (12–13).; Numbers 18: Holy gifts have differentiated uses: most holy portions belong to priestly males, other portions to the clean household, and Levite provisions may be eaten anywhere after the best tenth is removed (9–13, 29–32).  Numbers 19: Unpurified corpse contamination defiles the sanctuary.  Numbers 20:1–13: The leaders fail to sanctify Yahweh before Israel (12), yet the narrator concludes that he was sanctified in the waters (13). Numbers 35: The closing prohibition of land defilement rests on Yahweh’s presence (33–34). The anointing of the high priest is specified (25), but no new anointing rite is performed. Deuteronomy 5: The Sabbath must be kept holy (12). Deuteronomy 7:1–11: The whole people is called holy and Yahweh’s own possession (6). Deuteronomy 7:12–26: An abomination brought into the house makes its owner *a devoted thing like it* (26). Deuteronomy 26:16–19: Given holy status is also presented as the goal of the promise. Deuteronomy 28: conditional blessing in the land.  Deuteronomy 32:48–52: The exclusion is explained as failure to uphold Yahweh’s holiness among Israel (51), recalling Numbers 20:12. Leadership and past service do not exempt Moses from responsibility to represent Yahweh before the people.  Joshua 3: Joshua commands the people to sanctify themselves before the promised wonders (5). The text specifies no washing or other ritual here. The distance from the ark is expressly explained by the need to see the unfamiliar route (4); a further ritual explanation should not replace that reason.  Joshua 5:13–15: The commander tells Joshua to remove his shoes because the place is holy (15), closely recalling Exodus 3:5.  Joshua 6: Silver, gold, brass, and iron vessels are holy to Yahweh and enter his treasury (19, 24), while the city is burned. Devotion thus does not mean that every object is physically destroyed; it excludes private appropriation.| [[The Burning Bush]] · [[The Passover]] · [[The Consecration of the Firstborn]] · [[The Song of the Sea]] · [[Manna and Quail]] · [[Israel at Mount Sinai]] · [[The Ten Commandments]] · [[The Altar and the Hebrew Slave]] · [[Laws of Holiness and Compassion]] · [[The Blood of the Covenant]] · [[The Offering for the Sanctuary]] · [[The Ark, the Table, and the Lampstand]] · [[The Tabernacle Curtains and Frames]] · [[The Altar and the Court]] · [[The Priestly Garments]] · [[The Ordination of the Priests]] · [[The Incense Altar, the Ransom, and the Holy Oil]] · [[Bezalel, Oholiab, and the Sabbath Sign]] · [[Show Me Your Glory]] · [[The Covenant Renewed]] · [[The Shining Face of Moses]] · [[The Freewill Offering]] · [[Bezalel's Workmen and the Surplus]] · [[The Tabernacle Built]] · [[The Furnishings Made]] (Exodus 37:1–29) · [[The Altar, the Basin, the Court, and the Inventory]] (Exodus 38:1–31) · [[The Priestly Garments Made]] (Exodus 39:1–31) · [[The Work Completed]] (Exodus 39:32–43) · [[The Tabernacle Raised and the Glory]] (Exodus 40:1–38) · [[The Two Feasts]] · [[The Words of Agur]] (Proverbs 30:3, 9) · [[The Wicked Buried and the Work Beyond Finding]] — the wicked and holiness in the WEB  · [[The Burnt Offering]] · [[The Meal Offering]] · [[The Sin Offering for Ruler and Individual]] (Leviticus 4:22–35) · [[The Trespass Offering for Holy Things and Unknown Guilt]] (Leviticus 5:14–19); Wrong against Yahweh’s holy things requires repayment plus a fifth and a ram (15–16). | [[Hope]] | *Tiqvâ*, a cord thrown out and held — asked after rather than possessed at Job 17:15, plucked up like a tree at 19:10, and fifteen verses later replaced by *I know*; Proverbs 10:24, 28: The righteous anticipate joy and receive their desire, whereas the wicked meet feared disaster and lose their hope; Proverbs 13:12: hope deferred makes the heart sick, and fulfilled longing is a tree of life; Proverbs 19: Hope motivates correction directed toward the son’s life; Proverbs 23:18: a future, and a hope not cut off; Proverbs 24:14: wisdom found, and a hope not cut off; Proverbs 31:21, 25: Preparedness accompanies confidence toward the future; Ecclesiastes 9:1–12: Hope belongs to the living Deuteronomy 4:15–31: from exile Israel will seek and find, and return in the latter days (29–31). | [[Miserable Comforters and the Witness in Heaven]] · [[Bildad's Second Speech]] · [[I Know That My Redeemer Lives]] · [[The Cupbearer and the Baker]] · [[Pharaoh's Dreams and Joseph's Rise]] · [[Joseph Reveals Himself]] · [[Jacob's Family Settles in Egypt]] · [[Joseph and the Famine]] · [[Jacob Blesses His Sons]] · [[The Deaths of Jacob and Joseph]] · [[Let the Day Perish]] · [[Eliphaz's First Speech]] · [[The Arrows of the Almighty]] · [[Zophar's First Speech]] · [[Though He Slay Me]] · [[I Will Not Put Away My Integrity]] · [[The Months of Old]] · [[Now They Mock Me]] · [[The Restoration of Job]] · [[A Wise Son Makes a Glad Father]] · [[A False Balance]] (Proverbs 11:1–31) · [[Hope Deferred]] (Proverbs 13:12, 19) · [[Better Is the Poor Who Walks in Integrity]] (Proverbs 19:18) · [[Do Not Envy Sinners]] · [[Through Wisdom a House Is Built]] · [[The Worthy Woman]] · [[One Event Comes to All]] · [[Covenant Warnings and Remembered Promises]] · [[No Form Seen and No Image Made]] · [[Return Restoration and the Circumcised Heart]] |
| [[Hospitality]] | Receiving a stranger who has no claim on you, and the Bible's insistence that it is an obligation with judgment attached; the founding scene offers a morsel of bread and serves a banquet, and the guest turns out to be Yahweh; a covenant meal eaten in God's presence; Proverbs 23:1–3, 6–8: the ruler's deceitful food and the stingy host  Joshua 2: Rahab receives endangered strangers, conceals them, and enables their escape (1–16). Shelter becomes allegiance against her own king’s demand. The narrative identifies her as a prostitute but does not report a sexual encounter with the spies. | [[The Three Visitors and Abraham's Intercession]] · [[The Rescue of Lot and Melchizedek]] · [[Abram in Egypt]] · [[Hagar and Ishmael]] · [[Nahor's Line and the Burial of Sarah]] · [[A Wife for Isaac]] · [[Jacob, Leah, and Rachel]] · [[The Second Journey]] · [[Seven Days of Silence]] · [[Eliphaz's Third Speech]] · [[Job's Oath of Innocence]] · [[Moses Flees to Midian]] · [[Jethro's Counsel]] · [[The Blood of the Covenant]] · [[The Two Feasts]] · [[Thirty Excellent Things]] (Proverbs 23:1–3, 6–8) · [[The March from Sinai]] · [[The Assembly and the Holy Camp]]  · [[Rahab and the Spies]] |
| [[Idolatry]] | Forgetting moves into following, serving, and worshiping other gods (19). Economic self-sufficiency and rival worship are joined in the warning, though wealth itself is not called an idol. Deuteronomy 9:7–29: The molten calf is a rapid departure from the commanded way (12, 16). Moses burns, crushes, and grinds it, then throws its dust into the mountain brook (21); Deuteronomy does not report the drinking narrated in Exodus 32:20. Deuteronomy 11:1–17: serving other gods shuts the sky (16–17). Deuteronomy 11:18–32: the curse is following *"other gods, which you have not known"* (28). Deuteronomy 12:1–28: The nations' shrines destroyed; *"You shall not do so to Yahweh"* (2–4).  Deuteronomy 27:11–26: The first curse targets an engraved or molten image made by a craftsman and installed in secret (15). Concealment does not remove accountability to Yahweh. Deuteronomy 28: conditional blessing in the land.  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: Exile brings service to gods of wood and stone (36, 64), the very allegiance the covenant forbids. The threatened outcome is not permission to worship them. Deuteronomy 29: A hidden turning of any individual, household, or tribe toward other gods becomes a poisonous root (16–18). Future observers explain national ruin by abandoned covenant and worship of other gods (25–28).  Deuteronomy 31:14–23: Future worship of other gods is described as prostitution, abandonment, and covenant breach (16, 20). The metaphor concerns Israel's religious allegiance; these verses do not narrate a particular sexual act.  Deuteronomy 32: Israel’s sacrifices go to demons rather than God (17); its new gods provoke jealousy (16–21). The taunt in 37–38 asks those gods to rescue their worshippers, while 39 denies any god alongside Yahweh. The poem’s language about gods must be read with its own denial of their saving power. | [[Jacob's Flight from Laban]] · [[Jacob's Flocks]] · [[From Shem to Abram]] · [[The Call of Abram]] · [[Return | [[Image of God]] | Humanity's defining status — male and female — and the ground of its dominion; transmitted from father to son at 5:3, and the reason murder is capital at 9:6; Ecclesiastes 3:16–22: Human distinction set beside the denial of advantage in death Deuteronomy 4:15–31: the first forbidden likeness is *male or female* (16), the image Genesis 1:27 says God made. | [[The Creation of the World]] · [[The Garden of Eden]] · [[From Adam to Noah]] · [[The Covenant with Noah]] · [[From Dust to Dust]] (Ecclesiastes 3:16–22) · [[Righteousness and Wisdom Under Question]] (Ecclesiastes 7:29) · [[No Form Seen and No Image Made]] |
| [[Integrity]] | Being all of one piece — the *t-m-m* root, whose plain sense is *whole, complete, unblemished*; not sinlessness but the absence of a seam, and in Job a thing one *holds onto*, announced by God at 2:3 and demanded back by Job's wife six verses later; against the crowd and the bribe; Exodus 38: Accountable material use; no allegation of misuse or independent audit.; Proverbs 2:7, 21 promises Job's pair, *tām* and *yāšār*, a shield and the land; Proverbs 10:9, 29: Blameless walking brings security, while a twisted course is exposed (9); Yahweh’s way is a stronghold to the upright (29); Proverbs 19: Poverty with integrity outranks dishonest speech; wealth is no moral verdict; Proverbs 20: Character known by faithful conduct, without a boast of sinlessness; Proverbs 28: Verse 6 explicitly ranks a poor person with integrity above a rich person with perverse ways; it resembles 19:1 but replaces crooked lips and folly with crooked ways and wealth Leviticus 19:1–18: Truthfulness, fair wages, and impartial judgment (11–15) make the holiness command concrete in dealings with neighbors. Worship and reliable conduct are required together.; Leviticus 19:19–37: Just balances, weights, ephah, and hin require reliable dealings (35–36).  Deuteronomy 27:11–26: Secret idolatry and secret killing are explicitly condemned (15, 24). The public response therefore acknowledges obligations even where an offender might evade ordinary observation. | [[The Second Test]] · [[Job of Uz]] · [[The Wager in Heaven and the Four Messengers]] · [[Noah and the Flood]] · [[Circumcision, the Sign of the Covenant]] · [[Abraham and Abimelech]] · [[Jacob and Esau and the Sold Birthright]] · [[Seven Days of Silence]] · [[Eliphaz's First Speech]] · [[The Arrows of the Almighty]] · [[There Is No Umpire Between Us]] · [[Though He Slay Me]] · [[Why Do the Wicked Live]] · [[Eliphaz's Third Speech]] · [[Oh That I Knew Where I Might Find Him]] · [[I Will Not Put Away My Integrity]] · [[Job's Oath of Innocence]] · [[I Repent in Dust and Ashes]] · [[The Restoration of Job]] · [[The Passover]] · [[Jethro's Counsel]] · [[Laws of Property and Restitution]] · [[Justice, Sabbaths, and Feasts]] · [[The Altar, the Basin, the Court, and the Inventory]] (Exodus 38:1–31) · [[Do Not Consent to Sinners]] · [[If You Seek Her as Silver]] · [[A Wise Son Makes a Glad Father]] · [[A False Balance]] (Proverbs 11:1–31) · [[Whoever Loves Correction]] (Proverbs 12:5, 17, 19, 22) · [[Hope Deferred]] (Proverbs 13:6) · [[A Dry Morsel with Quietness]] (Proverbs 17:26) · [[Better Is the Poor Who Walks in Integrity]] (Proverbs 19:1, 22) · [[Wine Is a Mocker]] (Proverbs 20:6–7, 9, 11) · [[The Wicked Flee When No One Pursues]] (Proverbs 28:6, 18) · [[Where There Is No Vision]] (Proverbs 29:10)  · [[Be Holy and Love Your Neighbor]] · [[Holy Living in Field and Community]] · [[A Prophet Like Moses]] · [[Justice, Family Duty, Honest Weights, and Amalek]]  · [[The People Answer Amen to the Curses]] |
| [[Joy and Enjoyment]] | Mirth, pleasure, and the enjoyment of one's portion; Proverbs 14:13; 20:1; 21:17 warn of pleasure's costs; Ecclesiastes 2:1–11: joy real and a portion, yet no profit; Ecclesiastes 2:24–26: enjoyment from God amid uncertain succession; Ecclesiastes 3: Rejoicing and enjoyment received as God’s gift; Ecclesiastes 3:16–22: Present enjoyment commended within mortality and uncertainty; Ecclesiastes 4:1–3: Oppression qualifies any universal application of the enjoyment sayings; Ecclesiastes 4:8: the solitary laborer deprives his soul of enjoyment; Ecclesiastes 5:8–20: Enjoyment and the power to receive it are gifts of God; Ecclesiastes 6:1–9: Good unexperienced despite wealth, children, and years; Ecclesiastes 6:10–12: Who knows what is good beside earlier affirmations of enjoyment; Ecclesiastes 9:1–12: Bread, wine, clothing, marriage, and labor; Ecclesiastes 10:16–19: feasting in due season, not for drunkenness; wine makes life glad; Ecclesiastes 11:7–12:8: Joy in youth beside judgment and the coming loss of pleasure.; Song of Solomon 1:1–4: love above wine and shared rejoicing; Song of Solomon 1:9–2:7: fragrance, fruit, and great delight in the beloved's shadow; Song 3:11: *"the day of the gladness of his heart"*; honey, wine, and milk, and *"Eat, friends! Drink"* (Song 5:1); Song 6:11–7:9: Love for delights ; Song 7:10–8:4: stored fruit, spiced wine, and an imagined embrace Deuteronomy 12:1–28: The household, servants, and Levite rejoice before Yahweh (7, 12, 18).  Deuteronomy 27: Eating peace offerings and rejoicing before Yahweh are commanded at Ebal (7).  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: Verses 47–48 contrast joyful service in abundance with enemy service in deprivation. They address covenant ingratitude, not a general accusation against those who experience grief or cannot feel cheerful.  Deuteronomy 33: Zebulun and Issachar are summoned to rejoice (18), and Israel is called happy as a people saved by Yahweh (29). Enjoyment is tied to belonging and provision. | [[The Trial of Pleasure]] · [[The Labor Left to Another]] (Ecclesiastes 2:18–26) · [[A Time for Every Purpose]] (Ecclesiastes 3:1–15) · [[From Dust to Dust]] (Ecclesiastes 3:16–22) · [[The Tears of the Oppressed]] (Ecclesiastes 4:1–3) · [[Two Are Better Than One]] (Ecclesiastes 4:4–12) · [[The Lover of Silver and the Gift of Enjoyment]] (Ecclesiastes 5:8–20) · [[Riches Without Enjoyment]] (Ecclesiastes 6:1–9) · [[Who Knows What Is Good]] (Ecclesiastes 6:10–12) · [[The House of Mourning]] (Ecclesiastes 7:3, 14) · [[The Wicked Buried and the Work Beyond Finding]] — mirth beside injustice · [[One Event Comes to All]] · [[Folly in High Places and Everyday Work]] · [[Remember Your Creator]] · [[Love Better Than Wine]] · [[Delight and the Unawakened Love]] · [[Solomon's Wedding Procession]] · [[A Garden Locked and Opened]] · [[Return, Return, Shulammite]] (Song 6:11–7:9)  · [[Come into the Fields, My Beloved]] (Song of Solomon 7:10–8:4) · [[Make Haste, My Beloved]] (Song 8:13–14; closing invitation) · [[The Feast of Booths]] (Leviticus 23:33–44) · [[The Two Silver Trumpets]] · [[The Place Yahweh Will Choose]] · [[Tithes for Feasting and Provision]] · [[Passover, Weeks, and Booths]] · [[Divorce, Remarriage, and the Newlywed]] · [[First Fruits and the Tithe Declaration]]  · [[Stones and an Altar on Mount Ebal]]  · [[Curses for Disobedience]] · [[Return Restoration and the Circumcised Heart]]  · [[Moses Blesses the Tribes]] |
| [[Judgment]] | Israel is warned that it can perish like the nations displaced before it (19–20). Election and rescue do not grant immunity from the consequences of refusing Yahweh’s voice. Deuteronomy 9:1–6: The nations’ wickedness is the stated reason for their expulsion (4–5), but the beneficiaries are themselves called stiff-necked (6). Victory does not certify the conqueror’s righteousness. Deuteronomy 9:7–29: Destruction and erasure of the people’s name are threatened (14); Aaron too faces destruction (20). Yahweh’s listening to Moses (19) averts total destruction without making the rebellion innocent.  Deuteronomy 10:12–22: Yahweh’s justice accepts neither personal favoritism nor reward (17) and defends the fatherless and widow (18).  Deuteronomy 27:11–26: Withheld justice and payment to kill an innocent person are cursed (19, 25). The ceremony condemns these acts but does not replace the evidentiary procedures of Deuteronomy 19:15–21.  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: Disease, dispossession, siege, and scattering are presented as divine sanctions (20–68). Their terrible effects on children are explicit; describing judgment must not conceal the cost or turn the warning into permission for cruelty. Deuteronomy 29: An individual apostate’s erased name and a land made barren stand together (20–28). Sodom’s overthrow becomes the explicit analogy for Israel’s possible fate, explained to future generations as covenant breach.  Deuteronomy 31:14–23: The evils and troubles follow Israel's abandonment of Yahweh (16–18). The song will testify when those troubles come (21), supplying their covenant explanation in advance rather than making suffering itself sufficient proof of any individual's guilt.  Deuteronomy 32: The faithful God’s judgment reaches every age group (24–25); the violence is explicit. Threatened erasure is restrained because enemies would misconstrue their victory (26–27). Compassion at powerlessness (36) does not erase the threatened judgment; vengeance against adversaries follows (41–43).  Deuteronomy 32:48–52: Moses must see but not enter the land because of the named offense at Meribah (51–52). This is an explicit divine verdict on a specified act, not permission to infer comparable guilt from every human death.  Joshua 6: Jericho’s destruction includes young and old (21), without individual trials or a catalogue of each victim’s acts. The threat also reaches Israel’s camp if it takes what is devoted (18). Election does not grant immunity from the chapter’s sanctions. Joshua 20: The fugitive presents his case to the refuge city’s elders at its gate (4); the congregation’s trial remains ahead (6, 9). | [[The Fall]] · [[Cain and Abel]] · [[The Line of Cain and the The prediction supplies a covenant explanation, not a license to infer anyone’s guilt from suffering alone.  · [[The Song of Moses]]  · [[Moses Commanded to Ascend Nebo]] · [[The Second Circumcision at Gilgal]] — Joshua 5:1–9: The forty-year loss of the military generation is attributed to not listening to Yahweh (6). Numbers 14:29–30 and 26:64–65 specify the condemned cohort and the exceptions, Joshua and Caleb; Joshua’s summary should not erase those qualifications.  · [[The Fall of Jericho]] · [[The Capture of Ai]]  · [[The Cities of Refuge Appointed]] |
| [[Kingship and Empire]] | Human beings ruling other human beings at scale; *mamlākâ* enters Scripture at Genesis 10:10, attached to Babel and, on the same reading, to Nineveh; Proverbs 8:15–16: *"By me kings reign"*; Proverbs 19: Royal favor attracts petitioners; wrath and goodwill are pictured as lion and dew; Proverbs 20: Royal terror, discerning judgment, threshing, and sustaining love; Proverbs 21: The ruler's heart as directed water, without exemption from responsibility; Proverbs 22: Purity of heart and gracious speech make the king's friend; Proverbs 22:29; 23:1–3: serving kings and restraint at the ruler's table; Proverbs 24:21–22: the king feared with Yahweh; Proverbs 28: Rebellion multiplies rulers while understanding sustains order; Proverbs 30:22, 27–28, 31: servant king, kingless locusts, lizard in palaces, stately king; Proverbs 31:3–9: Royal power is constrained by sobriety, instruction, and the rights of the afflicted; Ecclesiastes 1:1–11: A king’s heading introduces limits shared by all human generations; Ecclesiastes 2:7–9: a king's treasure makes a comprehensive trial of pleasure possible; Ecclesiastes 2:12: the king's successor can only do what has been done; Ecclesiastes 4:13–16: a youth from prison to the throne, and a reign outlived by later generations; Ecclesiastes 5:8–20: Officials above officials and a king profiting from the field; Ecclesiastes 8:1–9: Royal commands, mortal limits, and harmful dominion; Ecclesiastes 9:13–10:4: the besieging king, the ruler among fools, and the angered ruler; Ecclesiastes 10:5–20: the ruler's error, the child king and the son of nobles, and the king not cursed; Song of Solomon 1:1–4: Solomon’s heading and the king’s rooms; Song of Solomon 1:9–2:7: Pharaoh's chariots and the king at his table as love imagery; Song 3:6–11: Solomon's carriage, armed escort, and wedding crown; Song 6:4–10: Queens and concubines as a scale of praise; Song 6:11–7:9: A king captive in her hair; Song 8:8–12: family plans, the woman’s answer, and her own vineyard; Leviticus 4:22–35: Ruler accountable to Yahweh; no specific monarch identified. ; Numbers 22:1–21: Balak responds to fear with diplomacy, rewards, and promised honor (2–17).  Numbers 22:36–23:26: Balak can supply honor, victims, and new vantage points, but cannot command the divine answer.  Numbers 23:27–24:25: Israel’s king is exalted above Agag (24:7); the star and scepter portray future dominion (24:17–19). The final sayings also threaten the powers that subdue others (24:22–24). No particular historical ruler is identified here. Deuteronomy 28:15–68: The king whom Israel will appoint shares the people’s deportation (36). Royal office offers no immunity from covenant judgment; neither the king nor the invading empire is named.  Deuteronomy 33: Verse 5 speaks of a king in Jeshurun when the tribes gather. The English pronoun alone does not settle whether Moses or God is intended; a confident identification requires further argument. Joshua 10: Adoni-Zedek draws four other city kings into a coalition against Gibeon (3–5). Gibeon is compared with royal cities, but no king of Gibeon is named (2). The defeated rulers’ capture and execution belong to the next unit.  Joshua 10:28–43: Horam’s attempt to help Lachish fails (33); the kings of Makkedah, Libnah, Hebron, and Debir fall with their cities. The unnamed king of Hebron in 37 creates a tension with Hoham’s prior death, not a securely identified succession. Joshua 12:1–24: Two named kings with realms, thirty-one unnamed and counted (1–24). | [[The Table of Nations]] · [[The Tower of Babel]] · [[The Rescue of Lot and Melchizedek]] · [[Circumcision, the Sign of the Covenant]] · [[The Treaty at Beersheba]] · [[Return to Bethel; | [[Land and Sojourning]] | Joshua 5:10–12: Israel now eats Canaan’s produce (11–12). The land promise becomes a source of food before conquest and allotment are complete; entry and completed possession remain distinct. The promised land is described by water, crops, iron, and copper (7–9). Its abundance calls for blessing the giver (10); settlement is not an exemption from covenant obligations. Deuteronomy 9:1–6: Possession fulfills the ancestral oath and follows judgment on the nations (5). The repeated denial of Israel’s merit prevents reading the land as proof of the recipients’ moral superiority.  Deuteronomy 10:1–11: Israel is to possess the sworn land (11), but Levi has no tribal portion: Yahweh is its inheritance (9). Numbers 18:21–24 supplies tithes for service, so lack of a territorial share does not mean lack of support.  Deuteronomy 10:12–22: Remembered foreignness in Egypt grounds Israel’s obligation to love the foreigner (19). Deuteronomy 11:1–17: the land of hills and valleys that drinks rain, unlike Egypt. Deuteronomy 11:18–32: every place the sole of the foot treads, within the maximal border (24). Deuteronomy 12:1–28: Not yet *"the rest and … the inheritance"*; distance in the land permits home slaughter (9, 20–21).  Deuteronomy 27: The promised fertile land is Yahweh's gift (2–3). Deuteronomy 28: conditional blessing in the land.  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: The land remains Yahweh’s gift even when its inhabitants are uprooted (52, 63). Scattering brings no secure resting place (64–65); possession does not make covenant accountability disappear. Deuteronomy 29: Resident foreigners stand within the covenant assembly (11), while a visiting foreigner later witnesses the ruined land (22). These are distinct roles. Possession can give way to uprooting into another land (28).  Deuteronomy 31: The ancestral land oath outlasts Moses’ leadership: Joshua will go with the people and cause them to inherit (7). The gift remains ahead of the assembly, with the Jordan still uncrossed. Deuteronomy 31:9–13: Resident foreigners join the men, women, and children who hear and learn the law (12). Life in the promised land depends on transmitting its obligations, not on treating settlement as the end of instruction (13).  Deuteronomy 31:14–23: Entry into the sworn land is assured to Joshua (23), while the people's coming betrayal in that same fertile land is foretold (16, 20). Receiving the inheritance and maintaining covenant loyalty are distinguished.  Deuteronomy 32:48–52: The land remains Yahweh’s gift to Israel (49, 52), yet Moses is denied entry. The people’s inheritance and their leader’s personal participation are distinguished rather than treated as identical promises.  Deuteronomy 33: Land receives dew and fertility; Gad’s portion and Naphtali’s possession sit inside Israel’s promised safety (13–16, 21, 23, 28). The poem is not a full boundary survey.  Joshua 2: Rahab acknowledges that Yahweh has given Israel the land (9), and the spies repeat that certainty to Joshua (24). A resident’s report of fear becomes encouragement to the people who are about to enter.  Joshua 3: The river crossing makes entry possible while the expulsion of the seven peoples remains a promise (10, 17). Crossing the boundary must not be equated with completing possession. Joshua 4: Entry has a dated camp at Gilgal and a memorial (19–20), while conquest and the other tribes’ rest remain unfinished.  Joshua 6: A shut city becomes a conquered city (1–20), but it is burned rather than immediately occupied as an ordinary settlement (24–26). The gift of land does not here authorize unrestricted use of its wealth or rebuilding.  Joshua 8:30–35: altar and inclusive public reading. · [[The Gibeonite Deception]] Joshua 9: The distinction between distant foreigners and neighbors living within the land determines the treaty question (6–7, 16). Gibeon’s survival means that receiving the land is not narrated as the immediate removal of every prior community.  Joshua 10:28–43: The campaign claims the southern kings and land (40–42), yet Joshua 13:1–3 says much land remains and 15:13–17 reports further action at Hebron and Debir. Military victory and enduring settlement are not simply interchangeable; the narrative tension remains. Joshua 12:1–24: East and west given in parallel formulas (6–8). Joshua 13: Inheritance is allocated before possession is complete: Yahweh identifies very much land remaining and nevertheless orders its division (1, 6–7). The claim that Joshua took the whole land in 11:23 remains in explicit tension with this inventory; neither statement should replace the other.  Joshua 13:8–33: Moses’ eastern grants are recorded by families (15–32), yet Geshur and Maacath remain (13). Levi’s inheritance is described through offerings and Yahweh rather than a territorial share (14, 33). The Ammon wording in verse 25 remains in tension with Deuteronomy 2:19, 37.  Joshua 14:1–5: Nine tribes and a half receive the western inheritance by lot; Moses’ eastern grants remain in force (1–3). Levi’s cities and surrounding land qualify the denial of a territorial share (4). Allocation does not erase the remaining-land report of Joshua 13:1–7. Joshua 14:6–15: The promised land becomes Caleb’s named inheritance, Hebron, for him and his children (9, 13–14). Legal allocation and actual removal of inhabitants are distinguished: the latter is still proposed in verse 12.  Joshua 15: The lot for Judah is delimited on four sides and framed by reference to its families (1, 12). Allotment defines an inheritance; it does not by itself assert occupation of every named place.  Joshua 15:13–19: Inheritance moves from Judah’s tribal boundaries to Caleb’s portion and Achsah’s request for water (13–19). A land grant and the resources needed to use it are distinct gifts; the request for springs makes that practical distinction visible. Joshua 16: Family-based boundaries and Ephraimite cities within Manasseh’s territory distinguish allotment from a simple block of exclusive occupation (5–9). Gezer’s remaining Canaanites further distinguish inheritance from complete possession (10).  Joshua 17:14–18: The claim of insufficient land is answered with forest clearance and future expulsion, not a newly narrated lot ceremony (14–18). Allotment, usable land, and occupation are distinct.  Joshua 18:1–10: The gift of the land requires action: seven tribes have not divided their inheritance, and Joshua rebukes their delay in possessing it (2–3). A written city survey precedes allocation by lot (9–10); the already allotted shares remain in place (5, 7). Joshua 18:11–28: Inheritance is defined by family, borders, cities, and villages (11–28). The list does not establish occupation. Beth Arabah’s inclusion in both Judah’s and Benjamin’s lists (15:61; 18:22) remains an explicit unresolved overlap. Joshua 19:1–23: Family inheritance has different forms: Simeon’s towns lie inside Judah’s oversized share, Zebulun receives a boundary circuit and twelve cities, and Issachar receives sixteen cities with a Jordan endpoint. Allotment does not itself establish occupation (1–23).  Joshua 19:49–51: Completed distribution frames Joshua’s own inheritance among Israel (49–51). The closing formula concerns allocation; it does not cancel the remaining-land report of Joshua 13:1 or the non-removal notices of 16:10 and 17:12–13.  Joshua 20: Six named cities distribute refuge on both sides of the Jordan (7–8).  Joshua 21: Forty-eight cities and their suburbs belong to Levi within the other tribes’ possessions (41–42). No separate tribal territory does not mean no homes or land; Caleb’s fields and villages are expressly retained at Hebron (12). | [[From Shem to Abram]] · [[The Call of Abram]] · [[Abram in Egypt]] · | [[Language and Speech]] | Words as the medium of creation, command, blessing, and curse — and the one occasion God alters the mechanism itself; the Bible's remedy for Babel is translation, not reunification; at Sinai, divine command, accountable human words, and the request for Moses’ voice; false reports and other gods; the covenant words written down and read aloud; Exodus 34:29–35: Conversation changes Moses; its commands are passed to the people.; Proverbs 3:21–35: Words can postpone available help; mockery receives judgment; Proverbs 4:1–9: Received speech becomes the next generation’s instruction.; Proverbs 4:24: speech as the heart's first outflow; Proverbs 6:1–19: trapped by one's own words; the perverse mouth and the lying tongue; Proverbs 6:22–24: the teaching talks with the son against the smooth tongue; Proverbs 8:6–9: Wisdom's words, *"nothing crooked or perverse in them"*; Proverbs 10:6, 8, 10–14, 18–21, 31–32: The mouth can be a spring of life, feed many, and produce wisdom; it can also hide hatred, slander, and bring ruin; Proverbs 19: False testimony receives repeated condemnation; listening remains the route to knowledge; Proverbs 20: Speech discloses counsel, sells goods, betrays secrets, and binds; Proverbs 21: Lying tongues, guarded mouths, and witnesses; Proverbs 22: Gracious lips, frustrated words, and the adulteress's mouth; Proverbs 22:18, 21: words ready on the lips and reliable answers; good words wasted (23:8–9); Proverbs 24:26, 28: the honest answer as a kiss; the witness without cause; Proverbs 26: the proverb in a fool's mouth; the whisperer, the joke, and the lying tongue; Proverbs 28: Confession is joined to renunciation, rebuke to eventual favor, and flattery contrasted with correction; Proverbs 30: God’s flawless word is set against human additions, falsehood, slander, and a child’s cursing; Proverbs 30:32–33: silence before pressing anger; Proverbs 31:1–2, 8–9: The mother teaches; the king must speak for those without an effective voice; Proverbs 31:26, 28–31: Wise teaching and deserved praise answer misleading charm; Ecclesiastes 1:1–11: Weariness beyond uttering and hearing without fullness; Ecclesiastes 3: Silence and speech each have a time; Ecclesiastes 5:2–7: let your words be few before God; the fool's multitude of words; Ecclesiastes 6:10–12: Many words create vanity; their profit is questioned; Ecclesiastes 8:1–9: The king's effective word and the difficulty of challenging him; Ecclesiastes 9:13–10:4: quiet words of the wise above a ruler's cry; the fool's self-disclosure; Ecclesiastes 10:5–20: the fool swallowed by his own lips and multiplying words; private words carried; Ecclesiastes 12:9–14: Truthful instruction as goads and fixed nails; endless books warned against.  Leviticus 5:1–13: Speech can fail by omission of testimony or by rash oath; the corrective response itself includes specific confession (1, 4–5). Leviticus 19:1–18: Lies, false oaths, cursing the deaf, and slander are forbidden (11–16); rebuking a neighbor is required (17). The passage distinguishes harmful speech from correction instead of treating silence as the only righteous response.; Leviticus 20: Cursing father or mother carries death and personal liability (9).; Leviticus 24:10–23: A fight leads to blasphemy and cursing (10–11).  Numbers 22:22–35: Yahweh opens an animal’s mouth (28), and the episode ends by restricting the human speaker to the word given him (35).  Numbers 22:36–23:26: A royal commission to curse becomes a blessing because God supplies the speech (22:38; 23:5, 16).  Numbers 23:27–24:25: Balaam repeats that even a house full of silver and gold cannot authorize speech from his own mind (24:12–13). Balak can dismiss the speaker but cannot dictate the oracle. Deuteronomy 4:1–14: Israel heard *the voice of words* (12). The covenant comes as speech declared and then written (13). Deuteronomy 4:15–31: from the formless voice (15) to gods that cannot hear (28) to listening to his voice (30). Deuteronomy 5: Yahweh speaks to the assembly and writes the words on tablets (22).  Deuteronomy 6:1–9: The hearer must speak the commanded words to children throughout ordinary daily activities (7). Writing them on door posts and gates (9) accompanies spoken teaching; inscription does not replace conversation. Deuteronomy 11:18–32: teaching the children *"talking of them"* through the day (19).  Deuteronomy 27:11–26: Loud Levitical proclamation is answered by the same response from all the people after each curse (14–26). Speech binds the assembly to acknowledge standards that must also be enacted.  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: The invader’s language is not understood (49), intensifying alienation and powerlessness. No language or nation is identified by name. | [[The Surety, the Sluggard, and the Seven Abominations]] · [[The Tower of Babel]] · [[The Table of Nations]] · [[Noah's Sons and the Curse of Canaan]] · | [[Law and Obedience]] | Joshua 5:10–12: Keeping Passover at evening on the fourteenth day (10) follows the appointed timing recorded in Numbers 9:3. Joshua narrates observance without a fresh command in this unit. Keeping the commands frames the chapter (1, 6, 11, 20). Obedience is the question disclosed by testing and the practical opposite of forgetting Yahweh. Deuteronomy 9:7–29: The written words given at the assembly contrast with Israel’s rapid departure from the commanded way (10–16). At Kadesh the same refusal takes the form of disobeying the command to go up (23).  Deuteronomy 10:1–11: Yahweh writes the ten commandments again and Moses deposits them as commanded (4–5). The repeated words establish continuity after the breaking of the first tablets; this passage supplies no new ten-item law collection.  Deuteronomy 10:12–22: Fear, walking in God’s ways, love, wholehearted service, and keeping the commands belong to one answer about what God requires, explicitly for Israel’s good (12–13). Deuteronomy 11:1–17: obedience tied to possession, long days, and seasonal rain. Deuteronomy 11:18–32: listening or not listening decides blessing or curse (26–28); the hinge into the statutes (32). Deuteronomy 12:1–28: The law code opens; right in one's own eyes against right in Yahweh's eyes (8, 25, 28). Deuteronomy 26:16–19: Wholehearted keeping of commands belongs within both declarations.  Deuteronomy 27: The law must be written plainly on plastered stones (2–4, 8), while the people must listen and obey (9–10).  Deuteronomy 27:11–26: The final curse concerns upholding the law by doing it (26). Recitation and assent do not replace conduct. The preceding eleven cases exemplify rather than exhaust the obligation. Deuteronomy 28: conditional blessing in the land.  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: Failure to listen and do frames the sanctions (15, 45, 58, 62). Verse 58 expressly speaks of the words written in this book; the warning concerns enacted allegiance, not merely hearing a reading. Deuteronomy 29: Keeping and doing frame the address (9, 29). Hidden things belong to God, but revealed law obligates the community and its children; uncertainty is not an excuse to disregard what has been disclosed.  Deuteronomy 31: Divine delivery of the nations is joined to the people’s duty to do what Moses has commanded (5). Courage and obedience belong together; the passage does not substitute confidence for commanded action. Deuteronomy 31:9–13: Writing, custody, reading, hearing, learning, fearing, and doing belong together (9–13). Access to the law is communal, and the command requires actual obedience rather than hearing alone. The exact extent of “this law” remains unspecified.  Deuteronomy 31:14–23: Beside the public reading commanded in 31:9–13, the song must be written, taught, and placed in Israel's mouths (19, 22). Instruction can survive its teacher and expose disobedience, without guaranteeing that its hearers obey.  Deuteronomy 32: After the song Moses commands attention, teaching children, and doing all the law (46). The word is Israel’s life and the means of prolonged days in the land (47); memorizing the accusation cannot substitute for obedience.  Deuteronomy 33: Law is an inheritance for Jacob’s assembly (4), and Levi teaches it (10). Receiving blessing includes receiving instruction rather than freedom from it.  Joshua 3: Commands pass through officers and Joshua to people and priests (2–9). The priests enter the water and remain while the nation crosses (15–17); obedience takes concrete form in ordered movement. Joshua 4: People carry the stones, eastern tribes cross as Moses instructed, and priests leave at Joshua’s order (8, 12, 17–18). Obedience also includes teaching the event’s meaning (21–24).  Joshua 5:13–15: Joshua asks what his lord says to his servant and obeys the footwear command (14–15).  Joshua 6: Six daily circuits and seven on the seventh day follow the given command (3–20). Silence yields to a shout at Joshua’s order. The warning against taking devoted things (18) makes obedience after victory as consequential as obedience before it.  Joshua 8:30–35: altar and inclusive public reading. · [[The Gibeonite Deception]] Joshua 9: The chapter follows the public reading of the law with a covenant forbidden by Deuteronomy 7:1–2. Joshua 9:14 specifically identifies omitted inquiry; verses 18–20 then preserve the oath’s obligation. The conflict between the prohibition and the sworn protection is explicit, not silently harmonized.  Joshua 10:28–43: Verse 40 interprets destruction of all who breathed as obedience to Yahweh’s command, recalling Deuteronomy 20:16–18. The severity toward inhabitants must remain explicit rather than being reduced to destruction of idols. Joshua 13: Joshua must allocate the remaining territory as Yahweh has commanded (6–7). Obedience now takes the form of division for an inheritance, even while the promised removal of inhabitants is still future.  Joshua 14:1–5: The report brackets distribution with Yahweh’s command through Moses (2, 5). The procedure, administrators, and provision for Levites recall Numbers 34:13–29 and 35:1–8. Obedience now takes administrative rather than military form. Joshua 14:6–15: Caleb’s wholehearted following is stated by Caleb, in Moses’ recalled oath, and by the narrator (8–9, 14). The passage confirms this assessment rather than leaving it solely as Caleb’s self-description.  Joshua 15:13–19: Caleb’s portion is assigned according to Yahweh’s command to Joshua (13). This authorizes the grant; the passage does not say that Caleb’s marriage offer or Achsah’s request was separately commanded. Joshua 16: The non-removal and forced labor at Gezer (10) stand in tension with Deuteronomy 20:15–18, which distinguishes distant cities from the inherited cities of the named peoples, including Canaanites. The narrative reports the result without presenting forced labor as fulfillment of that command.  Joshua 18:1–10: Joshua challenges neglect of the land already given by Yahweh (3). The surveyors obey his commission and return with their record (8–9); Moses’ eastern grants are honored rather than redistributed (7).  Joshua 19:49–51: Israel grants Joshua his requested city according to Yahweh’s command (50). The passage asserts that authority without quoting an earlier instruction naming Timnathserah.  Joshua 20: Yahweh’s instruction through Moses becomes a command to Joshua and a communal assignment of named cities (1–2, 7–9).  Joshua 21: The Levite heads invoke the command through Moses, and Israel gives cities accordingly (2–3, 8). The named distribution implements the earlier command of Numbers 35:1–8. | [[Isaac in Gerar]] · [[Joseph and the Famine]] · [[Bricks Without Straw]] · [[The Death of the Firstborn and the Departure]] the Ark]] (Deuteronomy 31:24–29): Moses completes a written law and orders its preservation (24–26), yet predicts departure from the commanded way (29). Possession of the text and obedience to it remain distinct.  · [[The Song of Moses]]  · [[Moses Blesses the Tribes]] · [[The Death of Moses]] · [[After the Death of Moses]] · [[The Eastern Tribes Pledge to Cross]]  · [[Crossing the Jordan]] · [[The Twelve Stones from the Jordan]] · [[The Second Circumcision at Gilgal]] — Joshua 5:1–9: Joshua obeys the immediate command (2–3), while verse 6 remembers the earlier generation’s refusal to listen. The contrast concerns obedience, not military capacity alone.  · [[Passover at Gilgal and the End of the Manna]]  · [[The Commander of Yahweh's Army]]  · [[The Fall of Jericho]]  · [[The Law Read at Mount Ebal]] · [[The Five Kings in the Cave at Makkedah]] — defeated kings, courage, and burial.  · [[The Southern Cities Taken]] · [[The Northern Kings at the Waters of Merom]] — Verse 15 traces command from Yahweh through Moses to Joshua and declares its completion. · [[The Whole Land Taken]] · [[The Land That Remains]]  · [[The Division of the Land Begins]] · [[Caleb Claims Hebron]] (Joshua 14:6–15)  · [[Achsah Asks for Springs]] · [[The Inheritance of Ephraim]] · [[The Inheritance of Manasseh]] — Joshua 17:1–13: grant, possession, and obligation.  · [[The Land Surveyed at Shiloh]]  · [[Joshua's Own Inheritance]]  · [[The Cities of Refuge Appointed]]  · [[The Cities of the Levites]] |
| [[Life and Death]] | Life as God's breath lent to dust; death as the stated penalty for one prohibited act, and then as a refrain — "then he died", eight times; at Sinai, life promised, murder forbidden, and a frightened assembly left alive; intentional killing, refuge, negligent ownership, and unequal remedies; death expressly prescribed for Sabbath profanation and work (31:14–15); The threatened destruction of the nation is averted (32:10–14), but about three thousand are killed and a later plague is reported (32:28, 35); Exodus 33: Protected revelation under a stated mortal limit; Exodus 35: The Sabbath death sanction is repeated without an execution scene; Proverbs 3:13–20: Length of days and wisdom as a tree of life to those who retain her.; Proverbs 3:21–35: Retained wisdom gives life, secure steps, and sweet sleep; Proverbs 4:1–9: Retained teaching and wisdom’s preservation sustain life.; Proverbs 4:10–13: many years, and instruction as the son's life; Proverbs 4:23: the heart as life's wellspring; Proverbs 6:23–32: the way of life against the precious life hunted; Proverbs 8:35–36: whoever finds Wisdom finds life; her haters love death; Proverbs 10:2, 11, 16–17, 21, 27: Righteousness delivers from death; the righteous mouth is a spring of life and feeds many; accepting correction marks the way of life; Proverbs 19: Wisdom, commandment, correction, and fear of Yahweh point toward life; Proverbs 20: Royal danger, household blessing, extinguished life, and rescue; Proverbs 21: Seekers of death, the departed spirits, and life found; Proverbs 24:11: those led away to death, to be rescued; Proverbs 30:15–17: Sheol and the barren womb never satisfied; the mocker left unburied; Proverbs 31:3, 6: The ruin of kings is warned against; a person near death receives attention; Ecclesiastes 1:1–11: Passing generations contrasted with the continuing earth; Ecclesiastes 2:14–17: one event for wise and fool; *"So I hated life"*; Ecclesiastes 2:18–26: mortality frames the loss of control over a legacy; Ecclesiastes 3: Birth and death frame the catalogue of times; Ecclesiastes 3:16–22: Humans and animals share breath, death, and return to dust; Ecclesiastes 4:1–3: The dead and never-existent preferred in response to oppression; Ecclesiastes 5:8–20: Nothing carried from life, yet enjoyment within the days God gives; Ecclesiastes 6:1–9: A stillborn child preferred to long life without good; Ecclesiastes 6:10–12: The days of life pass like a shadow; Ecclesiastes 8:1–9: No person, including the ruler, controls the day of death; Ecclesiastes 9:1–12: The living know death; the dead lose their portion; Ecclesiastes 11:7–12:8: Decline, death, dust to earth, and spirit to God.; Leviticus 11: carcasses of edible animals also defile; Leviticus 17: The life of flesh grounds the blood restriction (11, 14).; Leviticus 18: Obedience is associated with life (5), child sacrifice is forbidden (21), and prohibited acts bring cutting off (29). Leviticus 19:1–18: The WEB forbids endangering the neighbor’s life (16), adjacent to its slander prohibition. The passage protects life within ordinary communal dealings; it does not narrate a death.; Leviticus 20: Death penalties, cutting off, and childlessness are differentiated (2–21, 27).; Leviticus 21: Death affects priestly access through corpse defilement and differentiated kinship exceptions (1–3, 11).; Leviticus 24:10–23: The law distinguishes killing a human, which carries death, from killing an animal, which requires replacement (17–21).; Numbers 6: the Nazirite kept from every corpse; Numbers 15:32–36: a judicial death, with no response by the accused reported; Numbers 16:41–50: Aaron stands between the dead and the living (48). The stopping of the plague preserves  Numbers 17: The retained rod is expressly intended to prevent deaths (10), yet Israel concludes in terror that everyone approaching the tabernacle dies (12–13).; Numbers 18: Repeated death warnings mark unauthorized approach and profanation (3, 7, 22, 32).  Numbers 19: Corpse, bone, grave, and death-tent contact require cleansing of the living.  Numbers 20:1–13: Miriam’s actual death (1) precedes the congregation’s wish to have died and its fear of dying with the animals (3–4). Numbers 20:22–29: Aaron dies and Israel mourns; succession continues the office, not his life.  Numbers 21:4–9: Many die from the bites, while bitten people who look at the brass serpent live (6–9). Rescue here is survival, not a narrated resurrection of the dead. Numbers 22:36–23:26: Balaam wishes to die the death of the righteous and share Israel’s end (23:10).  Numbers 26: The old counted generation is gone except Caleb and Joshua; Korah’s sons survive, and the new count follows the plague (1, 11, 64–65). Continuity belongs to the people without erasing individual deaths. Deuteronomy 4:41–43: The stated purpose of flight is that the unwitting killer may live (42). Causing a death is not treated as sufficient reason to deny all protection. Deuteronomy 5: Murder is forbidden (17), while honoring parents carries a promise of long life and well-being in the land (16).  Deuteronomy 5:23–33: The people acknowledge surviving God's speech but fear that further hearing will kill them (24–26).  Deuteronomy 6:1–9: Prolonged days are a stated purpose of keeping the commands, across three generations (2). The promise concerns lived continuity in the land, not a claim here about life after death.  Deuteronomy 10:1–11: Aaron dies, but Eleazar succeeds him (6). Israel’s threatened destruction is averted (10); the continuity of people and office does not mean the absence of individual death. Deuteronomy 11:18–32: days multiplied *"as the days of the heavens above the earth"* (21).  Deuteronomy 31:14–23: Moses' approaching death frames the commission and the provision of enduring testimony (14, 16). Sleeping with his fathers names his death in this context; it does not by itself settle a doctrine of the intermediate state.  Deuteronomy 32: Yahweh alone kills and makes alive, wounds and heals (39). The closing exhortation calls the instruction Israel’s life (47). The first statement asserts divine sovereignty; it does not by itself specify a resurrection event.  Deuteronomy 32:48–52: Moses is told to die on the mountain and be gathered to his people, with Aaron’s death as the precedent (50). The command anticipates death; this unit does not narrate it or specify the condition of the dead.  Deuteronomy 33: The blessing is explicitly spoken before Moses’ death (1), and begins its tribal sayings with Reuben’s life rather than death (6). The leader’s mortality frames hope for the people’s continuance.  Joshua 20: The fugitive is protected from death before trial (9), while the high priest’s death marks the permitted return home (6). | [[The Garden of Eden]] · [[The Fall]] · [[Cain and Abel]] · [[The | [[Light and Darkness]] | The Bible's first made thing and its first act of ordering — light spoken into being and divided from a darkness that was already there and is never said to be created; both are named and bounded rather than one abolished, until Revelation removes night altogether. In Job the pair turns hostile: chapter 3 asks for a day back, *light* comes to mean simply *being alive*, Bildad makes extinguished light a sentence, and the whirlwind opens by accusing Job of darkening counsel; at Sinai, the thick darkness contains God’s presence rather than signifying his absence; The lampstand stands south of the table, outside the inner veil (26:35); The lamp needs supplied oil and evening-to-morning care, with no symbolism stated here.; Exodus 34:29–35: Brightness is borne by a person returning from divine speech.; Exodus 35: Oil and lamp equipment are specified, and the rulers bring oil; Exodus 37: One lampstand with seven lamps made; lighting comes later.; Proverbs 4:18–19: the righteous path as dawning light, the wicked way as darkness that trips; Proverbs 6:23: the commandment a lamp and the law light, as in Psalm 119:105; Proverbs 20: Two distinct lamps: life extinguished and inward searching; Proverbs 21: Pride named the lamp of the wicked; Proverbs 24:20: the lamp of the wicked snuffed out; Ecclesiastes 2:13–14: wisdom over folly as light over darkness; Ecclesiastes 5:8–20: Eating in darkness amid frustration contrasts with enjoyed provision; Ecclesiastes 6:1–9: The stillborn child departs and its name is covered in darkness; Ecclesiastes 11:7–12:8: Sweet light set beside darkness and declining sight.; Song 3:8: swords against *"fear in the night"*; Song 6:4–10: Morning, moon, and sun  Leviticus 24:1–9: The lamp is practical sanctuary light maintained with pure beaten olive oil (2–4). Its continual service has an explicit nightly schedule. The unit does not itself identify the oil with the Spirit or explain the light as a moral allegory. Deuteronomy 5: Fire, cloud, and thick darkness accompany the public divine voice (22).  Deuteronomy 5:23–33: The divine voice comes from the darkness while the mountain burns with fire (23). | [[The Creation of the World]] · [[Let the Day Perish]] · [[Bildad's Second Speech]] · [[I Know
