| [[Love]] | *ʾĀhab* — one ordinary verb for a father's love, a husband's, and eventually Israel's for God; its first occurrence in Scripture is Genesis 22:2, in the clause that identifies what is about to be asked for, and in Genesis it is comparative before it is anything else — every stated preference in the book detonates, and Deuteronomy 21:15–17 is written to limit the damage; at Sinai, love for God paired with commandment keeping; a servant's *"I love my master, my wife, and my children"*; help across enmity; Exodus 34: Abundant loving kindness forms part of the divine proclamation; Yahweh's love the reason for his reproof (Proverbs 3:12); Proverbs 4:1–9: Affection and embrace directed toward personified wisdom.; Proverbs 8:17: *"I love those who love me"*; Proverbs 8:36: *"All those who hate me love death"*; Proverbs 10:12: Love covers wrongs where hatred keeps conflict active; Proverbs 19: Self-care through wisdom and kindness contrast with friendships dependent on gifts; Proverbs 20: Love must be faithful; it also sustains the throne; Proverbs 22: Training and the rod as household care; Ecclesiastes 3: Love and hate; embracing and refraining; Ecclesiastes 9:1–12: Unknown love, ended love, and marital love; Song of Solomon 1:1–4: desire and affirmation; Song of Solomon 1:9–2:7: mutual praise and the charge not to awaken love until it so desires; Song 3:10: a carriage *"paved with love"*; the man returns her *"better than wine"* (Song 4:10; 1:2); Song 5:9–6:3: beloved and friend, with mutual belonging renewed; Song 6:4–10: One beloved among many; Song 6:11–7:9: Desire expressed through a body ; Song 7:10–8:4: mutual desire, the woman’s initiative, and restraint; Song 8:8–12: family plans, the woman’s answer, and her own vineyard Leviticus 19:1–18: Love of the neighbor as oneself (18) stands beside the rejection of inward hatred, vengeance, and grudges (17–18). Rebuke is commanded too: love is compatible with truthful correction. This develops the family preferences of Genesis into an obligation toward the neighbor.; Leviticus 19:19–37: The resident foreigner must be treated as native-born and loved as oneself (33–34). Deuteronomy 5: Loving Yahweh is paired with keeping his commandments, and divine loving kindness is promised to thousands (10).  Deuteronomy 6:1–9: Love of Yahweh is commanded with all heart, soul, and might (5). The following commands put the words on the heart and into daily household speech (6–9). Love is therefore expressed through sustained attention and teaching as well as obedience. Deuteronomy 7:1–11: Yahweh’s love explains Israel’s redemption alongside fidelity to the fathers’ oath (7–8); Israel’s answering love is joined to obedience (9). Deuteronomy 7:12–26: *He will love you, bless you, and multiply you*, as the first of the blessings that follow listening (13).  Deuteronomy 10:12–22: Love joins God’s delight in the fathers (15), Israel’s wholehearted response (12), God’s provision for the foreigner (18), and Israel’s duty to love that foreigner (19). Deuteronomy 11:1–17: love stated as keeping commands and wholehearted service (1, 13). Deuteronomy 11:18–32: love with walking and clinging as the condition of possession (22).  Deuteronomy 33: Divine love for the people frames the reception of words (3), and Benjamin is beloved and protected (12). Love is expressed through instruction and shelter. | [[The Binding of Isaac]] · [[A Wife for Isaac]] · [[Jacob and Esau and the Sold Birthright]] · [[The Stolen Blessing]] · [[Jacob, Leah, and Rachel]] · [[The Birth of Jacob's Children]] · [[Joseph Sold into Slavery]] · [[The Silver Cup and Judah's Plea]] · [[The Song of the Sea]] · [[The Ten Commandments]] · [[The Altar and the Hebrew Slave]] · [[Justice, Sabbaths, and Feasts]] · [[The Covenant Renewed]] · [[Trust in Yahweh with All Your Heart]] · [[Get Wisdom]] · [[The Strange Woman and the Wife of Your Youth]] · [[The Young Man Void of Understanding]] · [[Wisdom Calls at the Gates]] · [[Wisdom at the Creation]] · [[The Two Feasts]] · [[A Wise Son Makes a Glad Father]] · [[Hope Deferred]] (Proverbs 13:24) · [[A Way That Seems Right]] (Proverbs 14:22) · [[A Gentle Answer]] (Proverbs 15:9, 12, 17) · [[A Dry Morsel with Quietness]] (Proverbs 17:9, 17) · [[A Strong Tower]] (Proverbs 18:21, 24) · [[Better Is the Poor Who Walks in Integrity]] (Proverbs 19:4, 6–8, 17, 22) · [[Wine Is a Mocker]] (Proverbs 20:6, 28) · [[A Good Name]] (Proverbs 22:6, 15) · [[Do Not Envy Sinners]] · [[Do Not Boast About Tomorrow]] · [[A Time for Every Purpose]] (Ecclesiastes 3:1–15) · [[One Event Comes to All]] · [[Love Better Than Wine]] · [[Dark and Lovely]] · [[Delight and the Unawakened Love]] · [[Rise Up, My Love]] · [[Seeking Him by Night]] · [[Solomon's Wedding Procession]] · [[You Are All Beautiful]] · [[A Garden Locked and Opened]] · [[I Opened, but He Had Gone]] · [[Altogether Lovely]] · [[My Dove Is Unique]] · [[Return, Return, Shulammite]] (Song 6:11–7:9)  · [[Come into the Fields, My Beloved]] (Song of Solomon 7:10–8:4) · [[Love Is Strong as Death]] (Song 8:5–7) · [[Our Sister and My Own Vineyard]] · [[Make Haste, My Beloved]] (Song 8:13–14; | [[Marriage]] | Man and woman as one flesh; the created ordinance the New Testament argues every marriage question from, and the relation a patriarch denies three times to save himself; and a proposal of full intermarriage refused by the household its own policy would have had refuse it; at Sinai, parents are honored, adultery forbidden, and coveting a neighbor’s wife prohibited; a female servant's food, clothing, and marital rights protected; Exodus 34: Marriage alliances prohibited in the warning against rival worship; called a covenant of God in Proverbs 2:17, in Malachi 2:14's vocabulary; Proverbs 6:29–35: the injured husband's jealousy accepts no ransom; Proverbs 19: A quarrelsome wife is continual dripping; a prudent wife is Yahweh’s gift; Proverbs 21: The contentious wife, spoken from the husband's side; Proverbs 30:19, 23: the way of a man with a maiden; the unloved woman married; Proverbs 31:11–12, 23, 28–31: Trust, sustained good, and a husband’s praise of his wife’s own works; Ecclesiastes 9:1–12: Joy with the wife one loves as a portion; Song 3:11: *"the day of his weddings"*, the Song's only named wedding, seen as a public procession with no bride in view; in the Song, *"my sister, my bride"*, all six uses of *bride* in the book, and the locked garden opened (4:8–5:1); Song 6:4–10: Singular attachment and royal plurality; Song 8:8–12: family plans, the woman’s answer, and her own vineyard; Leviticus 18: The half-sister, aunt, and simultaneous sister marriages excluded in 18:9, 12, 18 stand in tension with the unions reported in Genesis 20:12; 29:23–30; Exodus 6:20.; Leviticus 19:19–37: The woman is pledged to another man but remains unfree (20).; Leviticus 20: The brother’s-wife prohibition and childlessness sanction (21) remain in tension with Judah’s command to Onan to raise offspring for his dead brother in Genesis 38:8.; Leviticus 21: Verse 7 prohibits priests from marrying a prostitute, a profane woman, or a divorced woman. Numbers 36: Heiresses may choose a husband within their father's tribal family (6–8); the five sisters marry paternal cousins (11). This rule concerns daughters possessing inheritance, not a stated universal ban on marriages between tribes. Deuteronomy 5: Adultery and coveting the neighbor’s wife are forbidden (18, 21). Deuteronomy 7:1–11: Both giving daughters and taking daughters in marriage with the named nations are forbidden (3).  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: Betrothal gives way to another man taking the woman (30); the siege destroys even affection between spouses (54–57). The protected beginnings of household life in Deuteronomy 20:5–7 are reversed.  Joshua 15:13–19: Caleb promises his daughter to the man who captures Kiriath Sepher; Othniel succeeds and receives Achsah (16–17). Her consent to that arrangement is not narrated. Her subsequent initiative in seeking a field and springs is explicitly narrated (18–19). | [[The Garden of Eden]] · [[The Fall]] · [[The Line of Cain and the Birth of Seth]] · [[The Sons of God and | [[Mediation and Advocacy]] | Someone to stand between; Job's four legal offices — umpire, witness, guarantor, redeemer — sought against God, and the New Testament's answer in a different configuration; at Sinai, the assembly requests the mediator who approaches where it will not; an angel guide who must be heard and does not pardon; Moses alone comes near, writes, reads, and sprinkles the covenant blood; The meeting place above the ark is situated within a separating veil; Moses transmits the rule; Israel provides oil; Aaron and his sons tend it on the people’s behalf; the high priest carries Israel's names, judgment, and iniquity before Yahweh; Moses ordains the priests at the place of meeting; Moses pleads for a guilty people and secures relief from the threat of total destruction (32:11–14); Exodus 33: The favored mediator pleads for shared presence; Exodus 34: Moses intercedes in solidarity after receiving the proclamation; Exodus 34:29–35: Moses moves between divine speech and public instruction.; Exodus 35: Moses brings the mountain instructions to the whole assembly; Moses announces, summons, distributes, listens, and stops the collection; Exodus 36:8–38: the inner boundary is made; no entry or intercession occurs here; Exodus 37: The promised meeting place prepared; no encounter or intercession narrated.; Exodus 38: Moses’ received commands and administrative direction organize shared service; Exodus 39: The objects bearing Israel’s names are made; their use remains ahead; Proverbs 6:1–5: the guarantor's handshake warned against, using Job 17:3's two verbs.; Proverbs 22:23; 23:11: Yahweh as advocate, *yārîb rîbām*; Proverbs 31:8–9: The ruler must use his own voice for another person’s cause  Leviticus 8: Moses installs the priestly mediators.; Leviticus 9: inaugural offerings, blessing, and public manifestation; Leviticus 10:12–20: bearing the congregation's iniquity  Leviticus 24:1–9: Israel supplies oil and the bread is arranged on Israel’s behalf, while Aaron tends the lamps and the priests eat the holy portion (2–9). Mediation here is ordered service representing the community, with no narrated plea or dispute.; Leviticus 24:10–23: Moses receives the accused, waits with the community for Yahweh’s will, and conveys the ruling (11–13, 23).; Numbers 15:32–36: Moses receives the sentence; no intercession recorded  Numbers 16:1–40: Moses and Aaron plead that one man’s sin should not entail anger against the entire congregation (22).; Numbers 16:41–50: Moses directs and Aaron acts for the congregation that has turned against them. Aaron’s  Numbers 20:1–13: Moses and Aaron withdraw from the assembly to fall before Yahweh (6); no spoken petition is recorded.  Numbers 21:4–9: Moses prays for the people who have spoken against him, then makes the object Yahweh commands (7–9). His work mediates rescue without making him its independent source. Numbers 27:1–11: The daughters speak for their family's claim before the assembled authorities; Moses brings their cause to Yahweh (2–5). The resulting judgment affirms their speech. Asking for a ruling is not treated as rebellion. Numbers 36: The clan heads' concern is affirmed just as the daughters' earlier claim was (27:7; 36:5). Moses conveys the ruling, though a separate consultation is not narrated here as it was in 27:5–6. Deuteronomy 3:23–29: The mediator petitions for himself here (23–25), unlike his request for a successor in Numbers 27:15–17. His denied request leaves him responsible for encouraging that successor (28). Mediation does not grant exemption from judgment. Deuteronomy 4:15–31: Moses' exclusion *for your sakes*, now sworn (21–22). Deuteronomy 5: Moses stands between Yahweh and the fearful people (5), yet the people themselves hear the divine speech (4, 22).  Deuteronomy 5:23–33: The people request mediation and promise obedience; Yahweh approves their words (27–28). Deuteronomy 9:7–29: The proposed greater nation from Moses (14) is followed by his advocacy for the endangered people and Aaron (18–20). His prayer insists on their identity as Yahweh’s people and inheritance (26, 29).  Deuteronomy 10:1–11: Moses’ forty-day stay and Yahweh’s listening answer the threatened destruction (10). The mediator is then commissioned to lead the continuing journey (11). | [[The Surety, the Sluggard, and the Seven Abominations]] · [[Miserable Comforters and the Witness in Heaven]] · [[I Know That | [[Mourning and Lament]] | The rite — torn garment, sackcloth, dust or ashes on the head, raised voice, sitting on the ground — and the stated term that bounds it, most often seven days; plus lament, its speech half, which Job 3 pushes past every boundary the rite has. Indistinguishable from repentance by any outward sign, which is what ruins Job's friends; Exodus 33: Jewelry removed in mourning over threatened absence; Ecclesiastes 3: Weeping and mourning have their time beside joy; Ecclesiastes 4:1–3: Tears of living victims; no funeral or bounded mourning rite; Ecclesiastes 11:7–12:8: Public mourners accompany the image of final departure.; Leviticus 10:1–11: priestly gestures restricted; Israel may mourn; Leviticus 10:12–20: Aaron appeals to the day's events; Leviticus 19:19–37: Cuttings in the flesh for the dead are prohibited (28).; Leviticus 21: Priests may defile themselves for the close relatives enumerated in 2–3, while the high priest may not even for parents (11).; Numbers 6: a vow that forbids defilement for near kin  Deuteronomy 1:34–46: Weeping follows the failed ascent in Deuteronomy 1:45, whereas Numbers 14:39 reports mourning before it. Both details can be retained without collapsing their narrative positions. | [[Seven Days of Silence]] · [[The Wager in Heaven and the Four Messengers]] · [[The Second Test]] · [[Joseph Sold into Slavery]] · [[The Deaths of Jacob and Joseph]] · [[Miserable Comforters and the Witness in Heaven]] · [[I Know That My Redeemer Lives]] · [[The Silver Cup and Judah's Plea]] · [[Joseph Reveals Himself]] · [[Let the Day Perish]] · [[The Arrows of the Almighty]] · [[Why Do the Wicked Live]] · [[Oh That I Knew Where I Might Find Him]] · [[The Months of Old]] · [[Now They Mock Me]] · [[I Repent in Dust and Ashes]] · [[The Restoration of Job]] · [[Bricks Without Straw]] · [[The Last Plague Announced]] · [[The Death of the Firstborn and the Departure]] · [[Show Me Your Glory]] · [[A Way That Seems Right]] (Proverbs 14:10, 13) · [[A Gentle Answer]] (Proverbs 15:13, 15) · [[A Time for Every Purpose]] (Ecclesiastes 3:1–15) · [[The Tears of the Oppressed]] (Ecclesiastes 4:1–3) · [[The House of Mourning]] (Ecclesiastes 7:2–4) · [[Remember Your Creator]] · [[Nadab and Abihu and the Strange Fire]] · [[The Uneaten Sin Offering]] · [[Holy Living in Field and Community]] · [[The Holiness of the Priests]] · [[The Nazirite Vow]] · [[The Seventy Elders and the Quail]] · [[The People Refuse the Land]] · [[The Defeat at Hormah]] · [[Baal Peor and the Zeal of Phinehas]] — Numbers 25: The congregation weeps at the Tent’s door as the man brings the Midianite woman (6).  · [[The Sentence and the Presumptuous Ascent]] (Deuteronomy 1:34–46) · [[A Holy People and Its Food]] · [[Captives, Firstborn Sons, and Public Shame]] · [[First Fruits and the Tithe Declaration]] · [[The Death of Moses]] |
| [[Murmuring]] |  · [[The Gibeonite Deception]] Joshua 9: The congregation murmurs against the princes after the deception is discovered (18). The narrator supplies a concrete leadership failure; the complaint is not automatically equated with the wilderness rebellions, nor is a punishment for it narrated. | The wilderness generation's complaint against Moses, and through him against God — *lûn*, a wilderness word found only in Exodus 15–17 and Numbers 14–17; first at Marah, a two-word question of need answered with sweet water and a statute, and later answered with judgment, first at Taberah under a rarer verb, and then at Kibroth Hattaavah, where the people craved with food in hand and Moses joined the complaint  Numbers 16:1–40: Moses identifies the complaint against Aaron as gathering against Yahweh (11).; Numbers 16:41–50: The next day’s complaint recasts the preceding judgment as killing by Moses and Aaron (41)  Numbers 17: The sign is intended to end complaints against Moses and Aaron, which Yahweh treats as directed against himself (5, 10).  Numbers 20:1–13: The lack of water is real (2), but the people turn it into an accusation against the exodus and its leaders (3–5).  Numbers 21:4–9: Discouragement on the detour becomes speech against God and Moses. The people reject the provision they have, then confess that their speech was sin (5–7).| [[Bitter Water at Marah]] · [[Manna and Quail]] · [[Water from the Rock]] · [[The Fire at Taberah]] · [[The Seventy Elders and the Quail]] · [[Miriam and Aaron Against Moses]] · [[The Twelve Spies]] · [[The People Refuse the Land]]  · [[The Rebellion of Korah]] · [[Aaron Stands Between the Dead and the Living]]  · [[Aaron's Rod Buds]]  · [[The Waters of Meribah]]  · [[The Brass Serpent]] · [[Reuben, Gad, and the Land Beyond the Jordan]] |
| [[Nakedness and Covering]] | *ʿĀrôm* and *ʿerwâ* — nakedness without shame in Eden, exposure after it, and a covering always supplied by someone else; the image the canon closes with; no steps to the altar, and the priests' breeches; linen breeches for the priests at the altar; Exodus 34:29–35: The face veil covers radiance rather than shameful exposure; Exodus 39: The linen breeches required in chapter 28 are now made.; Proverbs 31:21–22, 25: Literal textiles become the image for strength and dignity; Ecclesiastes 5:8–20: Naked arrival and departure mark the limits of ownership; Ecclesiastes 9:1–12: White garments within commended enjoyment; Leviticus 18: Uncovering nakedness organizes the kinship prohibitions (6–18); the father’s wife and brother’s wife are described through the corresponding male relative (8, 16).; Leviticus 20: The stored WEB describes intercourse with the father’s, uncle’s, or brother’s wife as uncovering the corresponding man’s nakedness (11, 20–21). | [[The Garden of Eden]] · [[The Fall]] · [[Noah's Sons and the Curse of Canaan]] · [[The Stolen Blessing]] · [[Joseph Sold into Slavery]] · [[Judah and Tamar]]  · [[Joseph in Potiphar's House]] · [[The Brothers' First Journey to Egypt]] · [[The Wager in Heaven and the Four Messengers]] · [[Eliphaz's Third Speech]] · [[Oh That I Knew Where I Might Find Him]] · [[The Months of Old]] · [[The Altar and the Hebrew Slave]] · [[Laws of Holiness and Compassion]] · [[The Priestly Garments]] · [[The Shining Face of Moses]] · [[The Priestly Garments Made]] (Exodus 39:1–31) · [[The Worthy Woman]] · [[The Lover of Silver and the Gift of Enjoyment]] (Ecclesiastes 5:8–20) · [[One Event Comes to All]] · [[I Opened, but He Had Gone]] · [[Forbidden Unions and the Defiled Land]] · [[Penalties and a People Set Apart]] · [[Care for the Neighbor and Ordered Distinctions]] · [[Marriage Accusations and Sexual Offenses]] · [[Divorce, Remarriage, and the Newlywed]] · [[Justice, Family Duty, Honest Weights, and Amalek]] |
| [[Names and Naming]] | Genesis stops about forty times to say why someone or somewhere is called what it is called, and the explanations are verdicts on the moment rather than etymologies — a name as a receipt, as a claim on a future that has not happened, or as an argument; the practice reaches its densest point in the twelve naming speeches of Genesis 29–30, every one spoken by a woman; tribal names engraved on the priest's stones; Exodus 33: Joshua’s patronymic and Moses known by name; Exodus 39: Tribal names engraved in stones; Yahweh named on the crown.; Proverbs 22: A good name above riches; Ecclesiastes 6:10–12: Naming long ago within an account of human limits; Song of Solomon 1:1–4: the name as fragrant oil; Song 6:11–7:9: Shulammite without a biography; Numbers 1: a count by name  Numbers 21:1–3: Hormah is named after victory despite its earlier use in Numbers 14.  Numbers 26: Ancestral names organize families and inheritance (5–56). Serah and Zelophehad’s five daughters are named despite the military register’s scope. Numbers 27:1–11: The five sisters are individually named (1), and their argument concerns the survival of their father's name within his family (4). Inheritance preserves family continuity; the text does not describe a requirement to change anyone's personal name.  Deuteronomy 3: Hermon is called Sirion by Sidonians and Senir by Amorites (9). These are coexisting names attributed to peoples, not an Israelite renaming ceremony or an etymology. Deuteronomy 3:12–22: Jair names his acquired territory Havvoth Jair after himself (14). The continuing place-name preserves conquest memory; its relation to the later Jair tradition remains unresolved. Joshua 14:6–15: Verse 15 supplies Hebron’s former name, Kiriath Arba, and associates it with the greatest man among the Anakim. It does not narrate when the name changed or identify who changed it.  Joshua 15: The boundary supplies paired designations: the Jebusite location/Jerusalem, Baalah/Kiriath Jearim, and Mount Jearim/Chesalon (8–10). These are explanatory identifications, not narrated acts of renaming.  Joshua 15:13–19: Kiriath Arba is identified with Hebron and linked to Anak’s father (13); Debir’s former name is Kiriath Sepher (15). The passage supplies these identifications without narrating a renaming ceremony or explaining the meaning of Kiriath Sepher. Joshua 18:11–28: The text explicitly pairs Luz with Bethel, Kiriath Baal with Kiriath Jearim, and the Jebusite with Jerusalem (13–14, 28). It does not similarly equate Geliloth with Gilgal or Kiriath with Kiriath Jearim. Joshua 19:1–23: The WEB renders Beersheba (or Sheba) as one entry (2), fitting the stated thirteen towns. Similar names in different tribal settings are not automatically identical: the two Rimmons, the northern Bethlehem, and Issachar’s Beth Shemesh need distinct treatment. | [[The Birth of Jacob's Children]] · [[Jacob's Flocks]] · [[Jacob's Flight from Laban]] · [[Wrestling at the Jabbok]] · [[The Reconciliation with Esau]] · [[Return to Bethel; the Deaths of Rachel and Isaac]] · [[Judah and Tamar]] · [[Pharaoh's Dreams and Joseph's Rise]] · [[Jacob's Family Settles in Egypt]] · [[Jacob Blesses Ephraim and Manasseh]] · [[Jacob Blesses His Sons]] · [[The Deaths of Jacob and Joseph]] · [[The Restoration of Job]] · [[The Oppression in Egypt]] · [[The Birth of Moses]] · [[Moses Flees to Midian]] · [[Bitter Water at Marah]] · [[Manna and Quail]] · [[Water from the Rock]] · [[The Battle with Amalek]] · [[Jethro's Counsel]] · [[The Priestly Garments]] · [[Show Me Your Glory]] · [[The Priestly Garments Made]] (Exodus 39:1–31) · [[A Good Name]] (Proverbs 22:1) · [[Who Knows What Is Good]] (Ecclesiastes 6:10–12) · [[The House of Mourning]] (Ecclesiastes 7:1) · [[Love Better Than Wine]] · [[Return, Return, Shulammite]] (Song 6:11–7:9) · [[The Census in the Wilderness of Sinai]] · [[The Fire at Taberah]] · [[The Seventy Elders and the Quail]] · [[The Twelve Spies]]  · [[Arad and Hormah]]  · [[The Second Census]]  · [[The Petition of Zelophehad's Daughters]] · [[Reuben, Gad, and the Land Beyond the Jordan]]  · [[The Defeat of Og Recounted]] · [[The Eastern Inheritance and the Charge to Joshua]] · [[Justice, Family Duty, Honest Weights, and Amalek]] · [[The Second Circumcision at Gilgal]] — Joshua 5:1–9: Verse 9 explains Gilgal’s name through Yahweh’s declaration that Egypt’s reproach is rolled away. This records the WEB narrative’s explanation without independently asserting a Hebrew derivation. · [[Caleb Claims Hebron]] (Joshua 14:6–15)  · [[The Borders of Judah]]  · [[Achsah Asks for Springs]] · [[The Cities of Judah]] · [[The Inheritance of Manasseh]] — Joshua 17:1–13: grant, possession, and obligation. · [[The Inheritance of Benjamin]] · [[The Lots of Simeon, Zebulun, and Issachar]] · [[The Lots of Asher, Naphtali, and Dan]] |
| [[The Names of God]] | What God is called and who calls him it; from Genesis 14 the *ʾĒl* titles accumulate, most of them attached to places and several supplied by people outside the covenant line; at Sinai, the known divine name must not be taken in vain; *"every place where I record my name"*; God's name placed *"in"* the angel sent ahead; Yahweh who sanctifies Israel (31:13); Exodus 33: The known name will be proclaimed with goodness and mercy; Exodus 34: The known name proclaimed through character; Jealous names exclusive allegiance; Proverbs 30: The Holy One and Yahweh; the son’s name is asked but not supplied; Leviticus 18: Yahweh’s self-identification frames the commands (2, 4–6, 21, 30). Leviticus 19:1–18: Yahweh’s repeated self-identification grounds the commands, and false swearing by his name is called profanation (12). The name belongs to the ethics of speech as well as worship.; Leviticus 19:19–37: Repeated divine self-identification grounds commands about produce, bodies, sanctuary, elders, foreigners, and measures.; Leviticus 20: Giving offspring to Molech profanes Yahweh’s holy name (3).; Leviticus 21: Priests must not profane the name of their God because they present his offerings (6).; Leviticus 24:10–23: The WEB calls the object of the offense the Name (11), and the ruling identifies Yahweh’s name (16).  Numbers 23:27–24:25: The WEB oracle introductions use God, Almighty, and Most High (24:4, 16), while the narrative names Yahweh. These are observations about the stored English translation, not a new claim about Hebrew forms. Deuteronomy 4:15–31: *a devouring fire, a jealous God* (24) and *a merciful God* (31). Deuteronomy 5: Misuse of Yahweh’s name carries guilt (11).  Deuteronomy 10:12–22: The WEB names Yahweh God of gods and Lord of lords (17). Deuteronomy 12:1–28: The nations' name destroyed from their places; Yahweh's name put at his (3, 5).  Deuteronomy 32: The Rock names the just and faithful God (4), rejected by his prosperous people (15). Fatherhood and birth imagery (6, 18) describe his relation to Israel. Rival rocks cannot protect their dependents (31, 37–39). These observations concern the stored WEB, without a source-language claim. | [[The Rescue of Lot and Melchizedek]] · [[The Covenant Between the Pieces]] · [[Hagar and Ishmael]] · [[Circumcision, the Sign of the Covenant]] · [[The Treaty at Beersheba]] · [[The Binding of Isaac]] · [[Isaac in Gerar]] · [[Isaac Sends Jacob to Paddan Aram]] · [[Jacob's Ladder at Bethel]] · [[The Birth of Jacob's Children]] · [[Jacob's Flight from Laban]] · [[Return to Bethel; the Deaths of Rachel and Isaac]] · [[The Second Journey]] · [[Jacob Blesses Ephraim and Manasseh]] · [[Jacob Blesses His Sons]] · [[Though He Slay Me]] · [[Where Shall Wisdom Be Found]] · [[Out of the Whirlwind]] · [[The Burning Bush]] · [[Bricks Without Straw]] · [[I Am Yahweh]] · [[Aaron's Staff Before Pharaoh]] · [[Water Turned to Blood]] · [[Frogs, Lice, and Flies]] · [[Livestock, Boils, and Hail]] · [[The Song of the Sea]] · [[Bitter Water at Marah]] · [[Manna and Quail]] · [[The Battle with Amalek]] · [[The Ten Commandments]] · [[The Altar and the Hebrew Slave]] · [[The Angel Sent Ahead]] · [[Bezalel, Oholiab, and the Sabbath Sign]] · [[Show Me Your Glory]] · [[The Covenant Renewed]] · [[A Strong Tower]] (Proverbs 18:10) · [[The Words of Agur]] (Proverbs 30:3–5, 9) · [[Forbidden Unions and the Defiled Land]]  · [[Be Holy and Love Your Neighbor]] · [[Holy Living in Field and Community]] · [[Penalties and a People Set Apart]] · [[The Holiness of the Priests]] · [[Eating the Holy Things]] · [[Acceptable Victims and the Holy Name]] · [[The Blasphemer and One Law for All]] · [[Yahweh Bless You and Keep You]]  · [[Balaam's Last Oracles]] · [[No Form Seen and No Image Made]] · [[Has Any People Heard God and Lived]] · [[The Ten Commandments Recounted]]  · [[What Yahweh Requires of Israel]] · [[The Place Yahweh Will Choose]] · [[A Prophet Like Moses]]  · [[The Song of Moses]] |
| [[The Nations]] | The destruction of the nations becomes a warning to Israel itself (20). The comparison concerns shared liability to judgment, not a new list of peoples or a newly narrated conquest. Deuteronomy 9:1–6: The nations are stronger than Israel, with fortified cities and the tall Anakim (1–2). Their wickedness explains expulsion (4–5); the unit names no new nation and supplies no list of specific offenses. Deuteronomy 11:18–32: nations greater and mightier driven out, on condition of obedience (23–25). Deuteronomy 26:16–19: Israel is promised honor above the nations Yahweh has made. Deuteronomy 28: conditional blessing in the land.  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: An unnamed distant nation invades, the king is deported, and Israel becomes a byword among peoples (36–37, 49–50, 64). The text does not identify a particular later empire, so no identification is imposed here. Deuteronomy 29: Israel has encountered other nations’ idols (16–18); later nations ask why Yahweh devastated Israel’s land (24–28). Their question receives a covenant explanation rather than an account of Yahweh’s defeat.  Deuteronomy 32: The nations receive bounds (8), a foolish nation provokes Israel (21), enemies might claim divine victory as their own (27), and nations are finally invited to rejoice with his people (43). No particular later empire is named.  Joshua 2: A woman within threatened Jericho confesses Yahweh’s rule in heaven and earth and seeks protection under an Israelite oath (9–14). The story distinguishes her response from the king’s pursuit rather than treating all inhabitants as a single moral actor.  Joshua 3: Canaanites, Hittites, Hivites, Perizzites, Girgashites, Amorites, and Jebusites are named in the promised expulsion (10). The passage gives no individual encounter with them, and no battle takes place here. Joshua 4: The purpose includes all peoples knowing Yahweh’s mighty hand (24). This states an aim, not a report that every nation already knows.  Joshua 6: Rahab moves from Jericho into Israel (25). This stands in tension with the sweeping destruction of the land’s peoples in Deuteronomy 7:1–2 and 20:16–18: Joshua names an exception, without supplying a general legal reconciliation. Joshua 8:30–35: altar and inclusive public reading. · [[The Gibeonite Deception]] Joshua 9: The Hittites, Amorites, Canaanites, Perizzites, Hivites, and Jebusites form the opposition (1–2). Gibeon’s Hivites take another course (3–7), surviving through a treaty and imposed service. This is another specific exception to the destruction program, alongside Rahab’s household, with different circumstances. Joshua 10: Five Amorite kings attack Gibeon because of its peace with Israel (3–5). Gibeon’s inhabitants, identified as Hivites in Joshua 9:7, now receive Israel’s military protection. The land’s peoples do not all occupy the same relation to Israel.  Joshua 10:28–43: The summary reports comprehensive destruction (40), while Gibeon remains protected and later Joshua 16:10 records Canaanites in Gezer. Horam’s defeated expedition (33) is not itself a report that every inhabitant of Gezer died. Joshua 12:1–24: Six peoples of the west; Geshur and Maacath at Og's border (2–8). Joshua 13: Philistine lordships, Geshurites, Avvim, Canaanites, Sidonians, Amorites, and Gebalites appear in the geography of remaining territory (2–6). The Philistine-controlled stretch is counted as Canaanite (3). These are collective populations, not personal names for new biographical pages.  Joshua 13:8–33: Geshur and Maacath remain within Israel (13). Ammon’s land appears in Gad’s portion (25), creating a territorial tension with the protected land in Deuteronomy 2:19, 37, not a narrated conquest of Rabbah. | [[The Table of Nations]] · [[Noah's crossing and lose heart (1). They are unnamed groups here, not identified individual rulers.  · [[The Fall of Jericho]]  · [[The Law Read at Mount Ebal]] · [[The Sun Stands Still at Gibeon]] · [[The Five Kings in the Cave at Makkedah]] — defeated kings, courage, and burial.  · [[The Southern Cities Taken]] · [[The Northern Kings at the Waters of Merom]] — The coalition includes Canaanites, Amorites, Hittites, Perizzites, Jebusites, and Hivites (3). · [[The Whole Land Taken]] · [[The Kings Struck Down]] · [[The Land That Remains]]  · [[The Inheritance Beyond the Jordan]] · [[The Cities of Judah]] |
| [[Oaths and Vows]] | Binding yourself with words; the first human oath in the Bible is Abram's at 14:22, sworn before it was spoken, and used to refuse a gift rather than to promise one; the first oath sworn *between two men* is at Beersheba, demanded by the foreign king he had lied to, and the place is named for the swearing; at Sinai, misuse of the divine name incurs guilt; false swearing is an application; The oath sworn by God’s own self is Moses’ ground of appeal (32:13); Exodus 33: God recalls the land oath after the calf; Proverbs 20: Consider the dedication before the obligation becomes a snare; Proverbs 31:2: The mother calls Lemuel the son of her vows without explaining their terms; Ecclesiastes 5:4–6: pay what you vow; better not to vow than to vow and not pay; Ecclesiastes 8:1–9: The oath to God grounds keeping the royal command; Ecclesiastes 9:1–12: Taking and fearing an oath before the common end; Song of Solomon 2:7: the daughters of Jerusalem adjured by the roes and the hinds; Song 5:9–6:1: the audience questions the adjuration and offers help ; Song 8:4: the refrain returns without the roes and hinds  Leviticus 5:1–13: Hearing an adjuration and failing to testify (1) is distinct from uttering a rash oath (4). The remedy does not command carrying out an evil promise.  Leviticus 6:1–7: False swearing about another’s property belongs to the wrong requiring repayment and an offering (3, 5–7). This differs from the rash oath of Leviticus 5:4 and exposes the abuse of the property oath accepted in Exodus 22:10–11.; Leviticus 7:11–21: Vow and freewill meat have a two-day eating window Leviticus 19:1–18: False swearing by God’s name profanes that name (12). The rule prohibits false oaths; it does not here prohibit every oath.; Numbers 6: a vow any Israelite may take, binding diet, hair, and contact with the dead  Numbers 21:1–3: Israel makes and fulfills a conditional vow of destruction.  Deuteronomy 1:34–46: Yahweh’s oath denying entry to the evil generation stands beside the earlier oath to give the land to the fathers (34–35). The children’s entry continues the promise without reversing their parents’ exclusion. Deuteronomy 7:1–11: The oath sworn to the fathers is named as a reason for the exodus alongside Yahweh’s love (8). Deuteronomy 7:12–26: The covenant and the land are both what Yahweh *swore to your fathers* (12–13).  Deuteronomy 10:12–22: Swearing by Yahweh’s name stands with fearing, serving, and clinging to him (20). Deuteronomy 11:1–17: the land sworn to the fathers frames the conditional blessing (9). Deuteronomy 11:18–32: the land Yahweh swore to the fathers (21). Deuteronomy 12:1–28: Vows paid only at the chosen place (11, 17, 26).  Deuteronomy 27:11–26: The people’s repeated Amen assents to stated curses (15–26). This resembles the woman’s assent in Numbers 5:22, but here it is communal covenant acknowledgment, not a voluntary vow or an individual ordeal. Deuteronomy 29: The entire assembly enters Yahweh’s covenant and oath (10–15), grounded in his oath to the fathers. This is communal allegiance across generations, not an optional private vow.  Joshua 2: Rahab asks for an oath by Yahweh, and the spies pledge their lives for hers (12–14). The scarlet line, the gathering of her household inside, and secrecy specify the obligation and its limits (17–20). This is promised rescue; fulfillment belongs to Joshua 6.  Joshua 6: The spies’ oath now obliges them to rescue Rahab’s household (22–25), while Joshua’s oath places a curse on rebuilding Jericho (26). Binding speech protects one household and threatens the future builder’s household. Joshua 14:6–15: Moses’ remembered oath grounds Caleb’s request for the land he walked and an inheritance for his children (9). Joshua’s grant in verses 13–14 gives the old promise a named territorial expression. | [[The Rescue of Lot and Melchizedek]] · [[The Covenant Between the Pieces]] · [[The Treaty at Beersheba]] · [[The Death of Moses]] · [[The Eastern Tribes Pledge to Cross]]  · [[Rahab and the Spies]] · [[The Second Circumcision at Gilgal]] — Joshua 5:1–9: Verse 6 places two divine oaths together: exclusion of the disobedient generation and the gift of the land to the fathers. Judgment on one generation does not annul the continuing promise to their children.  · [[The Fall of Jericho]] Joshua 10: The life-preserving oath of Joshua 9:15–20 has a military consequence: Gibeon appeals for rescue and Joshua comes (6–9). No new oath is sworn here; the connection is the practical effect of the earlier undertaking. | Joshua 9: The princes regard their oath by Yahweh as binding after discovering the fraud (18–20). They fear wrath for breaking it. This is a narrated decision in a conflicted case, not a general rule that every promise obtained by deception must be enforced. · [[The Sun Stands Still at Gibeon]] · [[The Gibeonite Deception]] · [[The Whole Land Taken]] · [[Caleb Claims Hebron]] (Joshua 14:6–15) |
| [[The Poor and the Vulnerable]] | The alien, the widow, the fatherless, and the poor — the people with no protector; shown without a law in Genesis, the test of the righteous in Job, and named together at Sinai, where God takes the protector's place, hears their cry, and forbids Israel Egypt's verbs of oppression; justice and access to food; Proverbs 3:21–35: A broad duty of timely help includes, but is not restricted to, the vulnerable.; Proverbs 10:4, 15: Verse 4 warns that laziness brings poverty; verse 15 recognizes poverty itself as destructive without assigning blame; Proverbs 19: The poor are abandoned by friends but honored for integrity; pity for them is lending to Yahweh; Proverbs 20: Warnings about idleness do not erase injustice or protection of debtors; Proverbs 21: Stopped ears answered by an unheard cry; Proverbs 22: One Maker of rich and poor; the poor fed, not oppressed; Proverbs 22:22–23; 23:10–11: Yahweh pleads the case of the poor and the fatherless; Proverbs 28: The poor may have integrity and discernment (6, 11), but poverty does not guarantee virtue: the oppressor in verse 3 is himself needy; Proverbs 30: Agur fears poverty for himself and condemns people who consume the poor as if with weapon-like teeth; Proverbs 31:5–9: Relief of misery is joined to the right to an advocate and righteous judgment; Proverbs 31:20: The productive household extends its hands beyond its own members; Ecclesiastes 4:1–3: Power with oppressors; victims without a comforter; Ecclesiastes 4:13–14: a youth born poor ranked above a king; Ecclesiastes 5:8–20: Poor people oppressed within layers of official authority; Ecclesiastes 6:1–9: The poor person who knows how to walk among the living; Ecclesiastes 9:13–10:4: the poor wise man whose wisdom is despised  Leviticus 5:1–13: Inability to afford a lamb, and then even two birds, receives explicit provision (7, 11). Access to the stated remedy is not restricted to livestock owners.; Leviticus 12: A mother unable to afford a lamb may bring two turtledoves or two young pigeons (8).; Leviticus 14:1–32: restoration after healing  Leviticus 19:1–18: The poor and foreigner receive harvest provision (9–10); hired workers, deaf people, and blind people receive explicit protections (13–14). Verse 15 rejects favoritism toward the poor in court, distinguishing support from a biased verdict.; Leviticus 19:19–37: Elders receive honor and foreigners protection and love (32–34); a father must not make his daughter a prostitute (29). Leviticus 23:9–22: Field corners and gleanings remain for poor people and foreigners.  Leviticus 25:1–22: Servants, hired workers, and resident foreigners have access to the land’s food (6–7).  Leviticus 25:23–34: Poverty can force a sale of family property (25). Kin redemption, self-redemption, and Jubilee keep that loss from necessarily being permanent (25–28); Leviticus 25:35–55: support without interest, then limits on a sold brother  Numbers 27:1–11: Five women facing exclusion from their father's possession can present a public petition and receive an enforceable inheritance ruling (2–11). The text establishes their vulnerability to loss of inheritance; it does not say they are already destitute. Numbers 36: Numbers 27 protects daughters without brothers against loss of their father's inheritance. Numbers 36 retains that protection while limiting heiresses' marriage options for the tribe's sake. Neither passage establishes that the sisters are destitute. Deuteronomy 5: Servants and resident strangers receive rest alongside the household head (14).  Deuteronomy 10:12–22: Yahweh executes justice for the fatherless and widow and gives the foreigner food and clothing (18).  Deuteronomy 27:11–26: Misleading the blind on the road and withholding justice from the foreigner, fatherless, and widow receive curses (18–19). Vulnerability must not become an opportunity for exploitation. | [[The Covenant Between the Pieces]] · [[Judah and Tamar]] · [[Eliphaz's Third Speech]] · [[Oh That I Knew Where I Might Find Him]] · [[The Months of Old]] · [[Job's Oath of Innocence]] · [[The Oppression in Egypt]] · [[The Burning Bush]] · [[Laws of Holiness and Compassion]] · [[Justice, Sabbaths, and Feasts]] · [[The Incense Altar, the Ransom, and the Holy Oil]] · [[Do Not Withhold Good]] · [[A Wise Son Makes a Glad Father]] · [[A False Balance]] (Proverbs 11:1–31) · [[Whoever Loves Correction]] (Proverbs 12:9, 11, 24) · [[Hope Deferred]] (Proverbs 13:7–8, 18, 23) · [[A Way That Seems Right]] (Proverbs 14:20–21, 31) · [[A Gentle Answer]] (Proverbs 15:15, 25) · [[A Dry Morsel with Quietness]] (Proverbs 17:5) · [[A Strong Tower]] (Proverbs 18:23) · [[Better Is the Poor Who Walks in Integrity]] (Proverbs 19:1, 4, 7, 17, 22) · [[Wine Is a Mocker]] (Proverbs 20:4, 13, 16) · [[The King's Heart]] (Proverbs 21:13) · [[A Good Name]] (Proverbs 22:2, 7, 9, 16) · [[Thirty Excellent Things]] (Proverbs 22:22–23; | [[Praise and Song]] | Speech to God that celebrates what he is and has done, and the music and dance that carry it — absent from Genesis except in Laban's never-given send-off (31:27), and first sung at the sea, after deliverance and after faith; Moses hears singing where Joshua hears the sound of war, and he sees dancing before the calf (32:17–19); Proverbs 31:28–31: Human excellence receives household and public praise; Song of Solomon 1:1–4: praise of human love; Song 5:9–16: her answer praises the beloved’s whole person; Song 6:4–10: The women praised her; Song 6:11–7:9: The upward description; Leviticus 7:11–21: Thanksgiving expressed through bread and sacrifice; Leviticus 19:19–37: All fourth-year fruit is holy for giving praise to Yahweh (24).; Numbers 21:21–35: The Heshbon poem is introduced as the speech of those who speak in proverbs (27), not as Israel singing to Yahweh. Its celebration of Sihon and lament over Moab stand within an account of Sihon’s own defeat; this preserved poetry differs from the nearby well song.  Deuteronomy 10:12–22: Yahweh is Israel’s praise because of the great deeds it has seen (21).  Deuteronomy 31:14–23: Song here serves as a witness against the people (19–21). This broadens the theme beyond celebration: a taught and remembered song can expose covenant betrayal. Moses writes and teaches it in 22; its words are reserved for the following song unit.  Deuteronomy 32: The poem begins with proclamation of Yahweh’s greatness and justice (1–4), accuses Israel, and ends by calling nations to rejoice with his people (43). Song carries warning and promised vindication together. The prose says Moses and Joshua spoke it (44), without describing instruments or a musical performance. | [[Jacob's Flight from Laban]] · [[The Song of the Sea]] · [[The Golden Calf]] · [[The Worthy Woman]] · [[Love Better Than Wine]] · [[Altogether Lovely]] · [[My Dove Is Unique]] · [[Return, Return, Shulammite]] (Song 6:11–7:9) · [[Thanksgiving, Vows, and the Peace Offering]] · [[Holy Living in Field and Community]] · [[The Two Silver Trumpets]] · [[The Journey to Moab]] · [[Sihon and Og]]  · [[What Yahweh Requires of Israel]]  · [[A Song to Witness Against Israel]]  · [[The Song of Moses]] |
| [[Prayer]] | A human being addressing God unbidden and asking for something — which Genesis takes twenty-four chapters to produce, because everything before it is either invocation (*calling on the name*) or a reply inside a theophany. The first one is a slave's, it is entirely on somebody else's behalf, it proposes a concrete test, and it is answered before he has finished speaking; Moses first argues from God’s deliverance, reputation among the Egyptians, and oath to the fathers (32:11–13); Exodus 33: Petition answered with presence and limited revelation; Exodus 34: Worship leads into a renewed plea for the people; Proverbs 8:17: seeking diligently and finding; Proverbs 21: The cry not heard, and worship refused from the wicked; Proverbs 28: Prayer becomes an abomination when the speaker refuses to hear the law (9), developing the contrast between upright prayer and wicked sacrifice in 15:8; Proverbs 30: Agur’s two requests concern truthfulness and sufficient provision; Ecclesiastes 5:2: speech before God unhurried and few  Numbers 16:1–40: Moses asks Yahweh not to respect the rebels’ offering (15), yet with Aaron pleads for the congregation (22).; Numbers 16:41–50: Moses and Aaron fall on their faces (45), but no words of prayer are supplied. Moses then  Numbers 20:14–21: The message remembers Israel crying to Yahweh and being heard (16). This is a retrospective report of answered prayer, not a recorded prayer for permission to cross Edom.  Numbers 21:1–3: Yahweh hears Israel’s collective petition.  Numbers 21:4–9: The people ask Moses to pray for the serpents to be taken away. His prayer is reported without its words; the answer provides life for the bitten, without stating that the snakes disappear (7–9). Deuteronomy 1:34–46: The people weep before Yahweh after defeat, but he does not listen (45). The text reports no words of a prayer and does not make this particular refusal a rule that every later cry will be rejected. Deuteronomy 3:23–29: Moses begins with praise of Yahweh’s incomparable works and asks for personal entry into the land (23–25). The refusal is explicit, including a prohibition on further petition about this matter (26). This is a particular denied request, not a rule that prayer is futile. Deuteronomy 4:1–14: No great nation has a god so near *whenever we call on him* (7). Calling is assumed as Israel's ordinary practice, not narrated as a particular prayer. Deuteronomy 9:7–29: Moses recalls forty days and nights without bread or water in intercession (18), prays specifically for Aaron (20), and appeals to redemption, the ancestors, and the interpretation Egypt would give destruction (26–29). Yahweh listens (19).  Deuteronomy 10:1–11: Verse 10 recalls that Yahweh listened to Moses again and would not destroy Israel. No prayer text is repeated here; the preceding appeal in 9:26–29 supplies the immediate context.  Deuteronomy 33: Moses asks Yahweh to hear Judah, bless Levi’s skills, and accept his work (7, 11). The poem includes petitions alongside declarations and commands. · [[The Gibeonite Deception]] Joshua 9: The men examine provisions but fail to ask counsel from Yahweh (14). This is neglected inquiry before a decision, not a recorded prayer refused. Numbers 27:21 provides an institutional context for Joshua’s access to counsel without proving which procedure should have been used here. Joshua 10: Joshua speaks to Yahweh while publicly commanding sun and moon to stop (12). The narrative interprets the result as Yahweh listening to a human voice (14). This exceptional battle day is not a general promise that every request controls nature. | [[The Three Visitors and Abraham's Intercession]] · [[Abraham and Abimelech]] · [[A Wife for Isaac]] · [[Jacob and Esau and the Sold Birthright]] · [[The Birth of Jacob's Children]] · [[Wrestling at the Jabbok]] · [[Return to Bethel; | [[Pride and Self-Exaltation]] | The lifted heart forgets rescue and credits its own strength with wealth (14, 17). The corrective is acknowledgment that productive power itself is given (18), not a denial that people work. Deuteronomy 9:1–6: The imagined inward boast now claims moral credit for possession (4), following the claim to self-made wealth in 8:17. Moses blocks the inference from success to deservingness. Deuteronomy 29: The inward claim of peace despite stubbornness (19) is a form of presumptuous security. Hearing a covenant warning while privately exempting oneself defeats its intended response. | [[The Surety, the Sluggard, and the Seven Abominations]] · [[The Tower of Babel]] · [[The Sons of God and the Corruption of the Earth]] · [[The Fall]] · [[Zophar's Second Speech]] · [[The Call of Abram]] · [[Elihu Speaks in Anger]] · [[Elihu on God's Justice]] · [[Elihu on God's Greatness]] · [[Out of the Whirlwind]] · [[Behemoth and Leviathan]] · [[The Oppression in Egypt]] · [[Bricks Without Straw]] · [[Livestock, Boils, and Hail]] · [[Locusts and Darkness]] · [[The Song of the Sea]] · [[Trust in Yahweh with All Your Heart]] · [[Do Not Withhold Good]] · [[Wisdom Calls at the Gates]] · [[A False Balance]] (Proverbs 11:1–31) · [[Whoever Loves Correction]] (Proverbs 12:9, 15) · [[Hope Deferred]] (Proverbs 13:10) · [[A Gentle Answer]] (Proverbs 15:25, 33) · [[The Plans of the Heart]] (Proverbs 16:5, 18–19) · [[A Strong Tower]] (Proverbs 18:11–12) · [[The King's Heart]] (Proverbs 21:2, 4, 24) · [[A Good Name]] (Proverbs 22:4, 10) · [[The Glory of Kings]] (Proverbs 25:6–7, 14, 27) · [[Answer a Fool]] · [[Do Not Boast About Tomorrow]] · [[The Wicked Flee When No One Pursues]] (Proverbs 28:11, 26) · [[Where There Is No Vision]] (Proverbs 29:23) · [[The Words of Agur]] (Proverbs 30:2–4, 9, 12–13) · [[Four Things]] (Proverbs 30:32) · [[The House of Mourning]] (Ecclesiastes 7:8) · [[Covenant Warnings and Remembered Promises]] · [[Miriam and Aaron Against Moses]] · [[The Defeat at Hormah]] · [[Offerings in the Land and Sins of Ignorance]]  · [[The Sentence and the Presumptuous Ascent]] (Deuteronomy 1:34–46) · [[Remember the Wilderness and the Giver of Wealth]] (Deuteronomy 8:1–20) · [[Not Because of Your Righteousness]] · [[Judges, Worship, and Difficult Cases]] · [[The Law for the King]] · [[The Covenant in Moab and the Hidden Things]] |
| [[Priesthood]] | Someone appointed to stand between God and people; *kōhēn* enters the Bible at 14:18 attached to a Canaanite king with no sanctuary, no sacrifice, no genealogy and no law — which is exactly what Psalm 110 and Hebrews 7 build on; covenant sacrifices offered by *"young men"*; Aaron and his sons receive a lamp duty before their appointment and ordination instructions; Aaron and his four sons named for the priest's office; the high priest bears names, judgment, and iniquity; the ordination rite, *filling the hand*, and the daily lamb; craftsmen commissioned to make the priestly garments (31:10); Aaron makes the calf while the instructions for his appointment have already been given (28:1; 32:1–6); Exodus 35: Aaron and his sons remain recipients of the commissioned garments after the calf; Exodus 38: Ithamar accounts; Aaron is named; the women’s ministry is not defined as priesthood; Exodus 39: Priestly clothing made before the priests are dressed or ordained.  Leviticus 1: The regulated offering retains active lay participation: the livestock offerer brings, lays a hand on the cattle victim, kills, and prepares the animal; the priests handle blood and the altar.; Leviticus 2: The priest brings the gift to the altar and burns its memorial; Aaron and his sons receive the most holy remainder (2:2–3, 8–10). Priestly service includes receiving a sacred portion, alongside handling offerings.; Leviticus 3: The offerer lays a hand on the victim and kills it; Aaron's sons handle its blood and the priest burns the specified portions (3:2–5, 8–11, 13–16); Leviticus 4:1–21: The anointed priest is both potentially liable for sin affecting the people (3) and the minister who makes atonement for the congregation (20). · [[The Sin Offering for Priest and Congregation]]; Leviticus 4:22–35: Offerer slaughters; priest handles blood and makes atonement.  Leviticus 5:1–13: The priest offers the sin-offering bird first, then the burnt offering; with flour he burns a memorial handful and receives the remainder (8–13).  Leviticus 6:1–7: The ram is brought to the priest, who makes atonement before Yahweh (6–7). Unlike the holy-things case of 5:16, the repayment with its fifth goes to the owner (5), not to the priest.  Leviticus 6:8–23: Maintained fire, restricted holy eating, and wholly burned priestly grain. Leviticus 6:24–7:10: Eligible male priests eat most holy flesh in a holy place; the officiant receives the assigned offering and the burnt offering’s skin. Leviticus 7:9–10 juxtaposes prepared offerings for the officiant with oil-mixed or dry grain for all sons equally. The exact distribution is not fully explained.; Leviticus 7:11–21: Peace-offering eating governed by time, purity, and sacred ownership  Leviticus 7:22–38: The breast belongs to Aaron and his sons; the right thigh belongs to the son who offers blood and fat (31–34). Leviticus 8: Public installation before inaugural service.; Leviticus 9: inaugural offerings, blessing, and public manifestation; Leviticus 10:1–11: sobriety, distinction, and teaching; Leviticus 10:12–20: portions eaten, and a burned sin offering questioned; Leviticus 11: Aaron shares the commission to teach distinctions; Leviticus 12: The mother brings the offerings to the priest at the Tent of Meeting's door (6). The.; Leviticus 13:1–46: examines and pronounces status without a described cure ; Leviticus 13:47–59: garment inspection and handling; Leviticus 14:1–32: restoration after healing ; Leviticus 14:33–57: house inspection, repair, and cleansing  Leviticus 15: paired bird offerings after the waiting period;; Leviticus 17: The priest sprinkles the peace offering’s blood and burns its fat at the sanctuary altar (6).; Leviticus 19:19–37: An unnamed priest makes atonement for the man with the trespass-offering ram (22).; Leviticus 21: Ordinary priests may incur corpse defilement for specified close relatives (1–3), but the high priest may not do so even for parents (10–12). Leviticus 23:9–22: The priest waves gifts for acceptance and receives a holy portion.  Leviticus 24:1–9: Aaron tends the lamps and Aaron with his sons receives the bread (3–4, 9). Priestly service includes maintaining furnishings and eating assigned holy food, alongside the slaughter and blood rites treated earlier.  Leviticus 25:23–34: Leviticus 25:32–34 explicitly gives Levites redeemable houses and unsaleable fields, qualifying the claim that they have no property.; Numbers 1: the Levites set apart from the army; Numbers 6: a lay Nazirite under rules alongside priestly law  Numbers 16:1–40: Levite service is already a gift of nearness, yet Korah seeks priesthood also (9–10).; Numbers 16:41–50: The defended office becomes a means of rescue: Aaron runs among the people who accuse him  Numbers 17: The chosen rod belongs to Aaron for Levi (3, 8).; Numbers 18: Priestly service and Levite assistance are gifts with distinct boundaries (1–7).  Numbers 19: Eleazar officiates; clean persons gather ashes and apply water. Numbers 20:22–29: Aaron’s garments pass to Eleazar, continuing the office after his death.  Numbers 26: Eleazar shares the count once performed with Aaron. Levi is separately counted from one month upward and explicitly excluded from the other tribes’ inheritance (57–64). Deuteronomy 9:7–29: Aaron himself is threatened with destruction and receives Moses’ intercession (20). His office does not exempt him from the judgment associated with the calf; no new ordination or replacement is narrated.  Deuteronomy 10:1–11: Aaron’s death is followed by Eleazar’s ministry (6). Levi’s collective appointment includes carrying the ark, ministering, and blessing (8–9). Numbers 18 distinguishes Aaron’s altar service from Levite assistance; Deuteronomy’s collective wording should not silently erase that distinction.  Deuteronomy 27: The Levitical priests speak with Moses to all Israel, calling for silence, listening, and obedience grounded in belonging to Yahweh (9–10).  Deuteronomy 27:11–26: The Levites must pronounce the curses loudly to all Israel (14). Levi also appears among the tribes on Gerizim (12); the text does not specify how the speaking group is positioned relative to that tribal assignment. Deuteronomy 31:9–13: Levitical priests and all the elders receive Moses’ written law (9). The ensuing command is addressed to them (10), without naming a single future reader or excluding the elders from responsibility. No personal priestly name is given.  Deuteronomy 33: Levi’s blessing unites sacred discernment, teaching, incense, burnt offering, and covenant loyalty (8–11); the godly one is not personally named. The tribe and its priestly functions are joined without a detailed account of internal offices.  Joshua 3: Unnamed Levitical priests carry the covenant ark into the Jordan and stand until all Israel crosses (3, 17). Their service supports the entire people. This passage does not reconcile their ark-bearing role with the Kohathite transport regulations of Numbers 4. Joshua 4: The ark-bearing priests hold their position until the work is finished and leave at the transmitted divine command (10–18). No individual priest is named.  Joshua 6: Seven unnamed priests sound rams’ horns before the ark, with armed men ahead and a rear guard behind (4–13). Priestly service accompanies the military procession; the account does not name these priests or identify their horns as the silver instruments of Numbers 10. Joshua 8:30–35: altar and inclusive public reading.  Joshua 13:8–33: Levi receives no territorial share: offerings made by fire are its inheritance (14), and Yahweh is its inheritance (33). Compare Deuteronomy 18:1–2; the collective wording does not resolve Numbers 18’s distinctions between priests and other Levites.  Joshua 14:1–5: Eleazar the priest participates with Joshua and tribal heads in allocation (1). The Levites’ exclusion from tribal territory is paired with cities and land for livestock and property (3–4); the passage does not collapse the distinction between priests and other Levites.  Joshua 18:1–10: Yahweh’s priesthood is called the Levites’ inheritance instead of a territorial portion (7). The broad tribal wording stands beside Numbers 18:1–7’s restricted altar priesthood; it does not explicitly erase that distinction or the cities allowed in Joshua 14:4.  Joshua 19:49–51: Eleazar’s priestly role includes shared responsibility for dividing the land with Joshua and the tribal household heads (51). Priestly participation does not make the grant a unilateral priestly decision.  Joshua 20: Residence continues until the death of the high priest of those days (6).  Joshua 21: The list distinguishes Aaron’s thirteen priestly cities from the remaining Kohathites’ ten, Gershon’s thirteen, and Merari’s twelve (4–7, 19–40). Not every Levite is thereby called an Aaronic priest. | [[The Rescue of Lot and Melchizedek]] · [[Jacob's Ladder at Bethel]] · [[Pharaoh's Dreams and Joseph's Rise]] · [[Jacob's Family Settles in Egypt]] · [[Joseph and the Famine]] · [[Jacob Blesses His Sons]] · [[The Birth of Moses]] · [[The Heads of the Fathers' Houses]] · [[Jethro's Counsel]] · [[Israel at Mount Sinai]] · [[The Blood of the Covenant]] · [[The Altar and the Court]] · [[The Priestly Garments]] · [[The Ordination of the Priests]] · [[The Incense Altar, the Ransom, and the Holy Oil]] · [[Bezalel, Oholiab, and the Sabbath Sign]] · [[The Golden Calf]] · [[The Freewill Offering]] · [[The Altar, the Basin, the Court, and the Inventory]] (Exodus 38:1–31) · [[The Basin Made and Used - Exodus 30, 38, and 40]] · [[The Priestly Garments Made]] (Exodus 39:1–31) · [[The Work Completed]] (Exodus 39:32–43) · [[The Tabernacle Raised and the Glory]] (Exodus 40:1–38)  · [[The Burnt Offering]] · [[The Meal Offering]] · [[The Peace Offering]] · [[The Sin Offering for Ruler and Individual]] (Leviticus 4:22–35)  · [[Confession and Offerings According to Means]] · [[The Trespass Offering for Holy Things and Unknown Guilt]] (Leviticus 5:14–19); The priest receives the holy-things restitution with its added fifth and makes atonement with the ram (16); the second case also brings the ram to the priest (18). confirms a future within Aaron’s line (7, 11–13).  · [[The Second Census]] · [[Joshua Commissioned]] · [[The War Against Midian]] Numbers 35: Levites receive forty-eight cities with surrounding land, including six refuges (2–8). This confirms the correction to absolute propertylessness: Numbers 18 withholds a tribal inheritance, while Numbers 35 grants residential and pastoral holdings. The anointed high priest’s death ends the required refuge residence (25–28); the text does not explain the mechanism or call that death a sacrifice. Deuteronomy 12:1–28: The landless Levite brought to the feast and not to be forsaken (12, 19).  · [[Crossing the Jordan]]  · [[The Inheritance Beyond the Jordan]]  · [[The Cities of the Levites]] | Numbers 34: Eleazar the priest shares the allotment task with Joshua and tribal leaders (17–29). | [[Prophecy and Vision]] | God putting words in a human mouth; the prophetic books' standing superscription — the word of Yahweh coming to someone — and *maḥăzeh*, vision, both enter the Bible at Genesis 15:1, and Genesis 20:7's first *nābîʾ* is defined by prayer rather than prediction; Exodus 34:29–35: Received speech is conveyed without a new dream or vision report.; Proverbs 30: Agur’s heading calls his words a revelation in the WEB; Proverbs 31:1: The WEB calls the mother’s teaching a revelation without narrating a prophetic call ; Numbers 22:1–21: A man approached with rewards of divination receives actual divine speech (7–12, 20).  Numbers 22:22–35: The summoned speaker cannot see the angel until Yahweh opens his eyes.  Numbers 22:36–23:26: The narrative twice attributes Balaam’s words to Yahweh (23:5, 16).  Numbers 23:27–24:25: Balaam sees Israel’s tents and claims a vision beyond the immediate scene (24:2–4, 15–19). The future ruler is not near in 24:17, but no date or personal name is supplied. Predictions must not be filed as already narrated fulfillments. Deuteronomy 31:14–23: Yahweh announces apostasy and its consequences before entry, explicitly grounding the warning in knowledge of the people's present ways (16–21). This unit predicts betrayal but does not narrate a later historical fulfillment.  Joshua 13:8–33: Balaam is called the soothsayer in the WEB death notice (22). This designation stands beside his constrained blessing of Israel in Numbers 22–24; the notice gives no new oracle or account of his counsel. | [[The Covenant Between the Pieces]] · [[The Call of Abram]] · [[Hagar and Ishmael]] · [[Circumcision, the Sign of the Covenant]] · [[The Three Visitors and Abraham's Intercession]] · [[Abraham and Abimelech]] · [[The Birth of Isaac and the Sending of Hagar]] · [[The Binding of Isaac]] · [[Jacob and Esau and the Sold Birthright]] · [[The Stolen Blessing]] · [[Jacob's Ladder at Bethel]] · [[Jacob's Flocks]] · [[Jacob's Flight from Laban]] · [[Joseph Sold into Slavery]] · [[The Cupbearer and the Baker]] · [[Pharaoh's Dreams and Joseph's Rise]] · [[The Brothers' First Journey to Egypt]] · [[The Second Journey]] · [[The Silver Cup and Judah's Plea]] · [[Jacob's Family Settles in Egypt]] · [[Jacob Blesses Ephraim and Manasseh]] · [[Jacob Blesses His Sons]] · [[Eliphaz's First Speech]] · [[Elihu Speaks in Anger]] · [[The Burning Bush]] · [[Moses Returns to Egypt]] · [[Bricks Without Straw]] · [[Aaron's Staff Before Pharaoh]] · [[The Death of the Firstborn and the Departure]] · [[The Song of the Sea]] · [[The Shining Face of Moses]] · [[Wisdom Calls in the Street]] · [[Where There Is No Vision]] (Proverbs 29:18) · [[The Words of Agur]] (Proverbs 30:1, 5–6) · [[The Words of King Lemuel]] · [[The Seventy Elders and the Quail]] · [[Miriam and Aaron Against Moses]]  · [[Balak Sends for Balaam]]  · [[Balaam's Donkey]]  · [[Balaam's First Two Oracles]]  · [[Balaam's Last Oracles]] · [[Do Not Follow Other Gods]] · [[A Prophet Like Moses]]  · [[A Song to Witness Against Israel]]  · [[The Book Beside the Ark]] (Deuteronomy 31:24–29): Moses predicts corruption after his death on the basis of already evident rebellion (27–29). The latter-days wording supplies no date here; no later fulfillment is narrated in this unit. · [[The Death of Moses]]  · [[The Inheritance Beyond the Jordan]] |
| [[Providence]] | Joshua 5:10–12: The manna ceases after Israel eats the land’s produce (11–12). Food continues in a new form. Read with Deuteronomy 8:3, 7–10, this is a transition in provision, not independence from the giver; Joshua does not identify who grew or supplied the grain. Preserved clothing and feet, manna, and water from flint accompany the remembered hardships (3–4, 15–16). Provision continues in the land through the power to acquire wealth (18), beyond the exceptional wilderness food. Deuteronomy 11:1–17: Yahweh's eyes on the land all year (12). Deuteronomy 29: Clothing and shoes endure forty years, and life without bread, wine, or strong drink teaches dependence on Yahweh (5–6). The point is sustained provision; the passage does not say Israel ate nothing.  Deuteronomy 33: Heaven, deep, sun, moon, hills, earth, and dew supply the picture of Joseph’s fertility and Israel’s security (13–16, 28). Blessing embraces ordinary provision as well as victory.  Joshua 14:1–5: The land is distributed by lot as Yahweh commanded (2), while named leaders administer it (1). Read beside Proverbs 16:33, this joins divine direction with human responsibility; Joshua supplies no description of the physical lot procedure. Joshua 14:6–15: Caleb attributes forty-five years of survival to Yahweh’s keeping him alive as promised (10). He treats preserved life as capacity for the remaining task, not merely as an occasion for retirement.  Joshua 18:1–10: Joshua casts lots before Yahweh only after the men have surveyed the land by cities and recorded seven portions (6–10). Divine allocation and human preparation work together; the physical lot mechanism remains unspecified. Joshua 18:11–28: Benjamin’s lot comes up between Judah and Joseph (11), giving a concrete result of the lots cast before Yahweh in 18:10. The passage does not describe the physical mechanism or infer equal acreage. Joshua 19:1–23: The second, third, and fourth lots continue the Shiloh distribution (1, 10, 17; compare 18:10). Allocation by lot coexists with the practical explanation that Judah’s portion was too large (9); neither equal acreage nor the lot mechanism is specified.  Joshua 19:49–51: The summary attributes tribal distribution to the lot before Yahweh (51). Joshua’s own grant is described as a requested city given by command (50); the text does not separately narrate a lot selecting his city. | [[A Wife for Isaac]] · [[The Binding of Isaac]] · [[Joseph | [[Redemption]] | Getting back by right or by price what has passed out of your hands — *gāʾal*, the kinsman's act, first in Jacob's *"the angel who has redeemed me"* (Genesis 48:16), promised at Exodus 6:6 and sung as kept at 15:13; *pādâ*, the firstborn's ransom; and *qānâ*, Israel *"purchased"* as Joseph bought Egypt for Pharaoh; at Sinai, the deliverer claims the allegiance of those already rescued; a female servant's right to be redeemed; a negligent bull owner may pay an imposed ransom, unlike the prohibited payments in Numbers 35; Exodus 34: The donkey has an alternative; the son must be redeemed; Exodus 38: Fixed half-shekel payment counted and used (38:25–28); Proverbs 6:35: a ransom offered for adultery and refused; Proverbs 21: The wicked as ransom for the righteous, a reversal of places; Proverbs 23:11: the fatherless's Defender, *gōʾēl*; Leviticus 11: rescue from Egypt grounds belonging to Yahweh; Leviticus 19:19–37: The slave woman has not been ransomed or freed (20); those absent changes of status matter to the penalty.  Leviticus 25:1–22: Jubilee returns each person to property and family (10, 13).  Leviticus 25:23–34: A nearest kinsman redeems land sold through poverty (25). A recovered seller may settle by elapsed years, and Jubilee is the last remedy (26–28); Leviticus 25:47–55: *gāʾal* applied to a sold brother, at a price by years to Jubilee; Numbers 15:37–41: Yahweh brought Israel out of Egypt to be their God (41). Deliverance here grounds belonging and obedience; no payment or purchase mechanism is described.; Numbers 18: The firstborn redemption is fixed at five sanctuary shekels from a month old (15–16).  Numbers 20:14–21: The embassy summarizes the exodus as Yahweh hearing the cry, sending an angel, and bringing Israel out of Egypt (15–16). Rescue becomes the ground of an appeal for passage. Numbers 33: The departure on the day after Passover is public, while Egypt buries its firstborn (3–4). The journey begins with deliverance rather than with Israel’s conquest of Canaan. Numbers 35: Two ransoms are forbidden: payment for a murderer’s life and payment to return from refuge before the priest’s death (31–32). This restricts monetary release without canceling the distinct ransom provision for a negligent bull owner in Exodus 21:29–30. Numbers 36: The clan heads say Jubilee will not undo the tribal loss caused by heiresses marrying out (4). This qualifies any claim that Jubilee automatically restores every tribal boundary: Leviticus 25:13, 28 concerns return of property, whereas this petition concerns the holder's tribal affiliation. Deuteronomy 4:15–31: brought out of *the iron furnace* to be a people of inheritance (20). Deuteronomy 5: The identity of the lawgiver rests on bringing Israel out of bondage (6). Deuteronomy 7:1–11: Verse 8 explicitly calls the exodus redemption from bondage and Pharaoh’s hand. | [[Joseph and the Famine]] · [[Jacob Blesses Ephraim and Manasseh]] · [[I Am Yahweh]] · [[The Consecration of the Firstborn]] · [[The Song of the Sea]] · [[Israel at Mount Sinai]] · [[The Ten Commandments]] · [[The Altar and the Hebrew Slave]] · [[Laws of Violence and Injury]] · [[The Incense Altar, the Ransom, and the Holy Oil]] · [[The Covenant Renewed]] · [[The Altar, the Basin, the Court, and the Inventory]] (Exodus 38:1–31) · [[The Lamp of the Commandment]] · [[The King's Heart]] (Proverbs 21:18) · [[Thirty Excellent Things]] (Proverbs 23:11) · [[Clean and Unclean Animals]] (Leviticus 11:1–47) · [[Holy Living in Field and Community]] · [[Acceptable Victims and the Holy Name]]  · [[The Sabbatical Year and the Jubilee]]  · [[Redemption of Land and Houses]] · [[The Poor Brother and Redemption from Service]] · [[Vows, Valuations, and Devoted Things]] · [[The Levites and the Firstborn Counted]] · [[The Unclean Sent Out and Wrongs Repaid]] · [[The Tassels]] · [[The Duties and Portions of Priests and Levites]]  · [[Edom Refuses Passage]] · [[The Stages of the Journey]] · [[The Levitical Cities and the Cities of Refuge]] (Numbers 35:1–34) · [[The Marriage of the Daughters of Zelophehad]] · [[No Form Seen and No Image Made]] · [[Has Any People Heard God and Lived]] · [[The Ten Commandments Recounted]] · [[Do Not Forget When You Are Full]] (Deuteronomy 6:10–25) · [[A People Chosen in Love]] · [[Do Not Follow Other Gods]] · [[Release the Servant with Gifts]] · [[An Unsolved Killing and the Broken-Necked Heifer]] · [[Return Restoration and the Circumcised Heart]] |
| [[Remembrance]]  Joshua 5:10–12: The Passover continues in the new land (10). Its memorial meaning comes from Exodus 12; Joshua reports the observance without repeating the institution or its explanatory speeches. | Remembering the whole journey (2) and the giver of productive power (18) counters forgetting through abundance (11–14). Memory takes practical form in obedience and blessing God after eating. Deuteronomy 9:7–29: Israel must remember its provocations (7), while Moses asks Yahweh to remember Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob (27). Memory both humbles the people and supplies the intercessor’s appeal.  Deuteronomy 10:12–22: Israel’s former foreignness in Egypt becomes the reason to love foreigners (19). Deuteronomy 11:18–32: words laid up in heart and soul, bound, taught, and written (18–20). Deuteronomy 29: Egypt’s signs, forty years of preserved clothing, and the victories over Sihon and Og ground the present summons (2–9). Remembered experience does not itself guarantee understanding (4).  Deuteronomy 31: The defeat of Sihon and Og becomes a precedent for the future (4). Moses gives the whole people the remembered-victory argument previously addressed to Joshua in 3:21–22. Deuteronomy 31:9–13: Recurring public hearing gives children who have not known a way to learn (13). This complements household teaching in Deuteronomy 6:6–9; transmission is scheduled for the community as well as practiced at home.  Deuteronomy 31:14–23: The song will survive in descendants' mouths as a witness against Israel (19–21). Retaining a text in memory is distinguished from remaining faithful to the God it names: the people may preserve the testimony that accuses them.  Deuteronomy 32: Israel must ask fathers and elders about former generations (7), yet forgets the God who gave it birth (18). The song preserves the memory of care and betrayal; the closing instruction requires transmission to children in obedience (46). Joshua 4: The stones are a lasting memorial designed to provoke children’s questions (6–7, 21–23). Fathers must explain the deliverance; the object preserves an occasion for testimony rather than interpreting itself. Joshua 14:6–15: Caleb’s appeal makes the commission, failed report, and oath at Kadesh govern the present allocation at Gilgal (6–10). Remembering here creates a claim on present leadership. | [[The Waters Recede]] · [[The Covenant with Noah]] · [[Miserable Comforters and the Witness in Heaven]] · [[Bildad's Second Speech]] · [[I eyes saw, nor let it leave its heart, and must make it known to children and grandchildren (9–10). Peor and Horeb are both summoned as seen events (3, 9). Deuteronomy 4:15–31: Israel must not forget the covenant; Yahweh will not forget it (23, 31). Deuteronomy 11:1–17: the eyewitness generation is told to know what it saw. | Numbers 33: The journey is preserved as a written record attributed to Moses at Yahweh’s command (2). | [[Retribution]] | Suffering as payment, and circumstances as a readable index of standing with God — the doctrine the book of Job breaks as a diagnostic without denying; at Sinai, generational consequences and promised long life do not yield an infallible diagnosis; matching injury is a legal response, not a diagnosis of the victim’s guilt; five promises qualified by the sixth couplet's reproof (Proverbs 3:1–12); Proverbs 3:13–20: Wisdom's promised riches and peace held against Job's innocent losses.; Proverbs 3:21–35: Security and household blessing are promised; Job supplies explicit counterexamples; Proverbs 4:1–9: Promised preservation and honor contrasted with Job’s lost crown.; Proverbs 4:19: the wicked stumble in darkness, in tension with Job 24's unseen judgment; Proverbs 6:11, 15: sudden poverty and breaking without remedy; Proverbs 6:29–33: whoever touches is touched back; Proverbs 8:18–21: riches promised to Wisdom's lovers, against Job 1; Proverbs 8:35: life to Wisdom's finder, stated without exceptions, read after Job; Proverbs 10:2–3, 22, 24–30: The promises are categorical: provision for the righteous, realized fear for the wicked, life prolonged by fear of Yahweh, and secure dwelling; Proverbs 12:21 remains in explicit tension with Job’s undeserved suffering; Proverbs 19: Consequences and promised safety remain alongside the upright poor and Job’s counterexample; Proverbs 20: Expected outcomes remain in tension with undeserved losses; Proverbs 21: Expected outcomes, measure for measure, held beside Job; Proverbs 22: Sowing and reaping, kept beside Eliphaz's misuse of the image; Proverbs 24:12, 16: each rendered according to his work; the righteous fall seven times and rise; Proverbs 24:29: rendering according to work forbidden to the wronged man; Proverbs 26:27: the pit and the stone that return on their makers; Proverbs 28: The trap catches its maker, exploitative wealth passes to someone who pities the poor, and generosity is answered with sufficiency; Proverbs 30: Reproof and guilt; the shield promise held beside Job’s harm; Proverbs 30:17, 33: the mocker's eye; pressed wrath yields strife; Proverbs 31:11, 21, 25, 28–31: The praised household is an ideal, not a diagnosis of a sufferer’s moral standing; Ecclesiastes 2:26: transfer to the person pleasing God, with Job’s counterexample retained; Ecclesiastes 3:16–22: Divine judgment affirmed without reading death as a moral verdict; Ecclesiastes 4:1–3: Oppressors retain power; their victims are not accused of guilt; Ecclesiastes 8:1–9: Safety and failed wickedness beside observed misery and harmful rule; Ecclesiastes 9:1–12: Common death cannot identify moral standing; Ecclesiastes 10:8: the pit as a hazard of work, *may* where Proverbs has *shall*; Ecclesiastes 12:9–14: Universal judgment affirmed without explaining present reversals.; Leviticus 24:10–23: The corresponding-injury rule assigns a judicial consequence to a specified offense (19–20).; Leviticus 26: collective outcomes for a people, not a rule for one person Deuteronomy 28: conditional blessing in the land.  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: The sanctions explicitly follow national disobedience (15, 45), but their symptoms cannot prove individual guilt. Job 2:3 affirms blamelessness amid ruin, and 2:7 describes a similarly extensive affliction; the tension rules out reversing the conditional. | [[The Surety, the Sluggard, and the Seven Abominations]]
