| [[Righteousness]] | Being in the right — a verdict before it is a virtue; *ṣaddîq* first at 6:9, declared of a man before he is shown doing anything, and at 15:6 *credited* by an accounting verb to a man for believing; the innocent in court; Proverbs 3:21–35: Friendship and blessing accompany uprightness; Job limits inference from prosperity.; Proverbs 4:18: the path of the righteous shining more and more; Proverbs 8:8, 18, 20: Wisdom's words and road in righteousness; *ṣəḏāqâ* among her gifts; Proverbs 10:2–3, 6–7, 11, 16, 20–21, 24–32: Righteousness is associated with life, beneficial speech, granted desire, joy, and lasting security.; Proverbs 20: Righteous conduct remains recognizable beside the purity question; Proverbs 21: Justice done with joy, pursued, and generous; Proverbs 24:16: the righteous man falls seven times and rises; Proverbs 24:24: *"You are righteous"* said to the wicked; Proverbs 28: The righteous are bold and their ascendancy brings public flourishing; wicked rule drives people into hiding; Proverbs 31:9: Righteous judgment means serving the poor person’s just cause; Proverbs 31:12, 20, 26, 30–31: Doing good and caring for the needy give an ethical shape to praise; Ecclesiastes 3:16–22: The righteous and wicked remain distinguishable before judgment; Ecclesiastes 5:8–20: Righteousness violated by oppression, not disproved by poverty; Ecclesiastes 9:1–12: The righteous share the event of the wicked Leviticus 19:1–18: Judging the neighbor in righteousness excludes partiality both to the poor and to the great (15). Material provision for the poor (9–10) and impartial adjudication are complementary duties.; Leviticus 19:19–37: Justice applies to measures of length, weight, and quantity as well as judgment (35–36).  Numbers 22:36–23:26: The wish for the death of the righteous (23:10) accompanies the declaration of Israel’s blessing. Deuteronomy 4:1–14: The statutes and ordinances are called righteous beyond those of any great nation (8). The claim is about the law's character; the verse does not call Israel righteous. Deuteronomy 9:1–6: Verses 4–6 repeatedly deny Israel’s righteousness as the reason for possession. Explicit tension: Deuteronomy 6:25 calls obedient conduct righteousness, whereas 9:4–6 denies that the people’s righteousness explains the land gift. These concern conduct and the ground of possession respectively; neither assertion cancels the other. Deuteronomy 9:7–29: The recollection of rebellion supplies the concrete evidence for the denial of merit in 9:4–6. Moses’ plea names the people’s wickedness rather than claiming their righteousness (27). | [[Noah and the Flood]] · [[Eliphaz's Second Speech]] · [[Miserable Comforters and the Witness in Heaven]] · | [[Sabbath]] | The seventh day, on which God rested; the first thing in Scripture called holy; at Sinai, creation-grounded rest required for the whole household; six years of service and release in the seventh; rest for the field and refreshment for dependents; Israel's perpetual covenant sign, with death sanction and divine refreshment (31:12–17); Exodus 34: Rest required even in plowing and harvest; Exodus 35: Rest precedes the public sanctuary commission; kindling fire is forbidden Leviticus 19:1–18: Keeping Yahweh’s Sabbaths stands beside respect for mother and father (3). The address to the whole congregation brings sacred time into the same instruction as everyday treatment of neighbors.; Leviticus 19:19–37: Keeping Yahweh’s Sabbaths is paired with reverence for his sanctuary (30). Leviticus 23:1–8: Weekly holy convocation distinguished from annual assembly days. Leviticus 23:9–22: Seven Sabbaths structure the count; the precise starting referent remains unresolved.; Leviticus 23:23–32: Evening-to-evening annual rest  Leviticus 24:1–9: Bread is arranged before Yahweh every Sabbath (8). This stands beside the prohibition of work in 23:3: sanctuary service is commanded on the rest day. Any synthesis making Sabbath an end to every activity must retain both instructions rather than suppress the explicit weekly duty.  Leviticus 25:1–22: The six-and-seven pattern governs years of cultivation; seven such cycles lead to a holy fiftieth year (1–12).; Leviticus 26:2 repeats 19:30 before the promises; in Leviticus 26 the land collects its unkept Sabbaths while Israel is in exile; Numbers 15:32–36: death sanction enacted; contrast with manna rebuke retained Deuteronomy 5: The explicit rationale here is remembered slavery and rescue (15), and servants must rest as the addressee does (14). Deuteronomy 31:9–13: The seven-year release cycle provides the time for public instruction at the feast of tents (10). This passage prescribes neither weekly rest nor a new fallow rule; it adds hearing the law to the year of release already described in Deuteronomy 15. | [[The Creation of the World]] · [[Let the Day Perish]] · [[Bricks Without Straw]] · [[The Passover]] · [[The Consecration of the Firstborn]] · [[Manna and Quail]] · [[The Ten Commandments]] · [[The Altar and the Hebrew Slave]] · [[Justice, Sabbaths, and Feasts]] · [[Bezalel, Oholiab, and the Sabbath Sign]] · [[The Covenant Renewed]] · [[The Freewill Offering]] · [[The Work Completed]] (Exodus 39:32–43)  · [[Be Holy and Love Your Neighbor]] · [[Holy Living in Field and Community]] · [[Sabbath, Passover, and Unleavened Bread]] · [[First Fruits and the Feast of Weeks]] · [[Trumpets and the Day of Atonement]] · [[The Feast of Booths]] (Leviticus 23:33–44)  · [[The Lamps and the Bread Before Yahweh]]  · [[The Sabbatical Year and the Jubilee]] · [[Blessings for Obedience (Leviticus)]] · [[Covenant Warnings and Remembered Promises]] · [[The Sabbath Breaker]] (Numbers 15:32–36) · [[Daily, Sabbath, and New Moon Offerings]] · [[The Offerings of the Appointed Feasts]] · [[The Ten Commandments Recounted]] · [[The Year of Release and the Open Hand]] · [[Plucking a Neighbor's Grain - Deuteronomy 23.24-25 and the Sabbath Grain Fields]] · [[Read the Law to All Israel]] · [[The Eastern Tribes Pledge to Cross]] |
| [[Sacrifice and Offering]] | Gifts brought to God with no command, altar, or rule given, and received unequally for reasons withheld; the altar law: earth or uncut stone, no steps, burnt and peace offerings; feasts, first fruits, and limits; covenant sacrifices offered by young men before the priesthood; A portable crafted altar is prescribed; its relation to the earth-or-stone law remains open; sin offering, burnt offering, ram of consecration, and the continual burnt offering; The feast at the calf includes burnt offerings and peace offerings (32:6), categories already used at the covenant ratification (24:5); Exodus 34: Offerings required, rival sacrificial meals refused; Exodus 35: Voluntary materials and spun yarn supply the sanctuary; Freewill contributions cease when the work is fully supplied; Exodus 37: Table vessels, incense altar, and incense prepare for later service.; Exodus 38: Altar made; contributed metals distinguished from the fixed census payment.; Proverbs 21: Justice ranked above sacrifice; the wicked man's offering refused; Ecclesiastes 5:1: drawing near to listen better than the sacrifice of fools; Ecclesiastes 9:1–12: Sacrificer and nonsacrificer share one event; Song of Solomon 4:7: *mûm*, the offering's *defect*, used in praise of a body  Leviticus 1: The burnt offering has three forms: cattle, sheep or goats, and turtledoves or young pigeons.; Leviticus 2: Grain, oil, and prescribed preparations are accepted offerings (2:1–10). A memorial is burned and the remainder belongs to the priests. This prevents treating all acceptable offerings as animal victims or wholly burned gifts.; Leviticus 3: The peace offering permits male or female cattle and flock animals without defect (3:1, 6); Leviticus 4:1–21: The priest and congregation each bring a bull; the elders represent the assembly in hand-laying (15). · [[The Sin Offering for Priest and Congregation]]; Leviticus 4:22–35: Male goat for ruler; female goat or lamb for individual.  Leviticus 5:1–13: A female flock animal, two birds with different functions, or fine flour serve the stated cases according to means (6–13).  Leviticus 6:1–7: The offender brings a ram without defect, according to valuation, as a trespass offering (6). No cheaper alternative, slaughter procedure, or blood rite is specified in this unit; the offering does not replace repayment.  Leviticus 6:8–23: Maintained fire, restricted holy eating, and wholly burned priestly grain. Leviticus 6:24–7:10: Sin and trespass offerings provide holy food, except that sin offerings whose blood enters the tent for atonement must be burned (6:30). Burnt-offering skins belong to the priest (7:8). Grain allocations in 7:9–10 retain the wholly burned priestly-offering exception of 6:23.; Leviticus 7:11–21: Peace-offering eating governed by time, purity, and sacred ownership  Leviticus 7:22–38: The donor personally brings fat and breast; the fat burns, the breast goes to Aaron and his sons, and the right thigh to the officiant (30–34). Leviticus 8: Bull, burnt ram, and consecration ram have distinct treatments.; Leviticus 9: inaugural offerings, blessing, and public manifestation; Leviticus 10:12–20: eighth-day offerings completed; the goat burned rather than eaten; Leviticus 12: After the purification days, the mother brings a year-old lamb for a burnt offering and.; Leviticus 14:1–32: restoration after healing ; Leviticus 14:33–57: house inspection, repair, and cleansing  Leviticus 15: standard bird offerings for prolonged discharges;; Leviticus 17: Livestock slaughter is brought to the Tent of Meeting as peace offerings (3–6); this redirects field sacrifices to Yahweh and excludes goat idols (7). Leviticus 19:1–18: Peace offerings may be eaten on the offering day and the next; third-day remains are burned (5–8). A tension in scope remains: this general wording gives the window of the vow/freewill case in 7:16–18, while thanksgiving in 7:15 has a same-day deadline. Chapter 19 does not explicitly repeal that distinction.; Leviticus 19:19–37: The man in the slave-woman case brings a ram as a trespass offering to the Tent of Meeting (21–22).; Leviticus 21: Priests offer the bread of God and offerings made by fire (6, 8, 21). Leviticus 23:1–8: Seven days of fire offerings, without an inventory here. Leviticus 23:9–22: Distinct offerings accompany sheaf and loaves; leavened bread is assigned to the priest.; Leviticus 23:23–32: Fire offerings on both days  Leviticus 24:1–9: Frankincense serves as the bread’s memorial fire offering (7), while the bread itself is assigned for priestly eating (9). The fire-offering classification therefore does not mean that all twelve cakes are burned.; Numbers 6: offerings for a broken vow and for a finished one; Numbers 7: twelve equal gifts for the altar's dedication; Numbers 15: flour, oil, and wine required with every animal; Numbers 18: Offerings sustain priestly households, with different eligibility for different portions (8–19).  Numbers 19: The heifer is burned outside the camp and its ashes stored for purification.  Numbers 22:36–23:26: Seven altars, each with a bull and ram, are prepared at each of two stations (23:1–2, 14).  Numbers 23:27–24:25: Seven altars with a bull and ram each are prepared again at Peor (23:29–30). The repeated expenditure does not reverse Yahweh’s pleasure in blessing Israel (24:1). Deuteronomy 12:1–28: Offerings only at the chosen place; ordinary slaughter released to the towns (13–15).  Deuteronomy 27: At Ebal an altar of uncut stones untouched by iron receives burnt and peace offerings (5–7); eating and rejoicing accompany the latter.  Deuteronomy 33: Levi offers incense and burnt offerings (10); Zebulun and Issachar summon peoples to offer righteous sacrifices at an unnamed mountain (19). The poem does not identify that mountain with a particular sanctuary.  Joshua 8:30–35: altar and inclusive public reading.  Joshua 13:8–33: Offerings made by fire provide Levi’s inheritance (14), framing sacrifice as support alongside the divine-inheritance statement in verse 33. No new sacrificial ceremony is narrated. | [[Cain and Abel]] · [[The Waters Recede]] · [[The Covenant with Noah]] · [[Job of Uz]] · [[The Call of Abram]] · [[Abram and Lot Separate]] · [[The Rescue of Lot and Melchizedek]] · [[The Covenant Between the Pieces]] · [[The Binding of Isaac]] · [[Isaac in Gerar]] · [[Jacob's Ladder at Bethel]] · [[Jacob's Flight from Laban]] · [[Return to Bethel; | [[Seeing and Being Seen]] | Sight as the Bible's most morally loaded sense, and its passive side — being looked at, or overlooked — where God's seeing is never observation but the move before a rescue; the only divine name a human being ever gives is *ʾĒl Rŏʾî*, *a God who sees*; *"They saw the God of Israel"* — only the pavement under his feet described — and lived; Exodus 33: Face-to-face speech and the face that cannot be seen; Exodus 34:29–35: Israel sees Moses shining, not the divine face withheld in 33:20.; Proverbs 4:25: eyes fixed straight ahead; Proverbs 6:25: the eyelids that capture, the beauty coveted in the heart; Proverbs 20: A judging gaze, created senses, and inward illumination; Proverbs 23:5–6: eyes set on what is not; the evil eye against 22:9's good eye; Proverbs 30: Eyes express moral estimation and social posture: supposed purity in one’s own eyes is followed by haughty eyes and lifted eyelids; Ecclesiastes 1:1–11: Seeing does not finally satisfy the eye; hearing does not fill the ear; Song of Solomon 1:15–16: *"Behold, you are beautiful"*, said and returned; Song 3:11: *"Go out … and see king Solomon"*; Song 5:10–16: the woman describes his body to others, without his presence established; Song 6:4–10: Eyes that overcome and onlookers who praise; Song 6:11–7:9: Return so that we may gaze; Song 8:8–12: family plans, the woman’s answer, and her own vineyard; Numbers 15:37–41: Israel must look at the fringe rather than follow its own eyes (39). Sight is capable of serving obedience as well as leading astray: the passage redirects attention rather than condemning the faculty of sight.  Numbers 21:4–9: Here the endangered person must look at the object God appoints (8–9). Sight is associated with life, not because every image heals but because this act answers a particular promise. Numbers 22:22–35: The donkey sees the angel three times before Balaam sees him.  Numbers 22:36–23:26: Balak changes the viewpoint after the first blessing (23:13–14).  Numbers 23:27–24:25: The man who failed to see the angel in Numbers 22 now sees the ordered camp and speaks of open eyes and divine vision (24:2–4, 15–17). This is narrative irony and dependence on revelation, not proof of an independently reliable seer. Deuteronomy 32:48–52: Moses may see the land from a distance but may not enter it (49, 52). The permitted sight restates Deuteronomy 3:27 and Numbers 27:12 without reversing the sentence. | [[The Creation of the World]] · [[The Fall]] · [[Abram and Lot Separate]] · [[Hagar and Ishmael]] · Refuse the Land]] · [[The Tassels]]  · [[The Brass Serpent]]  · [[Balaam's Donkey]]  · [[Balaam's First Two Oracles]]  · [[Balaam's Last Oracles]] · [[Joshua Commissioned]] Deuteronomy 4:1–14: Israel's eyes saw the judgment at Peor and the events of Horeb (3, 9), but at Horeb they saw no form (12). Sight confirms the event; hearing carries the revelation. Deuteronomy 4:15–31: what was not seen may not be made (15); exile's gods are seen but cannot see (28). Deuteronomy 11:1–17: Israel's eyes have seen; Yahweh's eyes are on the land. | Deuteronomy 3:23–29: Moses asks to cross and see, but is commanded to look from Pisgah without crossing (25–27). Sight grants a prospect of the promised land without granting possession. · [[Moses Pleads to Cross the Jordan]] · [[Hear the Statutes and Remember Horeb]] · [[No Form Seen and No Image Made]] · [[Remember His Deeds and Guard Your Hearts]]  · [[Moses Commanded to Ascend Nebo]] · [[The Death of Moses]] |
| [[Sexual Sin]] | Genesis narrates a great deal of illicit sex and legislates none of it — no commandment, no prohibition, and almost no verdict; the wrongness is assumed by the characters, usually by foreigners, and **stated out loud for the first time by a Hebrew slave who is refusing**; at Sinai, adultery and coveting a neighbor’s wife are expressly forbidden; the strange woman of Proverbs 2:16–19, whose adultery forgets *"the covenant of her God"*; Proverbs 6:24–35: the neighbour's wife named, coveting her beauty forbidden, the act as fire, and no ransom accepted; Proverbs 7: the seduction told as a scene, a wife in a prostitute's dress; Proverbs 22: The adulteress's mouth a deep pit; Proverbs 30:19–20: the adulteress wipes her mouth and denies wrong; Proverbs 31:3: The warning concerns destructive expenditure of royal strength without naming a specific offense; Leviticus 18: The chapter prohibits specified kinship unions (6–18), intercourse during menstrual impurity (19), adultery (20), male same-sex intercourse (22), and intercourse with animals (23).; Leviticus 19:19–37: Intercourse with the pledged slave woman incurs punishment and requires the man’s ram and priestly atonement (20–22).; Leviticus 20: Adultery and several prohibited unions carry death (10–16), while other cases name cutting off, bearing iniquity, or childlessness (17–21).; Leviticus 21: The priest’s daughter who prostitutes herself is said to profane her father and is sentenced to burning (9). Deuteronomy 5: The prohibition of adultery (18) stands beside the prohibition of coveting a neighbor’s wife (21).  Deuteronomy 27:11–26: The curses name intercourse with a father’s wife, an animal, a sister, and a mother-in-law (20–23). They restate selected sexual boundaries as communal sanctions, without supplying a new trial procedure. | [[Abram in Egypt]] · [[Lot and His Daughters]] · [[Abraham and Abimelech]] · [[Isaac in Gerar]] · [[Dinah and the Massacre at Shechem]] · [[Return to Bethel; the Deaths of Rachel and Isaac]] · [[Judah and Tamar]] · [[Joseph in Potiphar's House]] · [[Oh That I Knew Where I Might Find Him]] · [[Job's Oath of Innocence]] · [[The Ten Commandments]] · [[Laws of Holiness and Compassion]] · [[If You Seek Her as Silver]] · [[The Strange Woman and the Wife of Your Youth]] · [[The Lamp of the Commandment]] · [[The Young Man Void of Understanding]] · [[The Two Feasts]] · [[A Good Name]] (Proverbs 22:14) · [[Do Not Envy Sinners]] · [[Where There Is No Vision]] (Proverbs 29:3) · [[Four Things]] (Proverbs 30:19–20) · [[The Words of King Lemuel]] · [[Righteousness and Wisdom Under Question]] (Ecclesiastes 7:26) · [[Forbidden Unions and the Defiled Land]] · [[Holy Living in Field and Community]] · [[Penalties and a People Set Apart]] · [[The Holiness of the Priests]] · [[The Suspected Wife]] · [[The People Refuse the Land]] · [[Baal Peor and the Zeal of Phinehas]] — Numbers 25: Sexual misconduct with Moabite women is joined to participation in their sacrifices (1–3). · [[The Ten Commandments Recounted]] · [[Marriage Accusations and Sexual Offenses]] · [[Fugitives, Interest, Vows, and a Neighbor's Produce]] · [[Divorce, Remarriage, and the Newlywed]]  · [[The People Answer Amen to the Curses]] |
| [[Signs and Wonders]] | Things God attaches a meaning to, and things beyond the possible — tokens of reassurance in Genesis, instruments of persuasion in Exodus; the signs at the bush are given because of an objection and are said to have a *voice*, and one of them can only be checked after obeying; Sabbath as an observed covenant sign (31:13, 17); Exodus 34: Unprecedented marvels promised as covenant renewal begins  Numbers 17: The overnight flowering and fruiting of Aaron’s rod confirms the announced choice (5, 8). Deuteronomy 7:12–26: The signs and wonders of Egypt are evidence that Israel need not fear the nations (19). Deuteronomy 11:1–17: the signs in Egypt recalled as chastisement the hearers saw.  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: The curses themselves become a sign and wonder to Israel and its offspring (46). Signs here expose covenant failure rather than guaranteeing rescue. Deuteronomy 29: The people saw the signs and wonders in Egypt (2–3), yet lack a knowing heart (4). This stands in tension with Deuteronomy 4:35, where showing has knowledge as its purpose: visible evidence and effective understanding are not treated as identical.  Joshua 3: The announced wonder supplies knowledge: Yahweh is with Joshua as with Moses (7), and the living God is among Israel (10). Prediction precedes the priests’ entry and the stopping of the water (13–17). Joshua 4: The stones become a sign of the stopped river (6–7). Returning water completes the event (18); its explanation extends knowledge of Yahweh’s power to future generations and all peoples (21–24). Joshua 10: The WEB reports the sun and moon standing still and the sun delaying its setting about a whole day (12–13). The narrator calls the day unparalleled because Yahweh listened and fought (14). No physical mechanism is explained. | [[The Burning Bush]] · [[Moses Returns to Egypt]] · [[Aaron's Staff Before Pharaoh]] · [[Water Turned to Blood]] · [[Frogs, Lice, and Flies]] · [[Livestock, Boils, and Hail]] · [[Locusts and Darkness]] · [[The Last Plague Announced]] · [[The Passover]] · [[The Consecration of the Firstborn]] · [[The Crossing of the Sea]] · [[The Song of the Sea]] · [[Bitter Water at Marah]] · [[Water from the Rock]] · [[The Battle with Amalek]] · [[Laws of Holiness and Compassion]] · [[Bezalel, Oholiab, and the Sabbath Sign]] · [[The Covenant Renewed]] · [[The People Refuse the Land]]  · [[Aaron's Rod Buds]] · [[Has Any People Heard God and Lived]] · [[Do Not Forget When You Are Full]] (Deuteronomy 6:10–25) · [[Blessing and the Removal of the Nations]] · [[Remember His Deeds and Guard Your Hearts]] · [[Do Not Follow Other Gods]] · [[First Fruits and the Tithe Declaration]]  · [[Curses for Disobedience]] · [[The Covenant in Moab and the Hidden Things]] · [[The Death of Moses]]  · [[Crossing the Jordan]] · [[The Twelve Stones from the Jordan]] · [[The Sun Stands Still at Gibeon]] |
| [[Sin]] | The first disobedience and its anatomy; the word itself first appears at 4:7, crouching at a door, and by 6:5 it is the *imagination of the heart*; at Sinai, the commands reach desire; the encounter is intended to prevent sin; the peoples' gods as a cause of sin; the iniquity of the holy things borne by Aaron; the priests' own sin offering, and atonement for the altar; The people rapidly turn from the commanded way (32:8); Exodus 33: The destroyed image leaves an unresolved resistant people; Exodus 34: The mediator acknowledges continuing sin beneath covenant renewal; Exodus 39: Manufacture does not enact the earlier provision for bearing iniquity.; Proverbs 4:16–17: evil as a compulsion that takes away sleep; Proverbs 8:36: sinning against Wisdom as violence to one's own soul; Proverbs 10:12, 16, 18–19, 23: Sin appears in the use of gain, lying and slander, excessive speech, and the fool’s pleasure in wrongdoing.; Proverbs 20: No confident claim to a self-purified heart; Proverbs 28: Concealing sins is contrasted with confessing and renouncing them (13); Proverbs 30: The unit names or depicts falsehood, denial of God, theft, slander, parental dishonor, self-deception, pride, and oppression; Ecclesiastes 3:16–22: Wickedness within the institutions meant to judge it; Ecclesiastes 4:1–3: Evil work associated with oppression, not blamed on the victims; Ecclesiastes 5:6: the mouth leading into sin, and an unpaid vow excused as a mistake; Ecclesiastes 8:1–9: Evil persistence warned against; wickedness unable to deliver; Ecclesiastes 9:1–12: Evil and madness in human hearts; Ecclesiastes 9:13–10:4: one sinner destroys much good; Leviticus 4:1–21: Unintentional violations of Yahweh’s prohibitions incur guilt (2, 13). · [[The Sin Offering for Priest and Congregation]]; Leviticus 4:22–35: Unwitting offenses of ruler and ordinary person.  Leviticus 5:1–13: Withheld testimony, impurity contact, and rash swearing are addressed together, without making every offense identical or every case inadvertent.  Leviticus 6:1–7: Fraud, robbery, oppression, dishonest handling of lost property, and false swearing are addressed as trespass against Yahweh (2–3). Unlike the preceding unknown-guilt case, these offenses involve deception; the offering laws cannot all be restricted to inadvertent acts. Leviticus 6:24–7:10: The offering for sin is called most holy (6:25, 29), with authorized priestly eating. It must not be described indiscriminately as unclean material: 6:30 distinguishes burning by the blood’s sanctuary destination.; Leviticus 7:11–21: Peace-offering eating governed by time, purity, and sacred ownership; Leviticus 10:12–20: priests bear the congregation's iniquity; Leviticus 12: The WEB calls one bird a sin offering (6, 8), yet the law neither identifies childbirth.; Leviticus 14:1–32: restoration after healing ; Leviticus 14:33–57: house inspection, repair, and cleansing  Leviticus 15: ritual uncleanness distinguished from moral fault;; Leviticus 17: The text assigns blood liability to prohibited slaughter (4), condemns sacrifice to goat idols as unfaithfulness (7), and assigns iniquity to neglect of cleansing after carcass eating (16).; Leviticus 18: Prohibited acts defile persons and land (24–30). Leviticus 19:1–18: Eating the profaned offering incurs iniquity (8), and the rebuke command warns against bearing sin because of the neighbor (17). The latter clause does not specify every circumstance in which silence or correction creates liability.; Leviticus 19:19–37: Verse 22 calls the man’s act sin and promises forgiveness after atonement.; Leviticus 20: Some cases specify bearing iniquity or sin (17, 19–20); the chapter supplies no sacrificial remedy.; Leviticus 24:10–23: Cursing God entails bearing sin (15), and blaspheming Yahweh’s name carries death (16).; Numbers 6: *ḥāṭāʾ* of a defilement no one intended; Numbers 15: error against the high hand; Numbers 15:32–36: adjacency to high-hand law does not disclose motive; Numbers 15:37–41: The warning against following heart and eyes addresses the direction of desire (39). The fringe is prescribed as a reminder to obey, not as a remedy that cancels an offense.  Numbers 16:1–40: The plea distinguishes one man’s sin from the whole congregation, but Moses warns others away from the rebels’ possessions lest they share their destruction (22–26).; Numbers 16:41–50: The renewed accusation follows the warning memorial of 16:40. No confession or repentance; Numbers 18: Bearing iniquity is attached to sanctuary and priestly responsibility (1, 23).  Numbers 21:4–9: The people identify their own speech against Yahweh and Moses as sin (7). Confession precedes intercession and rescue; no sacrificial rite is prescribed in this unit. Numbers 22:22–35: Balaam confesses sin after his eyes are opened, mentions his ignorance of the angel, and offers to return (34).  Numbers 22:36–23:26: The WEB’s denial of seen iniquity or perverseness in Israel (23:21) stands in tension with the rebellion and judgment narrated in Numbers 14.  Numbers 27:1–11: The daughters distinguish dying in one's own sin from belonging to Korah's company (3). They do not claim their father was innocent. Neither his particular sin nor the precise circumstances of his death are supplied, and his death does not disqualify their inheritance.  Deuteronomy 1:34–46: The people confess that they sinned and then disobey the prohibition against battle (41–43). An admission of earlier wrongdoing is followed by a fresh act of rebellion. Deuteronomy 9:1–6: The nations’ wickedness and Israel’s stubbornness are both named (4–6). The judgment on the former is not an acquittal of the latter; the following remembrance will expand Israel’s indictment. Deuteronomy 9:7–29: The calf is evidence of a continuing history of rebellion from Egypt onward (7, 24). Moses names stubbornness, wickedness, and sin in his prayer rather than pleading innocence (27).  Deuteronomy 27:11–26: Specific wrongs against worship, parents, neighbors, vulnerable people, and sexual boundaries culminate in failure to do the law (15–26). Communal assent is not a claim that every speaker has committed each listed act.  Deuteronomy 32:48–52: The WEB calls the act at Meribah trespass against Yahweh and failure to uphold his holiness (51). The verse gives a theological explanation of the offense without retelling the striking of the rock in Numbers 20:11. | [[The Fall]] · [[Cain and Abel]] · [[The Line of Cain and the Birth of Seth]] · [[The Sons of God and the Corruption of the Earth]] · [[Noah and the Flood]] · [[Eliphaz's Second Speech]] · [[Noah's Sons and the Curse of Canaan]] · [[The Tower of Babel]] · [[Bildad's Second Speech]] · [[Zophar's Second Speech]] · [[Abram and Lot Separate]] · [[Lot and His Daughters]] · [[Abraham and Abimelech]] · [[Return to Bethel; | [[Slavery and Servitude]] | One person owned by another, and the Bible's long unfinished reckoning with the fact that its own world ran on it; the founding narrative is Genesis 16, where a household solves a theological problem with a woman it owns and the text passes no verdict at all; at Sinai, former slaves must give servants rest, while household servitude continues; in the first ordinance, six years and release, a family divided, the ear at the doorpost, and a sold daughter protected; injury can bring freedom, while property status still determines other remedies; the servant’s son refreshed; serve their gods or serve Yahweh; Proverbs 12:24 warns against laziness without explaining every imposed servitude; Proverbs 19: Resistance to a servant ruling princes stands in tension with the wise servant’s elevation in 17:2; Proverbs 22: The borrower is servant to the lender; Proverbs 30: The servant protected from slander; scrutiny of the accuser; Proverbs 30:22–23: the servant king and the servant heir, in tension with 17:2 and Genesis; Proverbs 31:15: Servant girls receive portions within the household; Ecclesiastes 2:7: servants bought and house-born listed among a king's possessions; Ecclesiastes 10:7: servants on horses and princes on foot, an evil from a ruler's error; Leviticus 19:19–37: The woman in 20 is enslaved, pledged to another man, and neither ransomed nor freed.  Leviticus 25:1–22: Servants and hired workers share the land’s food (6), and Jubilee proclaims liberty and return (10); Leviticus 25:35–55: brothers as hired servants until Jubilee, foreign slaves forever; the bars of the yoke broken (Leviticus 26:13) Deuteronomy 5: Former bondage grounds equal Sabbath rest for male and female servants (14–15). Deuteronomy 7:1–11: Israel remembers the house of bondage and Pharaoh’s hand as the condition from which it was redeemed (8).  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: Failure to serve Yahweh joyfully gives way to serving enemies in deprivation under an iron yoke (47–48). Return to Egypt culminates in attempted self-sale without a buyer in the WEB (68), a reversal of liberation. · [[The Gibeonite Deception]] Joshua 9: The envoys initially call themselves servants (8–11), but after discovery Joshua imposes enduring slave service as wood cutters and water drawers for the congregation and altar (21–27). Protection from death coexists with coercive labor; survival is not unrestricted freedom. Joshua 10: Gibeon’s appeal identifies its people as Joshua’s servants (6), continuing the dependent status imposed in Joshua 9:23–27. That dependence now accompanies a plea for protection; the passage does not announce their release from service. Joshua 16: Gezer’s surviving Canaanites become servants doing forced labor (10). Their continued residence is accompanied by coercion, not an announcement of equal status or freedom. Unlike Joshua 9, this passage supplies no protective oath. | [[Noah's Sons and the Curse of Canaan]] · [[Abram in Egypt]] · [[The Rescue of Lot and Melchizedek]] · [[The Covenant Between the Pieces]] | [[The Spirit of God]] | *Rûaḥ* as God's: over the waters, in Joseph by Pharaoh's word, in Bezalel, and first shared out from Moses to seventy elders and beyond the plan to two in the camp, with Moses' wish that it were on all  Numbers 23:27–24:25: The narrator explicitly attributes Balaam’s oracle to the Spirit coming on him (24:2), after he stops seeking enchantments. No duration is stated, and the episode does not certify all of his conduct.| [[The Creation of the World]] · [[Bezalel, Oholiab, and the Sabbath Sign]] · [[The Seventy Elders and the Quail]]  · [[Balaam's Last Oracles]] · [[Joshua Commissioned]] · [[The Death of Moses]] |
| [[Strife and Peacemaking]] | Genesis narrates quarrels constantly and their resolution almost never; the founding case is Abram at 13:8–9, who names the kinship, takes the dispute on himself, and gives away a choice that was his twice over; quarrels create duties to victims and can injure bystanders; helping someone who hates you; Proverbs 3:13–20: Peace characterizes wisdom's paths; no particular quarrel is narrated.; Proverbs 3:21–35: Protect the neighbor’s trust; refuse baseless disputes and violent example; Proverbs 6:14, 19: the sower of discord, last of the seven abominations.; Proverbs 10:10, 12, 18: The wink causes sorrow, hatred provokes strife, and love covers wrongs; Proverbs 19: Discretion overlooks offense; habitual anger and household quarrels reproduce trouble; Proverbs 20: Honor in avoiding quarrels and refusing personal revenge; Proverbs 21: Domestic contention and anger pacified by a gift; Proverbs 22: Drive out the mocker and quarrels stop; Proverbs 22:24–25: the hot temper learned from company; Proverbs 26:17, 20–21: the meddler, and the quarrel that dies without wood; Proverbs 28: Greed stirs up strife (25), while those who keep the law contend with the wicked (4); Proverbs 30:33: churning, wringing, forcing — wrath produces strife; Ecclesiastes 3: Peace has its time beside war; Ecclesiastes 9:13–10:4: gentleness lays great offenses to rest Leviticus 19:1–18: Hatred in the heart, vengeance, and grudges are rejected, while rebuke remains an obligation (17–18). Peace therefore need not mean concealing a wrong or refusing correction.; Leviticus 24:10–23: A fight in the camp occasions the curse (10–11).  Numbers 20:14–21: Repeated negotiation fails to secure passage, yet Israel turns away rather than engaging Edom’s force (17–21). Avoiding combat here is distinct from achieving reconciliation.; Numbers 21:21–35: Israel offers limited transit without using fields, vineyards, or wells (22). Sihon refuses and attacks (23). Unlike the withdrawal before Edom in Numbers 20:21, this refusal leads to conquest. Deuteronomy 2:1–23: Commands not to contend with neighboring kin direct Israel toward payment and avoidance of battle (4–9, 19). The narrative does not report a reconciliation ceremony or claim that national hostility has permanently ended.  Deuteronomy 2:24–37: Moses sends words of peace and offers paid provisions after receiving a command to fight (24–29). The text retains this tension alongside divine hardening and Sihon’s refusal; it does not explain the embassy as either a sham or a reversal of the command. | [[The Surety, the Sluggard, and the Seven | [[Suffering]] | Moses explicitly interprets this wilderness experience as fatherly discipline directed toward good (2–5, 16). This does not erase Job’s different case: Job 2:3 describes his ruin as without cause. Discipline must not be made the explanation of every sufferer.  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: The threatened suffering includes bodily disease, loss of children, hunger, and unremitting fear (27–35, 53–67). Job 2:3 explicitly resists applying this explanation to every sufferer; similar bodily symptoms do not establish similar guilt. | [[Eliphaz's Second Speech]] · [[Miserable Comforters and the Witness | [[Testing]] | Yahweh tests Israel to disclose whether it will keep his commandments (2, 16). This divine testing differs from Israel testing Yahweh at Massah (6:16); the same participants do not imply the same moral direction.  Deuteronomy 33: Levi’s godly one is proved at Massah and contended with at Meribah (8). This differs in focus from Israel testing Yahweh in Exodus 17:7; the verse supplies no detailed reconciliation or personal identification. | [[The Binding of Isaac]] · [[Job of Uz]]  · [[Joseph in Potiphar's House]] · [[The Brothers' First Journey to Egypt]] · [[The Second Journey]] · [[The Silver Cup and Judah's Plea]] · [[The Wager in Heaven and the Four Messengers]] · [[The Second Test]] · [[The Arrows of the Almighty]] · [[Oh That I Knew Where I Might Find Him]] · [[Elihu on God's Justice]] · [[Elihu on God's Greatness]] · [[I Repent in Dust and Ashes]] · [[The Restoration of Job]] · [[Bitter Water at Marah]] · [[Manna and Quail]] · [[Water from the Rock]] · [[The Ten Commandments]] · [[A Dry Morsel with Quietness]] (Proverbs 17:3) · [[Do Not Boast About Tomorrow]] · [[From Dust to Dust]] (Ecclesiastes 3:16–22) · [[The People Refuse the Land]] · [[Do Not Forget When You Are Full]] (Deuteronomy 6:10–25) · [[Remember the Wilderness and the Giver of Wealth]] (Deuteronomy 8:1–20) · [[Do Not Follow Other Gods]]  · [[Moses Blesses the Tribes]] |
| [[Theft and Restitution]] | *Gānab*, to steal — a wrong to a neighbor and a debt; in Genesis an accusation, often disputed, applied to animals, household gods, a deceived heart, and a kidnapped man; at Sinai, a commandment and then its cases: multiplied restitution, sale for a thief who cannot pay, the night thief, and liability limited to what could be prevented; Proverbs 6:30–31: the hungry thief restores sevenfold, the lesser case set against adultery; Proverbs 20: Fraudulent gain condemned without a restitution tariff; Proverbs 22:22–23, 28; 23:10: robbing the poor and moving the boundary stone; Proverbs 28: Robbing one’s father or mother and denying the wrong makes a person a partner with a destroyer; Proverbs 30: Agur fears that poverty might lead him to steal and dishonor God’s name  Leviticus 6:1–7: Goods obtained through robbery, oppression, fraud over deposits, or dishonest retention of lost property must be restored in full with an added fifth (2–5). Tension with Exodus 22:1–9: that law requires multiplied repayment in theft and disputed-property cases. These texts state differing provisions; neither expressly explains how the penalties interact in an overlapping case. Leviticus 19:1–18: Stealing, oppression, robbery, and keeping wages overnight are prohibited (11–13). This unit states obligations but supplies no new repayment multiplier, leaving the earlier restitution laws distinct.; Leviticus 24:10–23: Killing an animal requires making the loss good (18, 21).  Deuteronomy 27:11–26: Removing a neighbor’s landmark is cursed (17), reinforcing the boundary protection of Deuteronomy 19:14. The curse gives no new restitution amount. | [[Jacob's Flocks]] · [[Jacob's Flight from Laban]] · [[The Cupbearer and the Baker]] · [[The Silver Cup and Judah's Plea]] · [[The Ten Commandments]] · [[Laws of Violence and Injury]] · [[Laws of Property and Restitution]] · [[The Lamp of the Commandment]] · [[A False Balance]] (Proverbs 11:1–31) · [[Wine Is a Mocker]] (Proverbs 20:10, 14, 17, 23) · [[Thirty Excellent Things]] (Proverbs 22:22–23, 28; 23:10) · [[The Wicked Flee When No One Pursues]] (Proverbs 28:24) · [[Where There Is No Vision]] (Proverbs 29:24) · [[The Words of Agur]] (Proverbs 30:9) · [[The Trespass Offering for Holy Things and Unknown Guilt]] (Leviticus 5:14–19); Leviticus 5:15–16 extends the restitution theme to unwitting wrong in holy things: repayment plus one fifth, given to the priest, alongside a ram offering. This is distinct from the multiplied theft penalties of Exodus 22 and is not explicitly called deliberate theft. Verses 17–19 do not repeat the repayment rule.  · [[Restitution for Fraud and False Oaths]]  · [[Be Holy and Love Your Neighbor]] · [[Eating the Holy Things]] · [[The Blasphemer and One Law for All]] · [[The Unclean Sent Out and Wrongs Repaid]] · [[Care for the Neighbor and Ordered Distinctions]] · [[Fugitives, Interest, Vows, and a Neighbor's Produce]] · [[Pledges, Wages, Justice, and Gleaning]]  · [[The People Answer Amen to the Curses]] |
| [[Tithing]] | A tenth, given twice in Scripture before anyone is commanded to give it — out of war spoil at 14:20 and as a vow at 28:22 — and then organized into a system supporting a landless tribe; Numbers 18: Israel’s tithe becomes the Levites’ inheritance in return for Tent service (21–24). Deuteronomy 12:1–28: Tithes eaten by the households at the chosen place, unlike Numbers 18:21–24 (17–18). | [[The Rescue of Lot and Melchizedek]] · [[Jacob's Ladder at Bethel]] · [[Vows, Valuations, and Devoted Things]] · [[The Duties and Portions of Priests and Levites]] · [[The Place Yahweh Will Choose]] · [[Tithes for Feasting and Provision]] · [[First Fruits and the Tithe Declaration]] |
| [[Vanity]] | The question of lasting gain, unsatisfied senses, passing generations, and lost remembrance in Ecclesiastes; Ecclesiastes 2:1–11: mirth, works, and wealth judged vanity, with no profit under the sun; Ecclesiastes 2:15, 17: wisdom's advantage judged vanity before the common end; Ecclesiastes 2:18–26: vanity surrounds enjoyment’s affirmation; Ecclesiastes 3:16–22: The verdict attaches to humanity’s lack of advantage in death; Ecclesiastes 4:4–8: envious labor and solitary accumulation judged vanity; Ecclesiastes 4:16: a successor's passing popularity; Ecclesiastes 5:7: vanities in many dreams and many words; Ecclesiastes 5:8–20: Unsatisfied love of silver and labor for the wind beside a present portion; Ecclesiastes 6:1–9: Possessions, birth, and desire under the vanity verdict; Ecclesiastes 6:10–12: Many words without profit and life spent like a shadow; Ecclesiastes 9:1–12: Joy within days of vanity; Ecclesiastes 11:7–12:8: Youth passes; the opening verdict returns at 12:8.; Ecclesiastes 12:9–14: The refrain remains beside truthful teaching, obedience, and judgment. | [[Vanity of Vanities]] · [[The Burden of Wisdom]] · [[The Trial of Pleasure]] · [[The Wise and the Fool Share One End]] · [[The Labor Left to Another]] (Ecclesiastes 2:18–26) · [[From Dust to Dust]] (Ecclesiastes 3:16–22) · [[Two Are Better Than One]] (Ecclesiastes 4:4–12) · [[The Youth and the Old King]] (Ecclesiastes 4:13–16) · [[Let Your Words Be Few]] · [[The Lover of Silver and the Gift of Enjoyment]] (Ecclesiastes 5:8–20) · [[Riches Without Enjoyment]] (Ecclesiastes 6:1–9) · [[Who Knows What Is Good]] (Ecclesiastes 6:10–12) · [[The House of Mourning]] (Ecclesiastes 7:6) · [[Righteousness and Wisdom Under Question]] (Ecclesiastes 7:15) · [[The Wicked Buried and the Work Beyond Finding]] — burial, forgetting, and reversed outcomes · [[One Event Comes to All]] · [[Remember Your Creator]] · [[The End of the Matter]] (Ecclesiastes 12:9–14) |
| [[Vengeance]] | Harm returned for harm — God's sevenfold protection of Cain, Lamech's seventy-sevenfold boast made out of it, and the one-for-one rule that cancels both; its largest Genesis case states no ratio at all and is answered only by a curse on the anger behind it; matching penalties restrain escalation without settling their historical application; Proverbs 6:34–35: a wronged husband's vengeance that no ransom buys off; Proverbs 20: Personal repayment refused in favor of Yahweh’s rescue; Proverbs 24:17–18: no rejoicing when an enemy falls; Proverbs 24:29: *"I will do to him as he has done to me"* forbidden Leviticus 19:1–18: Taking vengeance and bearing a grudge against one’s people are prohibited, and love of the neighbor is commanded (18). Private retaliation is rejected; the same passage still requires righteous judgment (15).; Leviticus 24:10–23: Matching injury is prescribed as a legal response (19–20), not a report of private revenge.; *the vengeance of the covenant* (Leviticus 26:25) falls on the people inside it Numbers 35: The avenger executes the murderer (19–21), but the congregation must judge and rescue an unwitting killer (24–25). Protection is geographically conditional until the high priest dies; outside the boundary the avenger incurs no bloodguilt for killing him (26–28). The law both authorizes and restricts retaliation.  Deuteronomy 4:41–43: The eastern refuges preserve the unwitting killer’s life (41–43). Unlike Numbers 35:12, this notice does not name the avenger; the connection to restrained retaliation comes from reading the two passages together.  Deuteronomy 32: Recompense belongs to Yahweh (35), who avenges servants’ blood (43). Divine vengeance is not permission for private revenge; Romans 12:19 uses the declaration to forbid believers’ revenge. That verse was checked in WEB at bible-api.com on 2026-11-10. Joshua 10: Verse 13 places the delayed sunset in the context of the nation avenging itself on its enemies. This is a narrated wartime victory under divine aid, not an unrestricted permission for personal revenge.  Joshua 20: The avenger’s pursuit encounters an explicit communal limit: the elders must receive and protect the unintentional killer (4–5). | [[Cain and Abel]] · [[The Line of Cain and the Birth of Seth]] · [[The Covenant with Noah]] · [[I Know That My Redeemer Lives]] · [[The Stolen Blessing]] · [[Dinah and the Massacre at Shechem]] · [[The Deaths of Jacob and Joseph]] · [[Job's Oath of Innocence]] · [[Moses Flees to Midian]] · [[The Battle with Amalek]] · [[Laws of Violence and Injury]] · [[The Lamp of the Commandment]] · [[A Dry Morsel with Quietness]] (Proverbs 17:13) · [[Wine Is a Mocker]] (Proverbs 20:22) · [[Through Wisdom a House Is Built]] · [[The Field of the Sluggard]] · [[The Glory of Kings]] (Proverbs 25:21–22)  · [[Be Holy and Love Your Neighbor]] · [[The Blasphemer and One Law for All]] · [[Covenant Warnings and Remembered Promises]] · [[The War Against Midian]] · [[The Levitical Cities and the Cities of Refuge]] (Numbers 35:1–34)  · [[Three Cities Beyond the Jordan]] · [[Refuge for the Unwitting Killer]] · [[Justice, Family Duty, Honest Weights, and Amalek]]  · [[The Song of Moses]] · [[The Sun Stands Still at Gibeon]]  · [[The Cities of Refuge Appointed]] |
| [[Walking with God]] | An idiom the Bible introduces without explaining and uses of two men only — Enoch and Noah, both exempted from a general death; *"he will make your paths straight"* (Proverbs 3:6); Proverbs 4:10–19: the way of wisdom against the path of the wicked; Proverbs 4:26–27: a level path, turning neither right nor left; Proverbs 6:22–23: the teaching leads the walker and lights the way of life; Proverbs 8:20: Wisdom walks the paths of justice; Proverbs 10:9, 17, 29: Blameless walking, the way of life, and the way of Yahweh describe ethical direction.; Israel walks, Yahweh walks among them, and he made them walk upright (Leviticus 26:3, 12–13) Deuteronomy 11:18–32: *"walk in all his ways"* (22) against turning *"out of the way"* (28). | [[From Adam to Noah]] · [[Noah and the Flood]] · [[Circumcision, the Sign of the Covenant]] · [[A Wife for Isaac]] · [[Jacob Blesses Ephraim and Manasseh]] · [[Oh That I Knew Where I Might Find Him]] · [[The Months of Old]] · [[The Offering for the Sanctuary]] · [[Do Not Consent to Sinners]] · [[If You Seek Her as Silver]] · [[Trust in Yahweh with All Your Heart]] · [[The Two Paths]] · [[Keep Your Heart]] · [[The Lamp of the Commandment]] · [[The Young Man Void of Understanding]] · [[Wisdom Calls at the Gates]] · [[A Wise Son Makes a Glad Father]] · [[A False Balance]] (Proverbs 11:1–31) · [[Whoever Loves Correction]] (Proverbs 12:15, 26, 28) · [[Hope Deferred]] (Proverbs 13:6, 15, 20) · [[A Way That Seems Right]] (Proverbs 14:2, 8, 12, 14–15) · [[A Gentle Answer]] (Proverbs 15:9–10, 19, 21, 24) · [[The Plans of the Heart]] (Proverbs 16:7, 9, 17, 25, 29) · [[Do Not Envy Sinners]] · [[Blessings for Obedience (Leviticus)]] · [[Covenant Warnings and Remembered Promises]] · [[The Cloud over the Tabernacle]] · [[The March from Sinai]] · [[The Defeat at Hormah]] · [[Set These Words Before You]] · [[The Law for the King]] · [[The Assembly and the Holy Camp]] · [[The Word Is Near and the Choice Is Life]] |
| [[War]] |   Numbers 33:50–56: Expulsion commanded; no battle or killing of every resident narrated here.  Deuteronomy 1:34–46: Weapons and willingness to fight cannot substitute for Yahweh’s presence (41–44). The Amorites pursue Israel like bees; no casualty count is supplied. Deuteronomy 2:1–23: Israel must not contend with the descendants of Esau or Lot (5, 9, 19). The asides recall other peoples’ violent displacement, so this is a restriction on these campaigns rather than a universal ban on warfare.  Deuteronomy 2:24–37: Battle at Jahaz leads to the destruction of every inhabited city, expressly including women and little ones, with no survivors reported (32–34). Livestock and goods become spoil (35). This is not a bloodless transfer or a claim limited to combatants; Ammon remains outside the permitted conquest (37).  Deuteronomy 3: Sixty fortified cities and many unwalled villages fall (4–5). The account explicitly includes the destruction of women and little ones while livestock and goods are retained as plunder (6–7); it cannot be reduced to combat against soldiers. Deuteronomy 3:12–22: Armed men cross while families and livestock stay in the granted cities (18–20). Yahweh’s fighting for Israel (22) accompanies commanded human participation rather than replacing it. Deuteronomy 7:1–11: The seven nations are to be utterly destroyed after Yahweh delivers them to Israel; neither covenant nor mercy is allowed (1–2). Deuteronomy 7:12–26: The hornet, confusion, and delivered kings; complete destruction, but *little by little* (16, 20–24). Deuteronomy 9:1–6: Divine advance and human action belong together: Yahweh brings the nations down and Israel drives them out (3). Explicit tension: 9:3 says they perish quickly, while 7:22 says removal is little by little. Decisive encounters within gradual occupation are a possible reading, not an explanation supplied by either verse. Deuteronomy 11:18–32: no one able to stand before Israel; the dread of Israel on the land (23, 25). Deuteronomy 28: conditional blessing in the land.  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: Defeat reverses 28:7, and a distant, unnamed nation besieges trusted walls without sparing old or young (25, 49–57). These are threatened horrors, not commands authorizing siege atrocities.  Deuteronomy 31: Yahweh promises to destroy the nations as he destroyed Sihon and Og; Israel must act according to the command already given (3–5). The reassurance concerns this entry into the land, not an unrestricted warrant for later wars. Deuteronomy 33: Help against adversaries, wild-ox horns, lion imagery, and the expulsion of enemies accompany the promise of safety (7, 11, 17, 20–22, 27–29). Protection and violent victory coexist in the blessing.  Joshua 2: Reconnaissance involves local testimony, concealment, pursuit, and escape before any siege (1–24). The promise to spare Rahab’s household complicates an undifferentiated picture of destruction; Joshua 2 establishes the undertaking without yet narrating its execution.  Joshua 3: The stopping of the river is announced as assurance of the future expulsion of seven peoples (10). The crossing itself contains no battle, and the assurance is not a report that those peoples have already been defeated. Joshua 4: About forty thousand eastern-tribe men cross ready and armed for battle (12–13). No battle occurs in this unit.  Joshua 5:13–15: The armed figure identifies himself as commander of Yahweh's army (13–14).  Joshua 6: The army circles behind a priestly signal and enters after the wall falls (3–20). Verse 21 explicitly includes men, women, young, old, and livestock in the killing; it cannot be summarized as combatants alone. Rahab’s household is the stated exception. Joshua 10:28–43: The six city accounts repeat capture and destruction, including inhabitants rather than only opposing soldiers (28–40). Verse 42 attributes victory to Yahweh. Lachish takes two days (32), so the campaign summary is not a claim that every battle occurred on one day.  Joshua 13:8–33: The register recalls the defeats of Sihon, Og, the Midianite chiefs, and Balaam (10–12, 21–22), but expressly records populations not driven out (13). Conquest summaries do not establish universal removal. Joshua 14:6–15: Caleb claims continuing strength for war and anticipates driving out the Anakim (11–12), yet verse 15 says the land had rest from war. Rest cannot mean that no local fighting remains; Joshua 15:14 will narrate the expulsion.  Joshua 15:13–19: Caleb expels the three sons of Anak and Othniel captures Debir (14–17). These local actions stand beside Joshua’s earlier comprehensive victories (10:36–39; 11:21–23) and the rest notice of 14:15. Their chronological relationship is not stated.  Joshua 17:14–18: Iron chariots and strong inhabitants explain the petitioners’ hesitation (16). Joshua answers with an assurance of expulsion (18), while 17:12–13 records non-removal. Promise and narrated outcome must remain distinct.  Joshua 18:1–10: The narrator says the land was subdued (1), yet Joshua immediately rebukes the seven tribes for failing to possess it (2–3). This preserves the tension of the conquest summaries with unfinished occupation; political or military subjection and completed settlement need not be identical. | Organized violence between peoples — introduced late, at Genesis 14:2, between nine kings none of whom is Israelite; Genesis reports Abram's night raid in one clause and spends four times as long on what he refuses to keep; the first city Israelites take is taken by two men, by treaty and surgery rather than battle; God fights ahead, little by little; Proverbs 20: Wise guidance required without control over the whole way; Proverbs 21: The wise besieger and the prepared horse; Proverbs 24:5–6: war waged by wise guidance; Ecclesiastes 3: War and peace close the poem’s opposing times; Ecclesiastes 8:1–9: No discharge, in a sequence describing limits of human power; Ecclesiastes 9:1–12: The battle is not to the strong; Ecclesiastes 9:13–10:4: siege and weapons of war ranked beneath wisdom; Song 3:7–8: sixty men *"expert in war"* escort a wedding against *"fear in the night"*; Song 6:4–10: Awesome as an army with banners; no sword in the land, and few chasing many (Leviticus 26:6–8); Numbers 1: the muster of those able to go out to war  Numbers 20:14–21: Edom threatens the sword and comes out in force; Israel withdraws (18–21). Military coercion succeeds without a battle being narrated.  Numbers 21:1–3: Captivity is followed by vowed destruction of people and cities.; Numbers 21:21–35: The request for transit becomes battle when Sihon attacks (21–24). Og also comes out to fight (33); Yahweh promises victory and the account ends with no survivors and possession of the land (34–35). These are lethal conquests, not merely diplomatic successes.  Numbers 22:36–23:26: The second oracle ends with Israel as a lion consuming prey and the blood of the slain (23:24).  Numbers 23:27–24:25: Blessing includes violent victory over adversaries (24:8, 17–20), not merely fertility and peace. The later sayings widen the horizon to captivity and the destruction of conquering powers. Numbers 26: The 601,730 are those eligible for war from twenty upward (2, 51), not the entire population. Levi uses another age threshold and another total. · [[The Eastern Inheritance and the Charge to Joshua]]  · [[Crossing the Jordan]] · [[The Gibeonite Deception]] Joshua 9: Most kings unite to fight (1–2); Gibeon seeks survival through a deceptive treaty. Deuteronomy 20:10–18 distinguishes distant cities from the land’s peoples, explaining why the claim of distance matters. The local Hivites survive under oath despite the destruction command. Joshua 10: The attack on Gibeon is provoked by its peace with Israel (4). Joshua’s forced night march and pursuit coexist with the decisive divine action: hail kills more than swords (9–11), and Yahweh fights for Israel (14). Joshua 12:1–24: Thirty-one kings counted; a king struck is not a city held (1–24). Joshua 13: The inventory of remaining territory follows the conquest summaries. Yahweh promises future removal of inhabitants (6), but no battle occurs here. Rest from war in 11:23 cannot be expanded into a claim that every listed region was permanently occupied.  · [[The Inheritance Beyond the Jordan]] | [[The Rescue of Lot and Melchizedek]] · [[The | [[Wealth and Possessions]] | Houses, livestock, silver, and gold are anticipated gifts, yet their increase can produce self-credit (12–18). Even the power to acquire wealth is given to establish the ancestral covenant; this is a communal covenant address, not a promise of riches to every faithful individual. Deuteronomy 28: conditional blessing in the land.  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: The resident foreigner rises and lends while Israel becomes dependent (43–44), reversing 28:12–13. This national sanction does not establish that each poor or indebted person is disobedient.  Deuteronomy 31:14–23: Eating, fullness, and growing fat in the gifted land precede turning to other gods (20). The earlier warning against forgetting the giver in Deuteronomy 8:10–20 becomes a prediction of betrayal; prosperity does not ensure loyalty.  Deuteronomy 32: Rich food and provision (13–14) lead into Jeshurun’s growing fat and abandoning the giver (15). This realizes the warning of Deuteronomy 8:10–20 within the poem: prosperity is a gift that can become an occasion of disloyalty.  Joshua 6: The city is burned, but precious and useful metals go to Yahweh’s treasury (19, 24). Soldiers are warned against taking devoted property (18); Rahab’s possessions are expressly rescued with her household (23, 25).| [[The Surety, the Sluggard, and the Seven Abominations]] · [[Abram in | [[The Wilderness]]  Joshua 5:10–12: Manna’s cessation closes the wilderness feeding era (12), matching the broad endpoint of Exodus 16:35. Joshua places that cessation after crossing, when produce is available at the Gilgal camp, not before the Jordan crossing. | The forty years combine hunger, danger, preservation, and provision (2–5, 15–16). Moses interprets the journey as humbling and testing for eventual good, not simply as a route to the land. Deuteronomy 9:7–29: Horeb, Taberah, Massah, Kibroth Hattaavah, and Kadesh become a remembered history of provocation (7–24). This supplements the wilderness as a place of provision and testing in Deuteronomy 8. Deuteronomy 11:18–32: the wilderness as one edge of the promised border (24). Deuteronomy 29: Forty years of preserved clothing and unusual provision become evidence for knowing Yahweh (5–6). The wilderness past grounds the covenant obligation now.  Deuteronomy 32: The desert is the scene of being found, guarded, and carried like an eagle’s young (10–12). The poem stresses tender care where earlier narratives also stressed testing and rebellion. It need not supply a separate wilderness itinerary. | [[Hagar and Ishmael]] · [[The Birth of Isaac and the Sending of Hagar]] · [[Manna and Quail]] · [[The Census in the Wilderness of Sinai]] · [[The Passover in the Second Year]] · [[The Cloud over the Tabernacle]] · [[The March from Sinai]] · [[The Fire at Taberah]] · [[The Seventy Elders and the Quail]] · [[Miriam and Aaron Against Moses]] · [[The Twelve Spies]] · [[The People Refuse the Land]] · [[The Sabbath Breaker]] (Numbers 15:32–36)  · [[The Rebellion of Korah]] · [[Aaron Stands Between the Dead and the Living]]  · [[Aaron's Rod Buds]]  · [[The Waters of Meribah]]  · [[The Brass Serpent]] · [[The Journey to Moab]]  · [[Balaam's Last Oracles]]  · [[The Second Census]] Deuteronomy 11:1–17: what Yahweh did in the wilderness counted among the seen deeds (5). | Numbers 33: A long sequence of named camps links liberation to the border of the land. Most receive no event or duration; the list cannot supply a precise modern route by itself. · [[The Stages of the Journey]] · [[The Words of Moses Beyond the Jordan]] (Deuteronomy 1:1–5)  · [[The Sentence and the Presumptuous Ascent]] (Deuteronomy 1:34–46) · [[Around Seir and Past Moab and Ammon]] · [[Remember the Wilderness and the Giver of Wealth]] (Deuteronomy 8:1–20) · [[Remember the Calf and Moses' Intercession]] · [[Remember His Deeds and Guard Your Hearts]] · [[Set These Words Before You]] · [[The Covenant in Moab and the Hidden Things]]  · [[The Song of Moses]] · [[The Second Circumcision at Gilgal]] — Joshua 5:1–9: The journey is remembered both as forty years of judgment and as the period in which children grew up without circumcision (4–7). The text reports the omission without explaining its cause.  · [[Passover at Gilgal and the End of the Manna]] |
| [[Wisdom]] | Skill, then skill at living; an international and observational tradition, and in Job the tradition auditing itself until nobody in the book is qualified to speak; the wise-hearted makers filled with *"the spirit of wisdom"*; God gives wisdom to Bezalel and the wider company (31:3–6); Exodus 35: Wise-hearted women make skill visible in spinning; God-given skill includes teaching and is distributed among the makers; Exodus 36:8–38: the wise-hearted company makes its competence visible in construction; Exodus 37: The previously gifted skill becomes measured objects and compounded materials.; Exodus 38: Oholiab’s practical skills named beside credited work (38:23); Exodus 39: Skill takes material form in metalwork, stone engraving, and textiles.; in Proverbs 2, sought as silver and given by Yahweh, against Job 28's failed search; Proverbs 3: not leaning on one's own understanding; not wise in one's own eyes; Proverbs 3:13–20: Wisdom's worth, the tree of life, and God's founding of the earth.; Proverbs 3:21–35: Discretion protects life and takes concrete form in dealings with a neighbor; Proverbs 4:1–9: Received teaching, wholehearted pursuit, protection, and honor.; Proverbs 4:10–19: wisdom as a road, instruction held as life; Proverbs 4:20–27: the father's words kept in the heart; Proverbs 6:6: wisdom learned by considering the ant.; Proverbs 8:1–21: Wisdom at the gates, straight words, *"By me kings reign"*; Proverbs 8:22–36: Wisdom possessed at the beginning, present at creation, delighting in humanity; whoever finds her finds life; Proverbs 10:1, 5, 8, 13–14, 19–23, 31: Wisdom becomes observable in timely work, accepting commands, storing knowledge, and speaking with restraint; Proverbs 12 makes loving correction and listening to counsel tests of wisdom; Proverbs 19: Knowledge governs zeal, anger, household prudence, and lifelong reception of counsel; Proverbs 20: Sobriety, discernment, counsel, and the limits of understanding; Proverbs 21: Instruction, stored treasure, strategy, and wisdom's limit; Proverbs 22: The prudent sees danger; Yahweh watches over knowledge; Proverbs 22:17–21: the words of the wise, kept, spoken, and reported truly; Proverbs 24:3–4: a house built through wisdom, understanding, and knowledge; Proverbs 24:32: instruction received by looking; Proverbs 26:4–5: two true sayings that require judgment; Proverbs 28: Understanding sustains public order and recognizes justice; the law-keeping son is wise; Proverbs 30: Agur’s limited understanding and reliance on God’s words; Proverbs 30:15–33: ways too wonderful to know; four small creatures exceedingly wise; Proverbs 31:1–9: Maternal teaching makes royal restraint answerable to public justice; Proverbs 31:10–31: Wisdom embodied in skilled work, provision, generous hands, and faithful teaching; Ecclesiastes 1:1–11: A royal inquiry opens by questioning gain, satisfaction, and memory; Ecclesiastes 2:3, 9: wisdom guides the trial of pleasure and remains; Ecclesiastes 2:13–16: wisdom excels folly as light excels darkness, yet the wise man dies like the fool; Ecclesiastes 2:19–26: wisdom cannot secure succession, yet remains God’s gift; Ecclesiastes 3: Understanding cannot encompass God’s whole work; Ecclesiastes 3:16–22: Questions about spirit and the future mark the limit of knowing; Ecclesiastes 4:6–12: a quiet handful and a companion as the better portion; Ecclesiastes 4:13: a poor and wise youth ranked above an old and foolish king; Ecclesiastes 6:1–9: Wisdom’s advantage questioned before unfilled appetite; Ecclesiastes 6:10–12: Knowing good and the future remain questions; Ecclesiastes 8:1–9: A changed face and fitting conduct without knowledge of the future; Ecclesiastes 9:1–12: Wisdom cannot secure bread; Sheol has no wisdom; Ecclesiastes 9:13–10:4: better than strength and weapons, yet the poor man's wisdom despised; Ecclesiastes 10:10–11: wisdom's advantage over the blunt ax, lost if applied too late; Ecclesiastes 12:9–14: The wise teacher commended; study remains costly. Deuteronomy 4:1–14: Keeping the statutes *is your wisdom and your understanding* in the nations' sight (6). Wisdom here is located in observed obedience, not in a separate body of instruction. Deuteronomy 29: The secret belongs to Yahweh, while revelation is entrusted to the people and their children for obedience (29). Unlike royal investigation in Proverbs 25:2, this verse emphasizes the practical obligation arising from what is known. · [[The Gibeonite Deception]] Joshua 9: Joshua questions the envoys and the men sample their provisions, yet they omit counsel from Yahweh (8, 14). Sensory evidence and prudent questions prove insufficient when the evidence has been staged; compare Proverbs 3:5–6’s call to trust Yahweh rather than one’s own understanding. | [[The Surety, the | [[Work]] | Labour as vocation — commissioned in the garden before the fall; toil, not work, is the curse; at Sinai, the workweek is bounded by rest for dependents as well as the household head; a limit on production; Textile work, joinery, and metalwork serve a specified design (26:1–30, 36–37); Making the altar and court leads into the recurring work of maintaining the light; skilled weaving, engraving, and embroidery for vestments; Spirit-given craft bounded by Sabbath (31); Aaron receives gold and fashions the calf with a tool (32:4); the tablets and their writing are explicitly God’s work (32:16); Exodus 34: Human preparation of tablets; agricultural work bounded by rest; Exodus 35: Making and giving include the skilled labor of women, bounded by Sabbath; Making, teaching, and assessing the supplies needed for the task; Exodus 36:8–38: skilled making and joining produce the enclosure components; Exodus 37: Metalwork, woodwork, and perfumery produce the furnishings.; Exodus 38: Craft, women’s contributions, and material accounting serve the sanctuary; Exodus 39: Gold cut into wires and integrated with skilled textile and stone work; Proverbs 6:6–11: the ant works without an overseer; the sluggard's little sleep brings poverty as a robber.; Proverbs 10:4–5, 16, 26: Diligent hands and gathering in season bring provision; sleeping at harvest shames the household; Proverbs 19: Idleness brings hunger; the sluggard will not even lift food to his mouth; Proverbs 20: Neglected plowing and excessive sleep jeopardize provision; Proverbs 21: Diligent plans against haste; the sluggard's fatal desire; Proverbs 22: The sluggard's lion in the streets; Proverbs 22:29: the skilled worker serves kings; Proverbs 24:27, 30–34: the field before the house; the sluggard's field gone to thorns; Proverbs 26:13–16: the sluggard's last portrait; Proverbs 28: Cultivating one’s land brings food; chasing fantasies brings poverty; Proverbs 30:25–27: ants that store and locusts in ranks without a king; Proverbs 31:13–19, 24, 27, 31: Skilled production, investment, and the worker’s claim to recognition and fruit; Ecclesiastes 1:1–11: Labor’s gain questioned against recurrence and passing generations; Ecclesiastes 2:4–11: great works bring joy as a portion, yet no profit; Ecclesiastes 2:17: the work done under the sun grievous; Ecclesiastes 2:18–26: skilled labor left to another; enjoyment from God; Ecclesiastes 3: Profit questioned; enjoyment given; God’s work endures; Ecclesiastes 3:16–22: Rejoicing in works as a portion despite the unseen future; Ecclesiastes 4:1–3: Evil deeds in 4:3 beside works enjoyed in 3:22; Ecclesiastes 4:4–12: labor driven by envy, refused by the fool, rewarded when shared; Ecclesiastes 5:8–20: Sweet sleep and enjoyment in work despite empty-handed departure; Ecclesiastes 6:1–9: Labor feeds the mouth without filling appetite; Ecclesiastes 9:1–12: Work with might before Sheol; Ecclesiastes 10:5–20: the hazards of labor, the blunt ax, and the house that leaks through idle hands; Ecclesiastes 12:9–14: Teaching as labor; study’s weariness; every deed accountable.; Song 6:11–7:9: The hands of a skillful workman; Song 8:8–12: family plans, the woman’s answer, and her own vineyard Leviticus 19:1–18: The harvest includes provision for others (9–10), and a hired worker’s wages must not remain overnight until morning (13). Labor creates an obligation on the employer rather than merely an opportunity for gain. Leviticus 23:1–8: Weekly and festival work prohibitions retain distinct wording. Leviticus 23:9–22: Assembly rest and reserved gleanings limit harvest labor.; Leviticus 23:23–32: Regular work and all work distinguished  Leviticus 24:1–9: Lamp tending is recurring priestly work (3–4), and arranging bread is assigned to the Sabbath itself (8). Together with the rest command in 23:3, this qualifies any claim that Sabbath means every kind of work ceases: the text commands this service.  Leviticus 25:1–22: Sowing, pruning, and reaping are interrupted by the land Sabbath and Jubilee (3–5, 11–12); Leviticus 25:35–55: the hired servant as the standard for a sold brother; Numbers 15:32–36: gathering sticks judged; lighting a fire not reported; Numbers 18: The tithe is explicitly compensation for Levite service at the Tent (21, 31). Deuteronomy 5: Six days of labor are followed by a rest that the household head must extend to children, servants, animals, and the resident stranger (13–14).  Deuteronomy 10:1–11: Moses cuts the stones and makes an acacia ark; Yahweh supplies the writing (1–5). Exodus 37:1 names Bezalel as ark maker. Attributing commissioned work to Moses can reconcile the agency, but Deuteronomy does not state that explanation. Deuteronomy 28: conditional blessing in the land.  Deuteronomy 28:15–68: House-building, planting, sowing, and tending vineyards fail to yield enjoyment (30, 33, 38–40). The curse includes labor whose benefit goes to others, not simply the absence of effort.  Joshua 17:14–18: Joshua twice directs forest clearance (15, 18). The inheritance requires labor to become usable; this passage joins land received with work still required.  Joshua 19:49–51: Joshua builds the city he receives and lives there (50). Gift and labor belong together; the verse does not specify whether the work is new construction, rebuilding, or fortification. | [[The Surety, the Sluggard, and the Seven Abominations]] · [[The Garden
